Disclaimer:
This ISPE Good Practice Guide: Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation provides 
detailed practical guidance to help pharmaceutical companies meet global regulatory process validation expectations. 
This Guide is solely created and owned by ISPE. It is not a regulation, standard or regulatory guideline document. 
ISPE cannot ensure and does not warrant that a system managed in accordance with this Guide will be acceptable to 
regulatory authorities. Further, this Guide does not replace the need for hiring professional engineers or technicians.

ISPE Good Practice Guide:
	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
	
Foreword
During my tenure at the FDA, I presented at many industry conferences on various topics, but few represented such 
significant change and improvement as those which culminated in the lifecycle approach to Process Validation (PV). 
In 2002, FDA launched the Pharmaceutical Quality for the 21st Century initiative aimed at modernizing pharmaceutical 
manufacturing using sound science and risk-based approaches. ICH shortly thereafter also embarked on its vision for 
pharmaceutical quality and set forth ICH Q8, Q9, Q10, and Q11.
•	
Q8	 Pharmaceutical Development (August 2009)
•	
Q9	 Quality Risk Management (November 2009)
•	
Q10	Pharmaceutical Quality System (June 2008)
•	
Q11	Development and Manufacture of Drug Substances (Chemical Entities and Biotechnological/Biological 
Entities) (May 2012)
A fundamental principle to ensure product quality is PV. In the past, interpretation of FDA’s Compliance Policy 
Guide (http://www.fda.gov/ICECI/ComplianceManuals/CompliancePolicyGuidanceManual/ucm074411.htm), which 
addressed process validation and the subject of guidance from FDA over multiple versions, there had been the 
impression that three commercial PV batches were almost always enough to ensure a robust and consistent process. 
This guide was updated in 2004 to facilitate understanding of requirements to bring products to market, especially 
when extensive process development efforts and large numbers of PV batches are not practical, e.g., orphan drugs.
Over time in FDA and in other international guidances, three batches had become the standard to demonstrate 
a validated process. With the lifecycle approach, efforts were made to bring PV into the 21st Century - to provide 
scientific evidence to justify going from development to commercial manufacturing while maintaining the desired 
process control over the product’s lifecycle. The intent was also to capture modern manufacturing methods using 
enhanced approaches, e.g., continuous manufacturing.
In 2011, the 1987 Guideline on General Principles of Process Validation was replaced, reflecting the modernization 
efforts for pharmaceutical quality initiated by FDA and in the international arena. Of the regulatory guidances 
issued to date, the FDA Guidance for Industry: Process Validation: General Principles and Practices of January 
2011 contains the most detail in regard to expectations for the three stages of the lifecycle approach to PV, but was 
followed internationally by several other impactful guidances and regulations, outlined below.
In February 2014, EMA published its “Guideline on process validation for finished products – information and data to 
be provided in regulatory submissions.” The scope of this document covered:
•	
Human medicinal products
•	
Veterinary medicinal products
•	
Biological products
A companion guideline “Guideline on process validation for the manufacture of biotechnology-derived active 
substances and data for inclusion in the regulatory submission” was published in April 2014.
These documents introduced the concepts of science and risk-based validation to Europe via:
•	
Process evaluation (Stages 1 and 2)
•	
Process verification (Stage 3)
The EU PV guidance sets out three approaches for PV:
•	
Traditional (three batches)
•	
Continuous process verification
•	
Hybrid (three batches or Continuous in different steps of the process)
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 5 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
Additionally, these guidance documents refer to GMP requirements for PV in Annex 15: Qualification and Validation 
to the EudraLex Volume 4 (Good Manufacturing Practice Medicinal Products for Human and Veterinary Use). This 
guidance was published for comment in March 2015 and became operational in October of the same year concurrent 
with its publication by PIC/S. Unlike the FDA guidance; Annex 15 also contains specific validation approaches to:
•
Transport verification
•
Packaging qualification
•
Utilities qualification
•
Test method validation
•
Cleaning validation
The EMA guideline on PV for finished products underwent a minor update in November 2016.
With these modern approaches to pharmaceutical development and manufacturing and the advancement of the 
concept of PV to a lifecycle approach, ISPE’s Product Quality Lifecycle Initiative (PQLI®) has been providing the 
technical underpinnings to develop the necessary education, training, and discussion papers to pharmaceutical 
professionals engaged in all facets of pharmaceutical development, commercialization, and manufacturing. This 
Good Practice Guide: Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation brings together 
an excellent reference providing the technical and scientific detail needed to lead PV through scientifically sound 
development to robust reliable processes, ensuring the continued supply of quality medicines to patients.
An expert group of volunteers with manufacturing, quality, technical development, and statistician backgrounds has 
brought this effort to the point of providing this Guide to our ISPE members. It covers a wide variety of manufacturing 
types including small and large molecule, active pharmaceutical ingredients (including intermediate steps), solid oral 
dosage forms, sterile drug product, etc.
This Guide will be an invaluable reference bringing together many of the efforts to date for use in your daily activities 
and as an underpinning to ISPE’s ongoing education and training in this area.
Joe Famulare
Former FDA Deputy Director, Office of Compliance, CDER
Vice President, Compliance and External Collaboration, Genentech, A Member of the Roche Group

ISPE Good Practice Guide:

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
1	
Introduction
Between 2004 and 2009, ICH published guidance documents Q8 (Quality by Design – Pharmaceutical Development) 
[1], and Q9 (Quality Risk Management) [2], which describe the science and risk-based approach to the development 
of pharmaceutical products. These guidances represented a paradigm shift in development, commercialization, 
transfer, and management throughout the product lifecycle.
The science and risk-based approach to Process Validation (PV) leverages information and knowledge from product 
development activities to make certain patient requirements are translated into product attributes. This requires that 
suitable manufacturing processes are defined with a control strategy that reliably ensures these attributes are met. 
Regulatory authorities worldwide have published revised guidances for science and risk-based approaches to be 
applied to PV, which presents a significant change to the way validation had been conducted.
PV demonstrates manufacturing control strategy adequacy and robustness and provides ongoing assurance that the 
process remains in control. It is defined in the 2011 FDA PV Guidance as [3]:
“…the collection and evaluation of data, from the process design stage through commercial production, which 
establishes scientific evidence that a process is capable of consistently delivering quality product. Process 
validation involves a series of activities taking place over the lifecycle of the product and process.”
Other regulatory authorities have similar definitions for PV although terminology may differ.
This new (enhanced) approach to PV encompasses three stages, illustrated in Figure 1.1, as compared to the 
traditional or historic approach. The science and risk-based approach combines product development knowledge 
with a structured process performance and product quality monitoring system to provide for validation throughout the 
product lifecycle.
Figure 1.1: Process Validation Overview – Process Validation Lifecycle – the Enhanced Approach
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 10 =====
Page 8	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
The science and risk-based approach to PV brings an expectation for an improved understanding of process control 
and robustness via clearer focus of Critical Quality Attributes (CQAs) and the impact of Material CQA and Critical 
Process Parameter (CPP) variability on these product CQAs. PV is no longer considered a one-time event; a lifecycle 
approach is expected by most global markets. PV success relies on establishing a comprehensive science-based 
process design that focuses on understanding sources of variability to achieve product realization.
The intent of this Guide is to cover the following:
•
Present information to assist companies, globally or locally, in understanding the application of validation
requirements for US, EU, and elsewhere, and explain the role in assuring reliable supply
•
Demonstrate that process understanding and a well-designed control strategy are the keys to reliable supply and
validation success
•
Show how qualification is an essential element supporting PV
•
Assist in clarifying and aligning the different terminology used by regulators and demonstrating similarities in the
underlying fundamental principles
•
Provide details of how each of the three validation stages may be implemented and how the stages are linked
•
Illustrate application of the three-stage approach using case studies for both large and small molecule products
•
Provide practical implementation advice, including leveraging some of the formerly issued ISPE PV Team
discussion papers [4]
•
Offer guidance on the way science and risk-based approaches support validation
•
Reinforce that the principle of lifecycle PV is to help assure the adequacy of the development and manufacture of
pharmaceutical products – to both industry and regulators
•
Provide information for determining the number of batches for PV Stage 2
•
Provide statistical rationale and information that may be applied at different stages of the lifecycle
•
Illustrate ways for maintaining a state of control throughout the product lifecycle
•
Demonstrate applicability to all parts of the industry including development companies, manufacturers,
equipment suppliers, contract research organizations, Contract Manufacturing Organizations (CMOs), generics,
‘virtual’ companies, etc.
1.1	
Foundation for the Good Practice Guide
An international team from many companies has brought together their experience to provide this Guide. The ISPE 
Process Validation Team’s goal is to facilitate the practical implementation of the lifecycle approach to PV. The 
Team acknowledges the foundation by ISPE Product Quality Lifecycle Implementation® (PQLI®) Guide Series [5] 
that focused on understanding manufacturing process science and controlling those processes consistent with ICH 
Q8(R2), Q9 and Q10 [1, 2, 6]. The ISPE PQLI® Guides are:
•
PQLI® Part 1 – Product Realization Using Quality by Design (QbD): Concepts and Principles, including Overview,
Criticality, Design Space and Control Strategy (November 2011) [7]
•
PQLI® Part 2 – Product Realization using Quality by Design (QbD): Illustrative Example (November 2011) [8]

•	
PQLI® Part 3 – Change Management System as a Key Element of a Pharmaceutical Quality System (June 2012) [9]
•	
PQLI® Part 4 – Process Performance and Product Quality Monitoring System (June 2013) [10]
These Guides explain the principles and provide a basis for process understanding PV Stage 1 in preparation for PV 
Stages 2.2, 3.1 and 3.2.
Without process understanding and linkage to impacted CQAs, process consistency and robustness cannot be 
achieved. Understanding the variation of inputs as described in ICH Q8(R2) [1] and the subsequent link to variation in 
output product CQAs are also foundational to meeting regulatory expectations.
1.2	
Global Acceptance/Authorization
The FDA’s 2011 Process Validation Guidance [3] is often associated with enhanced PV approaches; however, many 
other authorities have applicable guidance.
PV guidance approved for Europe is well established and contains similar principles on lifecycle validation. The 
requirements are described in three related documents:
•	
EU Guideline – EudraLex Volume 4, Annex 15: Qualification and Validation [11]
•	
EMA “Guideline on process validation for finished products – information and data to be provided in regulatory 
submissions” [12]
•	
EMA “Guideline on process validation for the manufacture of biotechnology-derived active substances and data 
for inclusion in the regulatory submission” [13]
The latter two documents also describe data to be provided in regulatory submissions, however, the three documents 
should be read together.
The EMA “Guideline on process validation for finished products” [12] contains the following statement:
“This document provides guidance on the validation of the manufacturing process, which can be considered as 
the second stage in the product lifecycle. The first stage (process design) is covered in the note for guidance 
on pharmaceutical development (ICH Q8R2/ EMEA/CVMP/315/98) and the third stage (on-going process 
verification) is covered under GMP (Annex 15).”
Hence, a three-stage approach is appropriate for the EU as well as the US, and when the EMA/EU PV documents 
are considered together [11, 12, 13] the requirements are very similar in principle to US requirements [3], with minor 
differences in terminology and details, such as the level of emphasis on statistical principles.
PIC/S adopted the EU GMP Annex 15 [11] (PIC/S Annex 15 [14]), and from version 12 onwards, they are identical 
documents; however, as PIC/S is a GMP guidance applicable to other parts of the world, it does not reference the 
EMA PV guidances.
The following list provides an approximate comparison of the validation requirements in different parts of the world:
FDA
•	
PV Guidance [3]
•	
Based on ICH Q8(R2), Q9, Q10 [1, 2, 6]; incorporates CQAs, CPPs, and material attributes

•
Requires link between product/process development activities (PV Stage 1) and commercial scale manufacturing
•
Requires verification that the process is maintained in a state of control throughout the product lifecycle –
Continued Process Verification
•
Deliberate use of statistics to characterize variability
•
Applies to all drug products and APIs
EU
•
Annex 15: Qualification and Validation [11]; EMA Guideline on process validation for finished products –
information and data to be provided in regulatory submissions [12]; and EMA Guideline on process validation for
the manufacture of biotechnology-derived active substances and data to be provided in regulatory submissions
[13]
•
Based on ICH Q8(R2), Q9, Q10, Q11 [1, 2, 6, 15]; incorporates CQAs, CPPs, and material attributes
•
Requires a link between product/process development activities (PV Stage 1) and commercial scale
manufacturing
•
Requires verification that the process is maintained in a state of control throughout the product lifecycle –
Ongoing Process Verification
•
Recognizes traditional – minimum of three-batch PV, with supplementation from ongoing verification and
continuous verification as an alternate approach
•
Allows for a traditional PV approach or continuous verification as an alternative approach for processes
developed with QbD and/or utilizing PAT. Hybrid (combination) approaches are also allowed.
•
Europe requires PV data in the dossier for sterile products and complex dosage forms. A PV scheme is required
for all products.
•
EU GMP contains other qualification/validation guidance
•
Annex 15 [11] applies only to drug products, optionally to APIs; however, there is a specific EMA guidance for
biotechnology-derived APIs [13]
•
Contains specific PV requirements for non-standard processes (“…determined by a combination of the nature of
the active substance, the nature of the finished product, the actual process itself, and the production experience
of the manufacturer.” [12])
PIC/S
•
Annex 15 – as adopted by many regulatory authorities in other countries [14]
•
Released 30 March 2015, Operational 1 October 2015, concurrent with EU cGMP as PIC/S version 12 [16]
-
Note: PIC/S membership does not mean the latest version of Annex 15 must be adopted by a member
authority. PIC/S only requires that GMPs are “similar” to the PIC/S guideline [14].
•
Changes to PIC/S GMP guides and EU GMP guides are developed in parallel, so that the documents are
essentially the same [14].

Maturity/experience with lifecycle validation in other parts of the world varies across markets. Generally, adoption of 
lifecycle validation is slower outside the US and Europe.
1.3	
Scope
This Guide applies to:
•	
Active Pharmaceutical Ingredients (APIs) – large and small molecules
•	
Vaccines
•	
Combination products (drug product portion)
•	
Pharmaceutical drug products both sterile and non-sterile
•	
Veterinary drugs
While not specifically referenced, and without case study examples, the principles and approaches in this Guide 
generally also apply to antibody conjugates, orphan drugs, etc.
This Guide does not review the validation of packaging processes at this time. It is noted that beyond the product 
definition, risk level, and the technologies used, packaging validation differs little from the validation of processes 
used for drug manufacture. Similarly, equipment used in packaging processes may be commissioned and qualified by 
following the same general guidelines as those used for drug product manufacturing equipment. Further information 
can be found in the ISPE Discussion Paper: Overview of Packaging Validation for Drug Products [27].
This ISPE Good Practice Guide: Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation can be 
applied in other cases where regulatory requirements require enhanced PV approaches.
1.4	
Purpose
The intention of this Guide is to facilitate the implementation of the lifecycle approach to PV (as described, for 
example, by FDA’s Process Validation: General Principles and Practices [3], EU PV Guidances [11, 12, 13], and 
GMPs published by other regulatory authorities) and concepts presented in ICH Q8(R2), Q9, Q10, Q11 [1, 2, 6, 15].
This Guide provides step by step implementation approaches to PV and leverages process understanding to promote 
best practices.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
1.5	
Benefits
It is hoped this Guide will help companies advance a science and risk-based methodology for validation processes 
throughout the product lifecycle to support the provision of a reliable supply of consistent quality products to patients, 
while meeting regulatory expectations and providing business benefits.
The methodologies in this Guide have been compiled from a wide variety of companies, many of whom have applied 
the concepts successfully, achieving, for example, shorter process development to commercialization lead times, 
earlier identification of continuous improvement opportunities, and reduced deviations.

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
2	 Basic Terms Used in the Process Validation 
	
Lifecycle
2.1	
Process Validation Lifecycle Stages
“Process validation is defined as the collection and evaluation of data, from the process design stage through 
commercial production, which establishes scientific evidence that a process is capable of consistently 
delivering quality product” [3], thereby also assuring reliability of supply.
Lifecycle or enhanced PV should be based on the principles described in ICH Q8(R2), Q9, Q10, and Q11 [1, 2, 6, 
15], and is undertaken in three stages linking product and process development, qualification of the commercial 
manufacturing process, and maintenance of the process in a state of control during routine commercial production.
Guidances published in the US [3] and Europe [11, 12, 13] clearly outline these three stages and approach; however, 
some terminology differences exist. Other international guidances use terminology based on one of these two 
jurisdictions.
Common industry practice is to name the stages as Stage 1, Stage 2, and Stage 3. This is the approach adopted by 
this Guide; recognizing that there are terminology differences between regions. A summary of the stages, their goals, 
and typical activities completed in each are summarized in Table 2.1 and Figure 2.1.
Table 2.1: Goals and Typical Activities of the Stages of Process Validation

Figure 2.1: Process Validation Comparison
Table 2.2 (later in this chapter) summarizes the similarities/differences between the FDA PV Guidance [3], the EMA 
Guidelines [12, 13] and Annex 15 [11]. Note: the scope of the PIC/S guidance [14] only covers the manufacturing 
stages of the lifecycle, although a reference exists to EMA regulatory guidance [12, 13] for EU markets.
Lifecycle PV and QbD activities share many concepts and terms; however, some key terms are fundamental to PV 
activities and are discussed in the following sections. Comprehensive coverage of QbD terminology is contained in 
the ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 1 – Concepts and Principles [7].
2.2	
Stage 1 Process Design/Pharmaceutical Development
Lifecycle PV is fundamentally concerned with the development, verification, and maintenance of the control strategy. 
The conclusion of process design activities provides a control strategy, which is validated in subsequent PV stages. 
ICH Q8(R2) [1] defines control strategy as:
“A planned set of controls, derived from current product and process understanding that ensures process 
performance and product quality. The controls can include parameters and attributes related to drug substance 
and drug product materials and components, facility and equipment operating conditions, in-process controls, 
finished product specifications, and the associated methods and frequency of monitoring and control.”
From a validation perspective, the key focus of a control strategy is on CQAs, CPPs, and material attributes. ICH 
Q8(R2) [1] defines a CQA as:
“A physical, chemical, biological or microbiological property or characteristic that should be within an appropriate 
limit, range, or distribution to ensure the desired product quality.”
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 19 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 17
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
and a CPP as:
“A process parameter whose variability has an impact on a critical quality attribute and therefore should be 
monitored or controlled to ensure the process produces the desired quality.”
ICH Q8(R2) [1] does not use the term Critical Material Attribute (CMA); however, it is used quite frequently by industry 
and in this Guide. The definition for CMA for this Guide is:
A material attribute of either a raw/starting material or intermediate that has an impact to a critical quality attribute 
and therefore should be monitored or controlled to ensure the process produces the desired quality. Examples of 
CMAs are a reagent CQA, a CQA for an excipient added at a blending stage, and a packaging material CQA.
While the focus of PV is on CQAs, CPPs, and CMAs, this does not mean that non-critical quality attributes, 
parameters, and material attributes should be ignored in validation. These non-critical indicators remain important to 
other aspects of product and process understanding for business purposes.
2.3	
Stage 2 Key Terms and Substages
The FDA PV Guidance [3] discusses a subdivision of Stage 2. The initial substage (referred to hereinafter as Stage 
2.1) is where the fitness of facilities and equipment for intended use is assured and documented.
Following that is where the proposed commercial manufacturing process is shown to be consistent and reproducible, 
resulting in product meeting all required quality criteria with a high degree of assurance. Called Stage 2.2 in this 
Guide, it concludes with Process Performance Qualification (PPQ) runs prior to subsequent commercial distribution of 
the new or modified product.
The EU PV guidance [11, 12, 13] follows a similar approach and is more deliberate and detailed in its definition of the 
elements of Stages 2.1 and 2.2.
During Stage 2.1, EMA and PIC/S guidances (Annex 15) [11, 14] suggest completing the following qualification 
activities as applicable:
•	
User Requirements Specifications (URS)
•	
Design Qualification (DQ)
•	
Factory Acceptance Testing (FAT)/Site Acceptance Testing (SAT)
•	
Installation Qualification (IQ)
•	
Operational Qualification (OQ)
•	
Performance Qualification (PQ)
Additionally, Annex 15 [11, 14] describes specific verification/validation requirements for transportation, packaging, 
utilities, test methods, and cleaning.
Stage 2.2 is described as Process Performance Qualification in the FDA guidance [3] and identified as Process 
Validation in the EMA’s Annex 15 [11]. In this ISPE Good Practice Guide, the term PPQ is used to describe activities 
in Stage 2.2 to avoid confusion with the use of the word validation, which can also apply to the overall program.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 20 =====
Page 18	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
The EMA guidance [12] describes two approaches to product development:
“Traditional approach: A product development approach where set points and operating ranges for process 
parameters are defined to ensure reproducibility.”
“Enhanced approach: A development approach where risk management and scientific knowledge is used to 
identify and understand the material attributes and process parameters which influence the critical quality 
attributes of a product.”
PPQ (FDA) [3] and Process Validation (Annex 15) [11] can be considered to be the same. In either case, and 
regardless of the development approach, manufacturers are required to determine an appropriate number of batches 
for the PPQ study.
The EMA guidances [12, 13] also describe an approach termed Continuous Process Verification, defining it as:
“An alternative approach to process validation in which manufacturing process performance is continuously 
monitored and verified.”
This term is intended to apply to the use of PAT principles and should not be confused with the FDA terminology of 
Continued Process Verification (CPV) [3].
Additionally, the EMA defines a process validation (PPQ) strategy called a hybrid approach, which includes a 
combination of traditional process validation and continuous process validation. This can apply to products that may 
have been initially developed under a traditional development approach [12].
2.4	
Stage 3 Continued (Ongoing) Process Verification and Substages
FDA, WHO, EMA, and PIC/S guidances [3, 26, 11, 12, 13, 14] adopt ICH principles [1, 5, 6] requiring manufacturers 
to continue validation throughout the lifecycle of the product. Terminology differs between these documents, with 
FDA and WHO [3, 26] calling this phase Continued Process Verification (CPV), and EMA and PIC/S [11, 12, 13, 14] 
designating the same phase Ongoing Process Verification (OPV).
The term CPV is used throughout this ISPE Good Practice Guide. The objective of CPV is to provide:
“…continual assurance that the process remains in a state of control (the validated state) during commercial 
manufacture” [3].
CPV starts following successful completion of PPQ and is divided into two phases.
FDA PV guidance [3] requires:
“…continued monitoring and sampling of process parameters and quality attributes at the level established 
during the process qualification stage until sufficient data are available to generate significant variability 
estimates.”
After which time:
“Monitoring can then be adjusted to a statistically appropriate and representative level.”
Annex 15 [11] is less prescriptive but requires the extent and frequency of monitoring to take into account the current 
level of process understanding and performance. These requirements are widely understood to be the same.
This Guide defines the two stages in Sections 2.4.1 and 2.4.2.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 21 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 19
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
2.4.1	
Stage 3.1 – Heightened Monitoring Phase
Stage 3.1 is the phase where PPQ has been completed; however, the manufacturer maintains sampling and testing 
at the level of PPQ (heightened monitoring) for some or all parameters until additional confidence or process 
understanding is developed. The criteria for that point is defined by the manufacturer in the CPV monitoring plan.
The point at which this phase ends may be determined statistically or using other science and risk-based methods.
Following this, the validation sampling and testing can be reduced, and the validation program enters Stage 3.2 
– Routine Monitoring Phase. If the process drifts to an extent that it is out of the expected trend, it is appropriate
to reinstate Stage 3.1 – Heightened Monitoring Phase, for some or all parameters until the cause of the shift is
understood and accounted for in the validation program.
2.4.2	
Stage 3.2 – Routine Monitoring Phase
During this stage, the process is expected to be well controlled and stable; therefore, the sampling and testing 
strategy may be reduced, reflecting the level of process understanding and control (routine monitoring).
This stage has the following objectives:
•
Ongoing process verification over the life of the product to demonstrate that the process remains in control
•
Identification of trends that may be within normal process variability, but indicates a future potential to trend
outside expected limits
•
Continuation of understanding of the sources of process variability and their impact
•
Implementation of process improvement actions to reduce such process variability if deemed necessary
Table 2.2: Summary of Terminology Used in this Guide
Stage
Terminology in this Guide
Relates to:
Stage 1
Process Design
Pharmaceutical Development (EU and PIC/S [28, 6])
Stage 2

Qualification including URS, DQ, FAT, SAT, IQ, OQ, 
and PQ (EU [11, 12, 13])
Stage 2.2
Process Performance Qualification (PPQ)

2.5	
Key Statistical Terms
Interpretation of the language of the PV guidances related to use of statistical methods requires understanding of 
some fundamental statistical terms. Some key terms include:
Robust Process: A process that demonstrates acceptable quality and performance while tolerating input variability.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 22 =====
Page 20	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
State of Control (as defined in Annex 15 [11]): A condition in which the set of controls consistently provides 
assurance of continued process performance and product quality. Note: this definition differs from the definition of a 
Stable Process under statistical control.
Stable Process: A process in a state of statistical control (constant mean and variance) subject only to random 
causes (ISO 3534-2 [29]). Note: this state does not imply that the random variation is large or small, within or outside 
of specification; but rather the variation is predictable using statistical techniques. Also, very importantly, a process 
can fail to meet the definition of a Stable Process due to shifts in the mean, but still be considered in a State of 
Control.
Variability: A measure of the spread or dispersion of a distribution of data. Intra-batch variability reflects variability 
within a batch; Inter-batch variability reflects the variability between batches.
Statistical Distribution: A mathematical function that describes the probability of occurrence of possible outcomes of 
a population.
Confidence (Statistical): The confidence level associated with a statistical interval (such as confidence or tolerance 
intervals) is equal to the percentage of similarly computed intervals that contain the true parameter value or claimed 
population proportion. More generally speaking, statistical confidence reflects the sufficiency of data-based evidence 
to support a hypothesis or claim.
Capability (Statistical): A measure of the ability of a process to meet specification or acceptance criteria (often 
computed as a ratio of a statistical estimate for the process range compared to the specification or acceptance 
criteria).
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
3	 Design Development Phase (Stage 1)
Stage 1 is the developmental phase in which a product’s commercial manufacturing process is determined. In this 
Guide, Stage 1 is known as Process Design. See Chapter 2 for the different terminology used by regulators.
Science and risk-based development brings expectations for a more systematic, deliberate approach to product 
and process development with direct linkage to patient needs. This enhanced understanding of the product and 
process is an enabler of successful validation and subsequent commercial manufacturing. The FDA has the following 
statements in the 2011 PV Guidance [3]:
“Each manufacturer should judge whether it has gained sufficient understanding to provide a high degree of 
assurance in its manufacturing process to justify commercial distribution of the product.”
“The number of samples should be adequate to provide sufficient statistical confidence of quality both within 
a batch and between batches.”
In order to reach an accurate conclusion about the acceptability of the manufacturing process, input and process 
control variation should be included in PV Stage 1. Figure 3.1 shows key elements of Stage 1 useful for moving to 
Stages 2 and 3.
Process manufacturing results from PV Stage 1 should be considered together with PV Stage 2 results in making the 
overall conclusion about the adequacy of the manufacturing process control strategy. Prior to entering PV Stage 2, 
viability of the process should be assessed including evaluation of incoming and output variability. Section 3.4 and 
Table 3.2 provide guidance on selecting Stage 1 batches suitable for use when evaluating Stage 2.2 data.
Figure 3.1: Flow Diagram Outlining the Key Elements of Stage 1 Leading into Stage 2 and Stage 3 Activities [8]
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.

3.1	
Apply Statistics in PV Stage 1
Statistics is the science of making decisions in the face of uncertainty. Any time there is data, or more broadly 
information or knowledge, there is a statistical method or approach that can be employed to assess the data and 
determine if the desired outcome has been achieved.
Knowledge is defined as facts, information, and skills acquired through experience. As such, knowledge can be 
gained through the theoretical or past understanding of a process and does not need to be recreated for every 
product being developed, but should be created where necessary. That is, in the design process, teams leverage 
relevant existing data along with fundamental knowledge to make initial decisions, perform risk assessments to 
identify gaps, and take actions to gain more knowledge.
Statistical tools are a component of the data collection, summarization, and quantification of Stage 1 knowledge. 
These statistical tools fall into broad categories of data-based decision making, data collection and experimental 
design, simple data analysis, and complex modeling.
Some of the Stage 1 statistical methodologies are:
Visualization: The benefit of effective and simple graphical display of information cannot be overstated and the ability 
to take a set of data, summarize the information, and visually display this information is both an art and a science. A 
primary goal of data visualization is to communicate information clearly. Graphics may be used to illuminate data, and 
in some cases can be more revealing than conventional statistical computations and definitely increase clarity over 
tabular summaries.
There are many graphical tools available in good spreadsheet and statistical software programs. Some common 
graphical tools include:
•
Data exploration: y versus x, x1 versus x2
•
Data trending: y versus time, and x versus time
•
Data distribution: histogram, stem and leaf plot, box plot, pareto charts
Simple Descriptive Statistics: Descriptive statistics is the discipline of quantitatively describing a set of data. This 
usually includes a description of the central tendency of the data (mean, geometric mean, median, or mode) and a 
measure of the dispersion or variability in the data (range, standard deviation, or variance).
The data summary can be displayed visually as a box plot, histogram, or individual value plot by itself or with other 
groups of similar data as a comparison.
Summary statistics can also be calculated for attribute data (pass/fail) or count, which may include total counts, 
proportions, and percentages (e.g., proportion or percent of sample with a given non-conformance).
Statistical Intervals: Statistical intervals are a useful tool for quantifying uncertainty. These intervals include:
•
Confidence: A confidence interval reflects the sampling uncertainty regarding a population parameter, such as
the mean, standard deviation, or proportion non-conforming.
For example, a 95% confidence interval on the mean implies that if repeated samples from the same population
were taken and confidence intervals calculated from each sample, 95% of them will include (“cover”) the true
population mean. In other words, confidence can be thought of as the probability of being correct about the value
of the population parameter.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 25 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 23
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
•
Prediction: A prediction interval for one or more future observations is an interval that will, with a specified
degree of confidence, contain one or more randomly selected observation(s) from a population.
•
Tolerance: A tolerance interval provides limits with confidence on a proportion of a population. More speciﬁcally,
a 100 × p%/100 × (1-α) tolerance interval provides limits within which at least a certain proportion (p) of the
population falls with a given level of conﬁdence (1-α). For example, a 95/90 tolerance interval would provide
limits within which 95% of the population falls with a 90% level of confidence.
•
Bayesian Credible Interval: A Bayesian credible interval is an interval within which a population parameter
(mean, standard deviation, proportion non-conforming) lies with the stated probability (not the same as
confidence – see above).
•
Bayesian Prediction Interval: A Bayesian prediction interval is an interval within which a future individual value
from the population will be, with stated probability.
Sampling Plans: In the pharmaceutical industry Chemistry, Manufacturing and Control (CMC) applications, sampling 
plans are used for many applications, including making batch release decisions, demonstrating homogeneity of drug 
substance and drug product, accepting batches of raw material, and selection of units for environmental monitoring. 
Sampling decisions include the sample size, sample location, and sampling method, all to test a specified hypothesis.
Monte Carlo Simulation: Simulation is most useful for studying future events that can be predicted from historical 
data or knowledge. The impact of considered changes can be simulated to obtain an understanding of future 
outcomes under various possible scenarios.
Measurement System Analysis (MSA): These analyses are referred to as Repeatability and Reproducibility (R&R) 
in some industries. They involve the design and analysis of experimental data to understand, quantify, and reduce the 
variability in the measurement system (analytical method).
Variability in the measurement system is normally reduced to categories of bias, linearity, stability, precision, 
accuracy, repeatability, and reproducibility. This type of data analysis is critical for the development of useful analytical 
methods. MSA experiments are applicable for both continuous and attribute data.
Hypothesis Testing: Hypothesis tests are statistical analyses to assess the strength of empirical evidence to reject 
one hypothesis (the “null hypothesis”) in favor of another (the “alternative hypothesis”).
If the data collected is sufficiently unlikely under the assumption of the null hypothesis, then it is rejected; the 
probability of the observed results if the null hypothesis is true is referred to as the p-value. Lower p-values lead 
to rejecting the null hypothesis (typically the threshold to reject null hypothesis is set to p ≤ 0.05 but the threshold 
selected depends on the situation).
Notes for proper interpretation:
1.
A p-value greater than the threshold alone does not necessarily mean the null hypothesis is true; it can only be
claimed there is not enough evidence to reject. Further statistical assessment is required to assess the likelihood
of the null hypothesis or other statements regarding the hypothesized parameter.
2.
Statistical significance indicated by a p-value less than some threshold value is not the same as practical
significance. Practical significance of a statistical conclusion should always be assessed using a risk-based
approach incorporating patient context and possibly additional statistical analysis.
Empirical Modeling: Data-driven models are those developed through fitting models to data.
In Stage 1, there is often data related to the process or compound being developed. Sometimes, as is the case with 
material property data, chemical structure data, and processing data, large data sets exist and data-driven models 
are developed and refined to describe relationships between inputs and outputs.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 26 =====
Page 24	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
In the case of a material property data base, relationships between material properties and product attributes 
are examined and data-driven models developed to predict product properties based on the material attributes. 
These data-driven models permit a decrease in experimentation, or at least provide a starting point for further 
experimentation. Examples of data-driven models include simple linear regression, regression trees, partial least 
squares regression, and support vector machine.
First-principle or Fundamental Models: First-principle, engineering, physics, or fundamental models explain or 
hypothesize relationships between parameters, material attributes, or manufacturing factors and product attributes. 
These models seek to predict product attributes directly from established laws of science.
While first-principle models are not considered statistical tools, the models may need data collection and some 
statistical analysis to estimate parameters in the fundamental model. Prior to running experiments, whether via One-
Factor-at-a-Time Studies (OFAT) or through Design of Experiments (DoE), information based on either first principles 
or data-driven models can be used to help identify relationships between inputs and outputs, and to give direction for 
experimentation.
Design of Experiments: DoE is a highly-used tool to investigate relationships within the QbD framework of Stage 1. 
DoE provides a systematic approach to study prioritized factors/parameters/inputs and establish a relationship with 
quality or in-process attributes.
The strength of DoE is in the application of a systematic approach to data-based decision making along with the 
selection of a study design; the DoE approach facilitates the study of factors and interactions between factors in an 
efficient manner.
Due to the complexity of most processes, interactions often have an effect on quality attributes. An interaction means 
that the effect a process parameter has on the response may depend on the levels of another process parameter. 
Statistical experimental designs permit the estimation of interactions whereas changing OFAT does not.
Additionally, the statistical design approach provides a proper experimental plan that, when combined with the 
analysis method, maximizes the amount of information for the minimum number of runs (i.e., the knowledge 
development process is highly efficient).
Designing an efficient process with an effective process control strategy is dependent on the process knowledge 
and understanding obtained. DoE studies can help develop process knowledge by revealing relationships, including 
multifactor interactions, between the material or process parameters and the resulting quality attributes measured on 
process outputs (e.g., in-process material, intermediates, or the final product).
Efficiently determining an approximate mathematical representation of the underlying physical equation is best 
accomplished by DoE and an effective experimental strategy.
Greater discussion and use of these statistical tools are provided in Burdick, et al. [30].
3.2	
Assess Variability in Stage 1
Assessing the input variability realized in PV Stage 1 batches or studies helps establish the process control 
requirements prior to proceeding to Stage 2.2. Provided sufficient understanding is established ahead of Stage 2.2, 
then the process is more an exercise of verifying manufacturing consistency and reliability. The level of understanding 
of variability for each input can be categorized as either low or medium risk. High risk items need to be mitigated prior 
to initiating Stage 2.2. Low risk items are verified at the time of Stage 2.2 execution. Medium risk items may lead to 
continued assessment during Stage 2.2 and possibly more testing and/or monitoring in Stage 3.
Stage 1 process development consists of laboratory-scale, pilot-scale, or large-scale studies and/or batches that 
help confirm the designed process as a robust manufacturing process within which the impact of design space or 
parameter ranges on product quality attributes is understood.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
During process development, sources of variability should be identified and, where possible, variability should be 
induced in order to characterize and understand its impact on product quality attributes. These sources of input 
variability may include:
•
Batch size
•
Manufacturing site
•
Manufacturing date (e.g., time of year)
•
Drug substance batches (e.g., consider batches at ends of control ranges such as full range of particle size or
impurity levels)
•
Excipient batches (e.g., consider batches at ends of control ranges)
•
Storage conditions for raw materials
•
Shipping conditions of raw materials (e.g., capsule shells shipped overseas)
•
Multiple raw material batches per drug product batch
•
Container/closure component batches
•
Consumable material batches or types (e.g., disposable filter sets, sampling materials)
•
Manufacturing equipment sets (e.g., use of multiple sets or different makes/models)
•
Manufacturing suites or processing lines
•
Manufacturing shifts or operators
•
Manufacturing environment (e.g., temperature or light control)
•
Manufacturing process conditions (e.g., Batch at target conditions? Batch with challenged ranges? Commercial
manufacturing control strategy in place?)
•
Varied hold times or processing times
•
Process interruptions
•
Batch reprocessing/recovery
•
Sampling methods/equipment
•
Analytical testing laboratories’ methods qualification state (development mode, validate, etc.) and/or personnel
Practical tools can be used to summarize the input variability planned or experienced. Table 3.1 provides a simple 
table used to capture relevant variables across Stage 1 batches. Such a table may also be a version-controlled “living 
tool” that can be carried through Stages 2 and 3 batches to continue to assess the extent that process knowledge has 
been gained from input variability.
Alternatively, a color-schemed matrix could help visualize how variability has been incorporated across Stage 1, as 
provided in an example in Figure 3.2.

Figure 3.2: Parenteral Product Input Variability Color Map (colors represent discrete results for clarity)
These tool examples are qualitative ways to capture the process inputs that have been varied. This information 
contributes to understanding the process and control strategy and allows decisions to be made in terms of residual 
risk prior to PPQ and/or the need for continued input variability assessment in the later PV stages. For example, a 
parameter that is particularly critical or more directly impacts a critical drug product attribute may require continued 
induced input variability in order to gain needed experience and confidence.
Alternatively, an input or parameter may not have been varied in Stage 1, creating a knowledge gap; therefore, the 
extended control strategy can outline where continued monitoring and/or testing in later stages might be required 
if induced variability cannot occur. Some inputs are not easily varied (e.g., a critical specification for an excipient) 
without some intervention. Continued monitoring of such an instance in Stage 3 may allow an opportunity to 
experience additional input variability not previously seen.
The tools described in Section 3.2 help assess the robustness of the process to study input variability without 
necessarily requiring quantitative algorithms; however, consideration could be given to assess the risk associated 
with input variables and the possibility of yet unforeseen variability.
It may be helpful to establish a mechanism to qualitatively or quantitatively score the associated risk. A risk 
assessment prior to Stage 1 experience, which would leverage prior experience with similar formulations or 
processes, published literature, or first principles knowledge, may provide an initial baseline against which to compare 
a post-Stage 1 or later assessment. These risk assessments can vary in their level of complexity and quantitation, 
such as a formal risk assessment tool, or simply documenting the risk rationale and need for additional evaluation 
within the protocol of Stage 2 or 3 batches.
Whether the evaluation of input variability in Stage 1 is via simple qualitative tables or a highly-automated IT 
solution, the assessment is valuable in establishing the process and control strategy boundaries of knowledge. The 
understanding gained from Stage 1 is the basis for defining what further input variability evaluation should occur in 
Stages 2 and 3.

Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

3.3	
Leverage Stage 1 Data
When determining and justifying the number of Stage 2.2 batches needed to provide sufficient batch and data 
information to be collected as part of the testing program, it is appropriate to use current product and process 
knowledge gained from Stage 1 data, or data from similar products and similar manufacturing process steps (e.g., 
same composition but used for another filling volume).
The intent of leveraging Stage 1 batches is to support the existing body of knowledge and demonstrate the control 
strategy in order to potentially justify the number of Stage 2.2 (PV batches) to be manufactured to establish the 
validated state of the process.
For a new product, Stage 1 data typically includes development, clinical trial, technology transfer, engineering 
batches, and primary stability batches. Whereas for an existing commercial product, Stage 1 data is typically 
represented by the commercial batches manufactured (prior knowledge batches) at the site and/or technology 
transfer batches manufactured at a receiving site, as long as the process design has not been significantly changed.
Stage 1 data is leveraged in designing Stage 2.2 in many ways. Specifically, with respect to determining and justifying 
the number of Stage 2.2 batches needed, Stage 1 data provides information on process capability and reproducibility, 
feeding into the residual risk assessment regarding how much additional confidence is needed.
Representative Stage 1 batches can be appropriately incorporated into Stage 2.2 data analysis using a number of 
statistical approaches. These approaches may range from visual and summary statistics comparisons between Stage 
1 and Stage 2.2 batches, to statistical intervals combining Stage 1 and Stage 2.2 batches (with proper accounting 
for within and between campaign variability), or even Bayesian methodology using Stage 1 data to generate prior 
distributions for parameters.
No matter how it is done, combining Stage 1 and Stage 2.2 batches should be based on a scientific assessment 
of how representative the Stage 1 batches are using proper statistical methodology to assess data differences and 
account for campaign effects.
Following a risk-based assessment to determine the number of batches to be manufactured under Stage 2.2, the 
number of Stage 1 batches that could be included can be calculated.
In situations where multiple strengths of a drug product are manufactured using a similar process and will be 
validated collectively or separately, consideration should be given to whether grouping Stage 1 batch data is most 
appropriate. Factors should include:
•
Similarities in drug load and formulation
•
Process parameter Proven Acceptable Ranges (PAR)
•
Control strategy aspects
•
Historical comparative performance with respect to CQAs, etc.
Stage 1 batches should be considered based upon manufacturing date chronology, with the most recently 
manufactured batches studied first. No batches should be excluded simply based upon their performance results if 
that batch was manufactured in a manner representative of the intended commercial process.
Each Stage 1 batch should be individually evaluated to determine its suitability for use in the justification of the 
number of Stage 2.2 batches. Evaluation of the batches should be based on the representativeness or relatedness of 
the Stage 1 batches to the process under validation and not on the outcome of the process performance and testing 
results.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Note that the high-risk rank for several risk criteria is highlighted in bold red, which denotes that a Stage 1 batch 
meeting these criteria would automatically be excluded from consideration when determining the number of batches 
or assessing inter-batch variability, regardless of the results of the remainder of the risk assessment for that batch.
The cumulative risk points for a batch are compared to the criteria in Table 3.3 to determine the suitability of the Stage 
1 batch for inclusion in the combined Stage 1 and Stage 2.2 batches. The Overall Risk Scores presented in Table 
3.3 were constructed such that when 4 or more of the 10 criteria are determined to be medium risk, the overall risk is 
medium. Likewise, when there are 5 or more medium risks, or 2 or more high risks, the overall risk is high.
These criteria are sufficiently conservative to ensure that variability associated with the product and process are 
included in the data set while ensuring the batches selected do not represent unrealistic variability, which would result 
in artificially wide tolerance intervals when evaluating between batch variability.

3.4	
Determine Design Space and Control Strategy
3.4.1	
Determination of the Design Space
The design space is one of the QbD elements. Table 3.4 lists the terms described in ICH Q8(R2) [1] and outlined in 
the QbD roadmap, Figure 3.3. More information about QbD, how to identify CQAs, CPPs etc., can be found in the 
ISPE PQLI® Guides [7, 8].
The design space is optional to establish and submit to health authorities; however, the underlying methodology is 
very useful for generating process understanding, identifying CPPs, detecting interactions between parameters and/
or attributes, and establishing ranges for CPPs.
Table 3.4: Quality by Design Terminology Also Used for Process Validation
QbD Element
Acronym
Definition
Quality Target 
Product Profile
QTPP
A prospective summary of the quality characteristics of a drug product that ideally will be 
achieved to ensure the desired quality, taking into account safety and efficacy of the drug 
product. (ICH Q8(R2) [1])
Critical Quality 
Attribute
CQA
A physical, chemical, biological or microbiological property or characteristic that should be 
within an appropriate limit, range, or distribution to ensure the desired product quality. (ICH 
Q8(R2) [1])
Critical Process 
Parameter
CPP
A process parameter whose variability has an impact on a critical quality attribute and 
therefore should be monitored or controlled to ensure the process produces the desired 
quality. (ICH Q8(R2) [1])
Critical Material 
Attribute
Material 
CQA or 
CMA 
A critical quality attribute of a material (raw, feed, solvent, in-process, intermediate, 
excipient, drug substance, packaging, etc.) whose variability has an impact on a CQA and 
therefore should be monitored or controlled to ensure the process produces the desired 
quality.
Design Space
The multidimensional combination and interaction of input variables (e.g., material 
attributes) and process parameters that have been demonstrated to provide assurance 
of quality. Working within the design space is not considered as a change. Movement out 
of the design space is considered to be a change and would normally initiate a regulatory 
post approval change process. Design space is proposed by the applicant and is subject to 
regulatory assessment and approval. (ICH Q8(R2) [1])
Control Strategy
A planned set of controls, derived from current product and process understanding that 
ensures process performance and product quality. The controls can include parameters 
and attributes related to drug substance and drug product materials and components, 
facility and equipment operating conditions, in-process controls, finished product 
specifications, and the associated methods and frequency of monitoring and control. (ICH 
Q10) (ICH Q8(R2) [1])
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 34 =====
Page 32	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Figure 3.3: QbD Approach Showing Overarching Principles and Some Enabling Tools [7]
ICH Q8(R2) [1] defines design space as (cf. Table 3.4):
“The multidimensional combination and interaction of input variables (e.g., material attributes) and process 
parameters that have been demonstrated to provide assurance of quality.”
In other words, it is the multivariate combination of CMAs and CPPs that assures one or more CQA requirements 
are met. It is a process window, that when the process is operated in this specific space, the CQAs are met and the 
QTPP assured.
The understanding of the CQAs and the multivariate aspect are the main difference from the conventional method 
of establishing ranges for process parameters, PAR and Normal Operating Range (NOR), which does not reflect 
interactions and nonlinear effects quite common in complex biological, chemical, and physical systems.
ICH Q8(R2) defines PAR as [1]:
“A characterised range of a process parameter for which operation within this range, while keeping other 
parameters constant, will result in producing a material meeting relevant quality criteria.”
EMA defines NOR as a [31]:
“…Region around the target operating conditions that contains common operational variability (variability that 
can’t always be controlled).”
That is, NOR is a subset of PAR or of ranges derived from a multivariate design space. See Figure 3.4.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 35 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 33
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
Figure 3.4: Relationship between Knowledge Space, Design Space, and Derived Normal Operational Ranges
The complexity and robustness of the design space depends on the level of process understanding, the process 
complexity, the CPPs, CMAs, the number of unit operations covered, and the level of experimental work conducted to 
establish the design space.
Design space is established using an iterative science and risk-based approach linking the CQAs to the CPPs 
and CMAs (from raw material and other materials to be added to the process) and intermediate CQAs (e.g., 
blend uniformity or an intermediate impurity). It may include information regarding site, equipment, and scale of 
manufacture, depending on the level of process knowledge and the sensitivity of process performance on those 
aspects. See Figure 3.5.
Statistical considerations in design space development are not discussed here but can be found in articles such as 
“Statistical Considerations in Design Space Development,” Pharmaceutical Technology [32].
The process flow for design space determination as a QbD element of the product and process development process 
is discussed in detail in ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 1 – Concepts and Principles [7].
Figure 3.5: Iterative QbD Approach including QRM Steps [7]
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Establishing Design Space
The generation of design space begins with the QTPP and the related CQAs that it represents. The question is how 
the process and the materials for use affect these CQAs (factors).
Starting with a risk assessment identifying potential CPPs and potential CMAs results in identification of areas 
where there is a confirmed risk and a need to mitigate or control, or where additional work is needed for further 
understanding if risk is present.
Typically, for the additional work required, a DoE is used. The output from the experimental data is an identification of 
CPPs and CMAs or intermediate CQAs from the potential critical factors, and any interactions between these and the 
space where the process can be operated to meet the CQA expectations. See Figure 3.6.
The DoE also identifies the non-CPPs and material attributes with no impact on the CQAs that can be disregarded in 
the design space.
DoE as a multifactor tool is based on prior knowledge and requires a risk assessment focusing on material and 
process impact on the CQAs. The experiment design is established in such a manner that as few experiments as 
possible should be performed to get enough information to understand the impact on the CQAs. The risk assessment 
should be updated after the experiments and analysis to reflect the outcome from the DoE.
Examples of how to determine the design space can be found in detail in the ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 2 – Illustrative 
Example [8].
Figure 3.6: Typical Work Flow for Establishing a Design Space

Step 1: Quality Risk Assessment identifying potential CMAs and CPPs (see Figure 3.7)
