Step 4: Identification of CPPs and material CQAs and any interactions
Steps 5 and 6: Representation of the design space. The identification of CMAs and CPPs as well as the applicable 
design space where operation within will meet the CQA acceptance criteria, is an outcome of the DoE. The design 
space can be represented, for example, by a mathematical expression or as shown here, graphically (Figure 3.10). 
The right-hand picture shows an example of normal operational (operating) ranges.
Figure 3.10: Representation of the Design Space
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 39 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 37
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
The design space is a direct outcome of the DoE studies. The identified CMAs and CPPs can thus be selected for 
inclusion in the final design space. Independent design spaces can be presented for one or more unit operations, or 
there may be a single design space that covers multiple operations in the manufacturing process.
Multivariate Ranges – Used for Process Performance Qualification/Process Validation
The operating ranges for the CPPs can be the entire design space expressed as a set of dependent equations or a 
range; however, from a practical perspective, they are often extracted from the design space in a linear combination, 
shown in Figure 3.10 as the solid box, and each CPP range becomes a simple linear range from a minimal to a 
maximum value.
As these ranges are multivariate derived, the conventional definition of PAR cannot be applied. These DoE-derived 
multivariate-derived ranges of the CPPs are based on understanding the process and the potential interactions 
parameters and/or materials might have on the finished product quality.
When designing experiments to determine the multivariate ranges or PARs of CPPs, both measurement 
uncertainty and common cause variability of the commercial manufacturing equipment should be considered. Every 
measurement has uncertainty, and estimates based on samples should include sampling variability. Similarly, the 
common cause variability in regard to the ability to control a parameter should be taken into account.
To design processes that can be routinely managed within limits in the commercial manufacturing facility, develop 
experimental ranges wide enough to allow compensation for measurement uncertainty and common cause variability 
when the process is run in commercial manufacturing.
Measurement uncertainty is commonly managed by reducing the PAR or multivariate limits (as applicable) to a value 
inside the limit determined during development. These tighter limits can be included in the batch records as alert 
limits or decision limits. Typically, these new limits are determined based on a selected level of statistical assurance 
that the actual value is within the PAR or multivariate range for interacting parameters.
The likelihood of not meeting the CQA acceptance criteria when operating within these ranges is extremely low, 
assuming model uncertainty and response variability have been accounted for in estimating the design space. This is 
not the case when PARs have been established using the conventional OFAT approach.
The design space is likely established at the laboratory scale unless the laboratory and commercial scale are the 
same, as in the case of many continuous manufacturing operations. QRM and scale-up models/algorithms are used 
to scale the design space to pilot and/or commercial scale.
It is important that scalability of the design space and/or the multivariate ranges be addressed during process design 
to avoid having to redo the development work at commercial scale. The commercial proposed ranges are evaluated 
and tested after technology transfer, and set points and ranges defined for the PPQ.
3.4.2	
Determination of the Control Strategy
The control strategy is:
“A planned set of controls derived from current product and process understanding that assures process 
performance and product quality.” [6]
It is product centric and incorporates all those controls that should be implemented to ensure product safety, efficacy, 
and quality. It can be established using QbD principles or a traditional approach.
The control strategy aims at ensuring a product with the right CQAs and consistency is being manufactured and 
therefore in alignment with the expectations as expressed by the QTPP (patient focus). This focus should be applied 
throughout the product lifecycle from product and process design through to final product, including API and drug 
product manufacture, packaging, and distribution.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 40 =====
Page 38	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
The control strategy is vital to producing products and drives the strategy for how processes should be validated or 
verified during the PPQ stage (Stage 2.2). In short, the control strategy is verified at this stage.
Development of a control strategy requires a structured process involving a multi-disciplinary team of Subject 
Matter Experts (SMEs) linking pharmaceutical development to the manufacturing process and engineering controls 
of process equipment. This team may include representatives from formulation development, drug substance 
development, process development, analytical development, Quality Control (QC), Quality Assurance (QA), 
Regulatory Affairs, Manufacturing, Engineering, Validation, and PAT. See Figure 3.11.
To ensure that a product with the right CQAs is manufactured, CPPs, CMAs, and intermediate CQAs should be 
identified and controlled as elements of the control strategy. Ideally the process should be designed to accommodate 
variability during processing to ensure that the final product CQAs are consistent within and between batches. QbD 
principles and PAT tools are enablers in this case.
Figure 3.11: A Simple Illustration of the Different Activities from Development to Designed Facility, Input, 
Interactions, and Output
Patient  Product
The patient requirements or expectations in terms of clinical performance, drug delivery method etc., are input on how 
the product ideally should be designed. The patient’s needs are expressed by the Voice of the Customer and outlined 
in the QTPP, where the high-level quality characteristics are described, including administration form, dosage, purity, 
container closure system, etc.
Product  Process
From the defined QTPP, the CQAs are identified and rated according to criticalities such as patient safety, product 
release kinetics, product interaction with primary packaging materials, etc. The process steps are evaluated for 
their impact on the CQAs, and thus the critical process steps are identified. Further discussion on this criticality 
assessment is found in the ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 1 – Concepts and Principles [7].
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 41 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 39
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
For legacy products this might be an opportunity for improvement, as typically neither the QTPP nor the CQAs are 
defined. It is useful to define CQAs for a legacy product as it puts the validation into perspective (validation efforts 
commensurate with the level of risk to the patient and therefore risk from changes to the CQAs). Several discussion 
papers on how to define CQAs for legacy products have been published by ISPE [33, 34, 35].
For the critical process steps, the CPPs are identified as those process parameters having a direct impact on a CQA. 
In a similar way, CMAs are identified. These need to be monitored and/or controlled.
Process  Production
For each identified critical process step, the risk is determined according to the severity rating of the CQA, the facility-
dependent evaluation of occurrence for the failure in question, and the probability of detecting the failure if it happens.
The outcome of the process risk assessment (e.g., a process FMECA or FMEA) provides input to the control strategy, 
including the identified risk control measures, and/or monitoring setup.
Production  Equipment/System
The aspects of manufacturing systems and equipment that can impact CQA, for example a CPP and its control 
systems or the design of a piece of equipment, should be identified during the process risk assessment, and if 
relevant, included in the equipment qualification plan (Stage 2.2).
Critical Aspects
The identified part of the equipment or manufacturing systems that are aspects as discussed above are often referred 
to as Critical Aspects (CAs). These are further discussed in Section 4.1 and in ASTM E2500 [36], which provides this 
definition:
“Critical aspects of manufacturing systems are typically functions, features, abilities, and performance or 
characteristics necessary for the manufacturing process and systems to ensure consistent product quality 
and patient safety. They should be identified and documented based on scientific product and process 
understanding.”
The CAs can also be derived from the control strategy as discussed later in this chapter.
Intermediate Critical Quality Attributes
In Figure 3.12, a manufacturing process is illustrated with a set of process steps. During Stage 1, CPPs for each 
step were identified, showing how the different process steps can be characterized in terms of intermediate CQAs 
– a quality attribute of an intermediate stage that has a direct impact on a final product CQA. In the case of an oral 
solid dosage product, blend uniformity is normally an intermediate CQA as it has a direct impact on the CQA Content 
Uniformity of the final product.
The intermediate CQA might be measured directly by a process analyzer (e.g., NIR for blend uniformity) or measured 
indirectly by a process indicator (e.g., power consumption) during processing.
An example of how the control strategy controls CQAs and CPPs during processing is shown in Figure 3.12. The 
measured output of each step is preferable as an intermediate CQA or a final product CQA; however, if these 
cannot be measured directly, other measurements might be possible that indicate the performance and quality of the 
process. These are referred to as process indicators.
The outcome of the measurement of the intermediate CQAs or process indicators is used to adjust the process in 
real time to minimize variability during processing. There might be cases where a decision has been made to test 
the product CQAs at the end of processing rather than measuring and controlling intermediate CQAs or process 
indicators during processing. This decision should be made keeping the patient risk in mind.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 42 =====
Page 40	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Figure 3.12: Robust and Effective Control Strategy Example
The control strategy evolves throughout the product lifecycle. During development, the focus is on “how to establish 
the product and process” and “what to control and how” to ensure the CQAs and meet the expectations of the QTPP.
Later, after scale-up and technology transfer, the control strategy should be updated and verified to reflect any 
changes in parameter ranges or set points due to changes in scale and equipment type/brand.
The control strategy should also be expanded to include controls related to the process equipment, utilities, facility, 
the process control system, cleaning aspects, and supporting quality controls to ensure product quality.
3.4.2.1	 Manufacturing Control Strategy
The expanded overarching control strategy used during commercial manufacturing is often considered as the 
‘Manufacturing Control Strategy’. It helps ensure patient safety and product quality during manufacturing by 
establishing a robust process operated within suitable equipment ranges that are controlled by the manufacturing 
control strategy. This strategy connects all of the controls from CQAs, CMAs, CPPs, equipment, facility, and other 
GMP controls.
Figure 3.13 illustrates the regulatory relationship between the control strategy and the manufacturing control strategy. 
The control strategy established during development linking CQAs, CPPs, CMAs and intermediate CQAs needs 
regulatory approval, whereas the overall manufacturing control strategy is subject to health authority inspection as it 
includes GMP controls not normally submitted within the dossier.
Figure 3.13: The Manufacturing Control Strategy: The Expansion of the Control Strategy to Include 
Equipment, Facility, GMP, and Process Performance Controls
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 43 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 41
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
The manufacturing control strategy may include, but is not limited to:
•
Controls related to the fundamental control strategy
-
Control of CMAs: CMAs such as drug substance, excipients, primary packaging materials and their sampling
plans, analytical methods, and acceptance criteria
-
Control of CQAs: Intermediate and product CQAs, specifications, sampling plans, analytical methods,
acceptance criteria, procedural controls
-
Controls of CPPs and other process parameters, operating ranges, set points, process control systems
-
Predictive models (if submitted)
-
A monitoring program (e.g., full product testing at regular intervals) for verifying multivariate prediction models
•
Controls added to the manufacturing control strategy:
-
Facility controls, utilities, cleaning, environmental systems monitoring, process control systems, operating
conditions
-
Controls for unit operations that have no impact on product quality but are important for operating a robust
and consistent process
3.4.2.2	 Production Control Strategy
Controls related to business performance might also be included in the manufacturing control strategy as it embraces 
all controls relevant from a patient protection and business point of view.
These “business controls” might not be subject to health authority inspections as they are not covered by GMP, but 
to limit some complexity, firms may manage them in the same way as GMP controls. This is a decision that has to be 
made at the site.
A model using PQLI® [8] as an example (see Figure 3.14) is helpful when defining the production control strategy.
Figure 3.14: The PQLI® Control Strategy Model Applied to Identify the Production Control Strategy
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
The PQLI® Control Strategy Model in Figure 3.14 illustrates how patient and business requirements can be 
considered in parallel. The process can be described as follows:
1.	
CQAs and other requirements are identified.
2.	
CPPs, CMAs, intermediate CQAs, and CAs on equipment identified.
3.	
The analytical, engineering, automation, PAT, and other controls required for the identified parameters and 
attributes are defined. Typical controls could include:
•	
Off-line testing of an attribute of a starting material, component, or in-process material
•	
Monitoring and feedback control of a material attribute during processing (manual or automated)
•	
Monitoring and feedback control of an equipment parameter during processing (manual or automated)
•	
Monitoring and feed forward control of a material attribute or process parameter (manual or automated)
•	
Procedural control of an operation
•	
Equipment and facility design
•	
Process design, for example the addition of specific operations during drug substance manufacture to 
reduce the level of impurities
•	
Procedures to be followed in the event of a malfunction
Using this approach for establishing the control strategy results in clear links between CQAs, process steps, CMAs, 
CPPs, and actual controls. An example for an oral solid dosage product can be found in the ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 
2 – Illustrative Example [8].
An example of a control strategy table is shown in Table 3.5. Not only does such a control table provide a clear 
overview of the submitted control strategy but it can also be used to plan for process qualification activities. The table 
can be expanded with more specific columns to include activities to be performed during PV Stages 2.1, 2.2, and 3. 
Examples of activities for Stages 2.1 and 2.2 are shown in Tables 3.6 and 3.7, respectively.

Often process equipment are qualified using a generic approach not related to a specific product. In such cases, a 
gap analysis should be conducted to ensure the qualification performed is in line with the product-specific control 
strategy; that is, the design of the equipment reflects the design proposed during Stage 1 and the equipment has 
been qualified over the range of the specified CPPs. If gaps are identified, the remaining qualification activities should 
be performed.

3.5	
Validation Master Plan
A Validation Master Plan (VMP) formally communicates steps to be taken and the deliverables required for PV. EU 
Annex 15 Clauses 1.4 and 1.5 [11] provide clear direction on VMPs. It is expected that the key elements of the site 
qualification and validation program be clearly defined and documented in a VMP or equivalent document.
Additionally, the VMP should define the qualification/validation system necessary to execute the validation effort.
A structure for the VMP includes scope, references, responsibilities, risk management, and methodology. In cases 
where the system is not overly complex, a VMP can be combined with the validation protocol.

Why write a separate plan?
•	
It provides the strategy and rationale for design.
•	
It gives alignment with partners (e.g., stability, engineering, cleaning).
•	
Stability protocols can be written from information in the plan, which may be ready long before the protocol.
•	
It can be used to approve bracketing.
•	
It stipulates the justification for manufacturing at risk requirements.
While a plan often accelerates preparation on the front end of the project, the report needs to be cross checked 
against two documents (plan and protocol) to assure all requirements have been met.
3.6	
Readiness for Stage 2 (Process Performance Qualification)
PV Stage 1 Checklist
Prior to PV Stage 2.2 (the process validation exercise), it is recommended that firms perform a risk assessment using 
knowledge from Stage 1. Table 3.8 is an example of a high-level checklist that can be utilized to help assure PV 
prerequisite data has been obtained from Development.
Table 3.8: Example Process Validation Stage 1 Checklist	
Prerequisite
Status
CQAs and limits determined
CPP ranges appropriately stressed to reflect operational capability at intended commercial site
Map of CQAs impacted by each unit operation, and associated CPPs impacting those CQAs in each unit operation 
(where applicable) completed
Identification of critical raw materials and understanding of variability with respect to product quality achieved
Design space/multivariate interactions identified
Design space models fully verified or validated
Meaning of identified PAR understood by Manufacturing and documented. This is important as although the ICH 
Q8(R2) definition of PAR is univariate [1], PAR is utilized by some firms to represent a multivariate limit.
Validation of scale-down models considered if applicable
Effects of scale fully explored or not. Manufacturing Clinical Trial and/or development batches at full scale during PV 
Stage 1 can greatly improve knowledge of viability of the commercial manufacturing process.
Available information about impact of extended control strategy (such as hold time limits between various unit 
operations) reviewed
Control strategy risk assessment finalized
Understanding of impact of variation on product and process quality attained. PV will proceed more smoothly with 
more accurate results when a concerted effort to introduce variability and study impact has occurred during Stage 1.
Knowledge management plan prepared. This plan should explain the transfer of information to Manufacturing and 
how process knowledge documentation will be updated as learning occurs during commercial manufacturing.
VMP in place

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Assessing the probability of failing specifications and/or PPQ criteria to determine if advancing to Stage 2 is 
appropriate and recommended. One approach is to use heatmaps/contour plots of development data (or historical 
data for in-line products) to assess this risk. Vukovinsky et al. [37] and Burdick et al. (see Chapters 3 and 9) [30] 
provide examples of how this can be done.
Validation Readiness
The decision to commence Stage 2.2 PPQ should be taken when Stage 2.1 qualification activities have been 
approved and readiness has been demonstrated, that is, the risk of not being able to manufacture a consistent 
product meeting its CQA acceptance criteria (specification) is low.
Validation exercises are time consuming and costly, and failing validation due to equipment issues, lack of training, 
missing analytical or cleaning validation studies or other issues is avoidable when proper care and attention are 
exhibited via a readiness checklist or project management.
The purpose of the validation readiness assessment is to ensure a successful validation by identifying issues 
before the validation process begins. A systematic approach is recommended to assess and learn from previous 
experiences (collect and analyze data); to identify recurring issues; and to develop a mitigation plan that resolves 
issues before PPQ execution.
Written and approved prior to the execution of PV, the readiness assessment is often a compilation of assessments 
written by various manufacturing and quality system owners covering principal activities prior to PV. An overall 
summary written by technical experts is included.
Sites perform formal (documented) assessments of supporting equipment (including evaluation of validated 
equipment ranges as compared with the operational ranges of the intended process), utility, system, method 
validation, and training to ensure supporting processes/systems/procedures/training are in place for validation.
This more detailed readiness checklist (Table 3.9) can be used as a project management tool to plan for PPQ and to 
evaluate the risk of moving forward to PPQ.

4	 Design and Qualification of Facility, 
	
Utilities, and Equipment (Stage 2.1)
4.1	
Overview and Background
Part of the objective of PV is to ensure the fitness of facilities and equipment for intended use, commonly known as 
Stage 2.1 [3]. (Note: see Chapter 2 for a discussion on the different terminology used in PV.) Since extensive guides 
to the design and qualification of facilities, utilities, and equipment are available from various sources such as ISPE 
[40] and ASTM E2500 [36], this chapter provides only an overview of these activities.
Manufacturers need to control the CAs of their operations through qualification and validation over the lifecycle of the 
product and process. CAs, which are identified in the design space (see Figure 3.6), include equipment used in the 
manufacturing and analysis of the product.
Equipment and systems should be well designed and capable of consistently manufacturing quality products [3, 11]. 
The approach to the design and qualification of facilities, utilities, and equipment is based on sound science and the 
manufacturer’s overall level of product and process understanding to demonstrate control in achieving a consistent 
and robust product.
Cumulative data from all relevant studies and assessments are used to establish the manufacturing conditions. ISPE 
Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification [40] defines approaches for facilities, utilities, and equipment 
commissioning as well as qualification processes. It is highly recommended to comprehensively understand and 
apply the approaches outlined in the baseline guide as each project is different. The Baseline® Guide promotes Good 
Engineering Practices (GEP), commissioning, and qualification practices for related engineering systems [40].
This chapter incorporates science and risk-based quality system approaches and reviews the impact products and 
processes have on Stage 2.1 activities prior to the initiation of Stage 2.2 PPQ.
The ASTM E2500 Standard Guide for Specification, Design, and Verification of Pharmaceutical and 
Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Systems and Equipment [36] includes a new approach to enable more flexibility 
at this stage. This ASTM guide describes a systematic, efficient, and effective way of ensuring that manufacturing 
systems and equipment are fit for intended use, and that risks to product quality, and consequently to patient safety, 
are effectively managed to the extent that the risks are affected by such systems and equipment, including the 
following key concepts:
•	
Risk-based approach
•	
Science-based approach
•	
CAs of manufacturing systems
•	
QbD
•	
GEP
•	
Subject matter expert
•	
Use of vendor documentation
•	
Continuous process improvement
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 52 =====
Page 50	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Verification is a systematic approach to verify that manufacturing systems, acting singly or in combination, are fit for 
intended use, have been properly installed, and are operating correctly. This is an umbrella term that encompasses 
all types of approaches to assure systems are fit for use, such as qualification, commissioning and qualification, 
verification, system validation, or other [36]. The level of effort, formality, and documentation of the QRM process 
should be commensurate with the level of risk.
4.2	
Review of Establishing Equipment Qualification Ranges
This section reviews the elements required for establishing equipment ranges to be qualified. DoE studies conducted 
at Stage 1 establish the design space for the product, wherein operating parameters are defined as part of the initial 
control strategy to ensure the desired QTPP is met. The control strategy is verified in Stage 2.2 and continually 
improved in Stage 3.
Equipment to be used in the manufacturing process needs to be qualified. Qualification should demonstrate that the 
equipment is designed appropriately, built to suit user and process requirements, and fit for its intended use. ISPE 
Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification [40] provides advice and guidance relevant for qualification of 
equipment that is generally accepted industry wide.
Section 3.4 of this PV Guide describes how to define CPPs related to CQAs. Typically, this is done by identifying the 
range in which equipment and facilities need to be qualified to ensure they are capable of consistently producing 
specified product or results. See the example in Table 4.1.

4.3	
Impact of Product and Process on Facilities, Utilities, and Systems
The introduction of a new product and/or process has an impact on existing or newly commissioned facilities, utilities, 
and systems. Multiple factors should be considered for a new product launch or site transfers to a new or existing 
facility. This section describes industry best practices and factors to consider when introducing a new product, 
process, or both that impact facilities, utilities, and other systems.
The direct impact system is designed and commissioned in line with GEPs as well as qualification practices. In 
relation to facilities, utilities, and equipment, the following factors should be considered:
•
GMP design and installation with operational, maintenance, statutory, and regulatory requirements, as well as
other elements such as health, safety, and environmental conditions
•
Documentation of design, drawings, tests, manuals, inspections, and certifications
•
Construction management, standard procurement practices, installation, and commissioning

It is good practice to include a design verification document covering all of the facilities, equipment, and utilities

Technology Transfer Considerations
Process design stages and a technical transfer project should consider the risks associated with the transfer of 
product/process from Research and Development to commercial manufacturing, or in the transfer of product/process 
between sites.

minimum base of documentation in support of transfers. A gap analysis comparing the product control strategy 
and technical risk assessment with the facility, utility, system, and equipment should be performed to identify the 
requirements associated with adapting existing equipment or the need for acquiring new equipment. Assessment 
considerations are summarized in Table 4.2.

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
5	 Initial Process Qualification Activities 
(Stage 2.2)
The goal of PPQ is to demonstrate that the activities in Stage 1 have yielded a process/product design and control 
strategy that will deliver a commercial manufacturing process that will consistently perform as expected. (Note: this 
Guide uses Stage 2.2 and PPQ for this verification portion of PV. See Chapter 2 for a discussion on the different 
terminology used in PV.)
For a successful PPQ, good planning is key and essential for activities such as:
•
Determining the number of PPQ batches
•
Producing a well written protocol providing instructions for execution
•
Establishing the representative number of samples to assess process performance and to determine variability
•
Developing clear specification of acceptance criteria and expectations for successful PV
•
Evaluating data and test results obtained from PPQ
•
Generating a concise summary of test results, observations from PPQ batches, and a closure statement
concluding acceptance of the validation activity
This chapter provides guidance for PPQ planning, discussing strategies for determining the number of PPQ batches, 
developing the sampling plan and acceptance criteria (with some examples), and evaluating and interpreting the data 
generated from PPQ batches.
5.1	
Determine an Appropriate Number of Initial Process Validation Batches and 
Inter-Batch Variability Assessment
Traditionally, PV acceptance criteria were based on the number of batches produced during validation. Under a 
science and risk-based paradigm, the focus also includes determining the degree of control exhibited over the 
process (inter-batch variability).
Determining the number of batches necessary to demonstrate that acceptable control over the process has been 
achieved begins with assessing residual risk based on product knowledge, process understanding, and control 
strategy effectiveness (see Figure 5.1). Turning this risk knowledge into the justification for the number of validation 
batches to manufacture can be based on experience, statistics, or a combination of both.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 56 =====
Page 54	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Figure 5.1: Process Risk Assessment Translating into the Number of Batches
The number of PPQ batches depends on the residual risk remaining following Stage 1 and the expected inter-
batch variability, with an understanding of the sources that influence product quality. Manufacturers should justify 
the number of validation batches and the design of the PPQ study based on this product knowledge and process 
understanding (see Table 5.1).
Table 5.1: Example Elements of Process Risk Assessment, Prior to Process Validation Stage 2.2
Risk Assessment Following Stage 1
The risk assessment should consider the knowledge gained through Stage 1 activities and the similarity of the Stage 
1 manufacturing process to the process undergoing PPQ.
For example, when process knowledge is high – including an understanding of process robustness to the input 
variability from raw materials, excipients, and drug substance – then the residual risk is low and a minimally-sized 
PPQ could be appropriate, for example, three batches.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 57 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 55
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
When the risk assessment identifies some potential knowledge gaps (e.g., only one excipient batch was used in 
Stage 1), then there is a higher residual risk and it is appropriate to include a larger number of PPQ batches to 
address that gap. For example, when multiple excipient batches are included in the PPQ, more PPQ batches may be 
needed.
The risk assessment following Stage 1 should be a structured evaluation of the knowledge and evidence that the 
manufacturing process is capable and robust, and that the control strategy ensures acceptable product variability.
An example, based on the inter-related considerations of product knowledge, process understanding, and process 
control strategy, comes from ASTM E2475 [42] and is detailed in Sections 5.1.1 – 5.1.3.
Manufacturers may choose other factors to include in their risk assessment, place maximum emphasis on the control 
strategy, or both.
The assessment should:
•
Consider all product CQAs, either in combination or individually. If considered individually, the assessment
should include the CQAs with the highest associated residual risk and highest associated severity levels. The
number of PPQ batches should be commensurate with the highest residual risk.
•
Be performed by a cross-functional team with representation from manufacturing, technical services, process
development, quality assurance, statistics, and PV.
•
Be aligned with QRM principles and provide justification for the evaluation and ranking of each factor.
5.1.1	
Assess Product Knowledge [42]
Identified product quality requirements as outlined in ICH Q8(R2) [1] are referred to as the QTPP, which can be 
further related to the CQAs of the drug product or drug substance.
At a minimum, the severity and probability of variation that might impact product safety or efficacy should be 
documented in the risk assessment. The confidence in CQA acceptance ranges depends on the strength of 
experimental data (laboratory, clinical, non-clinical), predictions/information from proven models, and other sources of 
knowledge.
Even in circumstances where the drug’s mechanism of action is not well understood, efforts made to understand 
CQAs may help to mitigate the risk to safety or efficacy.
5.1.2	
Assess Process Understanding [42]
Understanding the relationships between material attributes, process parameters, and CQAs is the fundamental tenet 
of assurance of product quality. The design and effectiveness of the control strategy depends on the correctness of 
these relationships.
Process understanding can be established from several sources, including:
•
Development phase – an understanding of variability from development and product characterization, primarily
from Stage 1 of the product lifecycle, including impact of material CQA variability and input and parameter
interaction. This depends on sufficient analytical capability to evaluate the consequences of variability.
Process understanding from the development phase may be lacking for legacy products or for generic
manufacturers, thus requiring process understanding from other sources. However, in the case of legacy
products, production data can be evaluated in place of Stage 1 data.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 58 =====
Page 56	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
•
Prior knowledge – understanding from prior experience with similar processes or platform technology. For a
mature product, APRs, product quality reviews, deviation investigations, complaint investigations, and change
control information may be used as sources of process understanding.
•
Degree of process understanding/unit operation – the extent of knowledge gained/explored during the
development of each unit operation, including the potential impact from personnel, selection and performance of
equipment, and other factors such as environmental considerations.
•
Process predictability and modeling – understanding the effect of scale on performance, and the usefulness of a
laboratory or small-scale model to predict the effects of variation at commercial scale.
5.1.3	
Assess Control Strategy Risk [42]
The process control strategy evolves through the development of process and product knowledge in Stage 1. It is 
designed to control input variability from materials, environment, and operational practice so that the desired product 
quality and performance is maintained or improved.
Factors that could be considered in the risk assessment of the control strategy include:
•
Raw material variability: The potential impact and management of variability of CMAs and interaction with other
input or control variables
•
Equipment capability versus process requirements: Manufacturing facilities and process equipment are qualified
for the commercial process. These activities may include batches produced at full scale, or at a scale that is
considered representative of full scale. Equipment capability derived from these qualifications can be compared
with process requirements as one contributor to risk determinations.
•
Experiences with process performance: Successful performance of development or historical batches resulting
from adequate management of process variability and control for scale effects (as applicable) provides evidence
of process reproducibility.
5.1.4	
Determine Residual Risk Level
Following the risk assessment of the factors of product, process, and control strategy understanding, a residual risk 
level should be determined. This residual risk level reflects the confidence in performance of the commercial process 
and can be used as an input to the decision for determining the appropriate number of PPQ batches.
When high residual risk is not minimized or mitigated, it is reasonable to expect that more batches will be required to 
confirm that the process is capable of reproducible commercial manufacture; however, increasing the number of PPQ 
batches is not a substitute for insufficient process development or understanding. Reasonable efforts should be made 
to identify and mitigate higher risks before attempting process qualification.
Application of a QbD approach to development of the process, product, and control strategy should result in lower 
residual risk by improving process understanding, and therefore minimize the number of required PPQ batches.
Identification of a severe or high residual risk indicates there are significant gaps in process knowledge, control 
strategy, or both. Additional effort should be invested in improving process knowledge and the control strategy before 
preparing PPQ batches.
Further explanation of the ASTM E2475-based risk assessment and scoring system [42] is located in Appendix 5 
(Section 13.1), along with an example application to the ISPE PQLI® Guide Series Part 2 – Illustrative Example, 
Product and Process [8] (Appendix 5 (Section 13.2)).
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 59 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 57
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
5.1.5	
Determine the Minimum Number of Batches (N)
It is recognized that a complete assessment of inter-batch variability and process robustness cannot be known until 
Stage 3 of the PV lifecycle due to limited exposure to inter-batch sources of variability, small numbers of Stage 1 and 
PPQ batches, and other factors. However, some remaining uncertainty regarding process variation after Stage 2 does 
not prevent progression to commercial manufacture (Stage 3), assuming there is evidence that this variation does not 
pose patient risk. (See Table 5.1: Example Elements of Process Risk Assessment, Prior to Process Validation Stage 
2.2.)
The number of PPQ batches1 (M) may be different from the number of batches (N, N ≥ M) used in the PPQ report to 
evaluate inter-batch variability.2
The minimum number of batches (N) should be aligned with the residual risk (discussed earlier in this 
section) remaining from Stage 1. A process that has higher residual risk should require more batches to provide 
enough assurance that the inter-batch variability is adequately controlled before progression to routine commercial 
manufacture. On the other hand, a process that has low residual risk should require fewer batches.
Batches manufactured in Stage 1 can count toward the minimum number of batches (N) required to 
determine inter-batch variability. Use of Stage 1 batch data in the PPQ report is discussed in Section 3.3. Per FDA 
PV Guidance of 2011 [3], it is an expectation that Stage 1 data be considered together with PPQ data in making a 
validation conclusion.
The justification for the minimum number of batches (N) should be documented and approved prior to 
execution of the PPQ protocol. These justifications fall into two general categories: experience based and 
statistically based. A combination of the two approaches is also possible.
Many different approaches have been proposed in the literature, including two ISPE discussion papers published in 
2012 (updated in 2014) [34] and 2016 [43]. Although no approach is currently considered best practice across the 
industry, a number of manufacturers apply a risk-based approach that relies on scientific rationales and experience to 
determine the number of batches.
In all cases, PPQ performance should be evaluated, along with relevant Stage 1 results, for sufficient evidence of 
acceptable inter-batch and intra-batch variability, along with any potential impact to quality. This evaluation continues 
in Stage 3.
Assurance of robustness to expected sources of variability such as raw material, equipment train, operator, and 
environmental conditions can influence the number of PPQ batches. Bracketing, matrixing, or a product family 
approach can be applied to specific situations such as multiple dosage strengths, package sizes, batch sizes, or 
division of drug substance batches into multiple drug product batches.
No matter the approach, the objective of PPQ is to establish scientific evidence that the process is reproducible 
and will consistently deliver quality products. The observed inter and intra-batch variation should be as expected 
based on prior performance and scientific rationale. These claims should include all relevant evidence of process 
understanding, control, and performance from both Stage 1 and PPQ.
1	 This is the number of batches manufactured as part of the PPQ protocol (M).
2	 These are the combined Stage 1 and Stage 2.2 batches as discussed in Section 3.4 (N).
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
5.2	
Determine Science and Statistically-Based Process Validation Acceptance Criteria 
and Sampling Plans
The development of suitable sampling plans and associated acceptance criteria are essential to achieving the 
objectives of Stage 2.2. These activities are best addressed by incorporating the scientific knowledge of the product 
and process with statistical sampling methodology. It is important that science and statistics are leveraged so that the 
plans are rigorous and practical.
5.2.1	
Sampling
“There is a wide body of literature on sampling. Sampling plans are used to generate the required samples that 
will be tested to support release of manufactured batches, PV, or for process understanding.
“A sampling plan describes where (locations) and how the samples are taken from the batch, and the number 
of samples taken from each location. The sampling plan should ensure that the data collected is representative 
of the parameter to be measured and that the sample size is sufficient to support the conclusions needed for 
process qualification.” [44]
The choice of acceptance criteria is part of generating a sampling plan. The sampling plan should be justified; 
Operating Characteristic (OC) curves are useful for this purpose. There are three common sampling strategies:
1.
Simple Random Sampling
“Simple random sampling gives each possible unit an equal probability of being chosen to be tested. A true
random sample from a batch could theoretically be obtained by identifying each unit in the batch and then by
a completely random process, pick ‘n’ units. To obtain a true simple random sample is nearly impossible or
impractical for sampling a batch of units.” [44]
An example that is generally considered a simple random sample is taking a sample from a coating pan, since
the tablets have been continuously mixed up during the coating process. A sample of units, for example 30
tablets, from the coating pan is considered a random sample.
2.
Stratified Random Sampling
It is possible with simple random sampling that, just by chance, the samples may not contain units from
segments of the batch that are of interest. “For example, in process validation, the beginning and end of the
batch may be of interest. So, although a simple random sample is statistically valid, one may not be satisfied with
a sampling plan that does not include samples taken from the beginning or end of the batch.” [44]
A stratified sampling strategy partitions the batch into “strata,” or groups, where differences may exist between
the strata. The combination of all strata must cover the entire batch. Then random sampling is performed within
each stratum.
For example, the lot may consist of 500,000 dosage units during a batch process. The strata could be defined as
20 sets of 25,000 dosage units where strata 1 is the first 25,000 dosage units in the run, strata 2 is the second
25,000 dosage units, etc. In this case, each stratum represents a part of the batch (a drum in tablet production; a
tray/tub in vial/syringe fill-finish process) producing 25,000 dosage units. Then a random sample from each part
of the batch, such as 2, would be taken to obtain a total of 50 dosage units for testing.
3.
Systematic Sampling
“A systematic sampling strategy is performed by taking a sample(s) at equal intervals throughout the batch,
typically based on the total number of units or manufacturing time. The first sample location is determined at
random and then the remaining samples are taken at equal intervals.” [44]
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 61 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 59
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
	
For example, using manufacturing time, assume the manufacturing run takes 15 hours and the desired sample 
size is 30 dosage units. The time can be divided into 30 half-hour intervals. A time is selected in the first interval 
at random – 10 minutes; then samples would be taken at 10 minutes, 40 minutes, 1 hour 10 minutes, etc.
	
In practice, the first and last samples may be taken from the beginning and end of the batch. Then the remaining 
18 samples could be done using a random time in the second interval. This is typically acceptable as long as 
the starting point and subsequent sampling points do not fix the samples in such a way as to potentially miss 
important elements of the process.
The difference between stratified and systematic sampling strategies is that stratified samples are taken at random 
within each stratum whereas systematic samples are taken at specified equal intervals usually based on time or total 
number of units in the batch.
In practice, the systematic samples may not be taken exactly at the specified points but effort is taken to be as close 
to these points as possible, which is generally acceptable.
It also may be desirable to include the beginning and end of the batch as sampling time points. In this case, for 
example, if a total of 20 sampling points were desired throughout the batch, the beginning and end could be included, 
and then the other 18 samples could be taken using a stratified or systematic sampling plan.
The three sampling strategies described above provide ways to select locations for sampling. Typically for simple 
random samples, only one unit is tested from each location. For stratified or systematic sampling plans, one unit (or 
blend sample) or multiple units could be tested from each location.
In this document, if only one unit is tested from each location then the sampling plan is called Sampling Plan 1. 
Figure 5.2 plots 20 results for each sampling strategy using Sampling Plan 1. Notice that the random sampling plan 
produced a long gap in the batch.
Figure 5.2: Comparison of Sampling Strategies
Combinations of different strategies could be used if justified.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Page 60	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Sampling may be performed following specific events (e.g., breakdown, machine alterations) where standard 
adjustments were applied to ensure the process remains centered. These additional sampling locations should 
be evaluated during development or prior to validation, since the acceptance criteria assume that locations in the 
batch are not purposely selected. A company could perform additional sampling at selected locations to provide 
supplementary information for inclusion in the validation document; but this data may require different acceptance 
criteria than those that have been developed for the three sampling plans described above due to non-random 
sampling.
In process development and during PPQ, it is often of interest to understand the sources of variability in a process. 
In particular, it is of value to understand the size and relative comparisons of the within location and between location 
variability.
Using Sampling Plan 1, one cannot determine whether the variation among units is caused more by differences 
between locations in the batch or by variation within a location. However, by testing more than one unit from each 
location, called Sampling Plan 2, the variation between locations can be separated from the variation within location 
using a statistical procedure called Variance Components Analysis (VCA).
Figure 5.3 is a plot of the same systematic plan data as in Figure 5.2, but with two additional samples taken from 
each location. There appears to be a lot of variation within locations.
Figure 5.3: Systematic Sampling Strategy with Three Results per Location
An example of using a VCA for a sampling plan with 20 locations with 3 results per location is given for CU in Section 5.3.
5.2.2	
Goal for Process Performance Qualification Sampling Plans
In order to design appropriate sample plans and criteria, it is essential first to determine how to define successful 
PPQ performance for each parameter. A parameter could be continuous or discrete data. The key is to develop 
a plan that provides confidence that the manufacturing process will consistently produce product that meets all 
specifications.
Criticality and risk should also be considered when developing sampling plans and criteria. It is reasonable to have 
greater scrutiny on parameters that have the greatest risk in terms of consistently meeting quality requirements.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 63 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 61
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
It is also of significant importance to consider the risk to the patient. Drug products with greater severity, potential for 
harm, or both when exhibiting too much variability or being off target may require a higher level of sampling and/or 
more stringent criteria.
One possible goal for a PPQ sampling plan is to provide confidence that the parameter of interest will have a high 
probability of passing the release requirements during ongoing supply. Each manufacturer will need to define and 
justify the level of confidence needed in the probability of passing the release requirements. These levels may vary 
based on the risk associated with the attribute.
When using statistical approaches for defining sampling plans and criteria, OC curves for the proposed PPQ plan and 
the release test can be used to assess a PPQ plan’s performance and its performance relative to the release test.
Figure 5.4 is an example of a PPQ and release test OC curve comparison. In this example, the probability of passing 
the release test is shown as a function of the variation in the sample.
As the data has more variation within the batch (i.e., larger within batch standard deviations), the release test is less 
likely to be passed. This is why the standard deviation of the data is placed on the x-axis. The y-axis is the probability 
to pass the sampling plan.
In this example, the relationship between the PPQ test and the release test OC curves have been set such that 
when the release test has a 95% chance of passing, the PPQ test has less than a 5% chance of passing. The PPQ 
test has been made stricter (harder to pass) than the release test to provide assurance that the PPQ study will not 
be successful when the process produces product that would have less than a 95% chance of passing its expected 
quality requirements.
Sampling plans and acceptance criteria are typically made stricter by increasing the sample size and/or adjusting the 
acceptance criteria. Examples provided in Section 5.3 illustrate this concept.
Figure 5.4: Process Performance Qualification and Release Test Operating Characteristic Curve Comparison
The PPQ OC curve shown in Figure 5.4 is for a single batch. In addition to demonstrating appropriate levels of 
confidence/conservativeness on a batch basis, a manufacturer may leverage the information across the batches used 
in the PPQ for a target overall level of conservativeness demonstrated by the PPQ exercise.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 64 =====
Page 62	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Figure 5.5 demonstrates this by showing a specified level of assurance for individual batches compared to the 
release test OC curve. Additionally, it shows, in the case of a three-batch PPQ, the OC curve for all three PPQ 
batches combined, that is, the OC curve shows the probability that all three batches pass the PPQ acceptance 
criteria (assuming the three batches are independent).
Figure 5.5: Operating Characteristic Curve of the Probability of Passing a Three-Batch Process Performance 
Qualification Compared to Operating Characteristic Curves for Passing Individual Process Performance 
Qualification Batches and the Release Test
In Figure 5.5, the OC curve for a single PPQ batch is significantly more conservative than for the release test (though 
not as conservative as in Figure 5.4). Additionally, when the three-batch PPQ is considered jointly as an overall 
criterion where each batch must independently pass the one-batch PPQ criteria, the OC curve for the overall three-
batch PPQ is much more conservative.
In this case, when there is a true within batch variability of 6% of Label Claim (LC), the probability of passing the 
three-batch PPQ is about 2%, whereas the probability of a batch passing the release test with 6% variability is 
approximately 97%.
5.2.3	
Sampling Plans/Statistically-Based Criteria for Critical Quality Attributes
Statistical techniques that have been either suggested in the literature or used in practice when conducting validation 
studies include computer generated acceptance tables, simulations used to estimate the probability of passing 
complex multiple-stage tests such as CU and dissolution, tolerance intervals, and variable acceptance sampling. 
These techniques are discussed below or in Section 6.3.
The example provided in Figure 5.4 focuses on intra-batch (or within batch) criteria. The acceptance criteria apply to 
a given PPQ batch and indicate whether that batch has met the PPQ acceptance criteria.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 65 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 63
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
An acceptance criterion needs to be met for each batch in the PPQ study for a successful PPQ outcome. One 
advantage of these criteria is that a failure to meet the acceptance criteria clearly defines the batch or batches that 
are causing the PPQ failure. This can help direct the focus of a subsequent investigation. While it is still possible that 
the root cause of the failure applies to all batches in the study even if some batches pass the intra-batch criteria, the 
failure of particular batches to meet batch-specific acceptance criteria helps focus the investigation on the most likely 
root causes.
Intra-batch plans can be used on many different parameters where intra-batch variation is important to assess. Blend/
content uniformity and protein concentration are discussed in the remainder of this section.
Blend Uniformity and Content Uniformity
Intra-batch variability is typically assessed during PPQ at blending for powders, and after sieving and transfer to a 
bin(s) for suspension-coated pellets as well as at forming tablets and filling of capsules. Blend uniformity with respect 
to a target drug load, mg active/g product and dosage units (% LC), is a common attribute for the assessment.
Intra-batch variability is typically assessed during PPQ at forming or filling for tablets and capsules, respectively. The 
quality attribute that is used for the assessment is the drug CU throughout the batch in terms of percent of LC on a 
unit dose basis.
This is typically the most extensive within batch evaluation during PPQ and provides the most insight into potential 
changes or trends during the manufacture of the batch because of the sample ordering.
Additionally, because of the extensive sampling and evaluation of CU, further within batch assessment of other 
analytical properties such as individual impurities and total impurities is not required or may be limited, for example, to 
performing beginning, middle, and end of batch sampling and testing.
In August 2013, the FDA withdrew its draft Guidance for Industry: Powder Blends and Finished Dosage Units – 
Stratified In-Process Dosage Unit Sampling and Assessment [45]. In the reasons for making this withdrawal, the FDA 
stated that acceptance criteria based on USP <905> [46] do not have sufficient statistical assurance or confidence 
that batches of drug products have met appropriate specifications and statistical quality control. Further, the FDA and 
USP have stated that the USP <905> test is not a release test but rather a surveillance test [47, 48].
The USP Uniformity of Dosage Units (UDU) <905> test is described in Table 5.2 (assumes symmetric potency 
limits about 100% LC). The test consists of two stages with criteria within each stage. If Stage 1 criteria are not met 
based on 10 dosage units, 20 additional dosage units are tested and compared to the Stage 2 criteria. There is an 
“indifference” zone, meaning that if X̅  falls between 98.5 and 101.5% LC, then the first term in the acceptance value is 
0 since M = X̅  [46].
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Table 5.2: USP Uniformity of Dosage Units Test [46]

recommendations to companies on the assessment of blend and content uniformity [49]. Figure 5.6 is a flow chart 
that shows the Technical Team’s recommendations for assessment of blend and content uniformity for process design 
and qualification batches [50].
All measurements of dosage units and criteria values are in percentage label claim (% LC). At each stage 
calculate the sample average X̅  and the sample standard deviation s.

Figure 5.6: Process Flow Diagram for Assessment of Blend and Content Uniformity for Process Design and 
Process Qualification Batches [50]
The flow chart does not provide or recommend specific statistical methods for CU other than that the method should 
provide assurance of compliance to USP <905> [46].
The ISPE Blend Uniformity and Content Uniformity Technical Team flow chart provides a framework for performing 
the CU assessment [50]. Key aspects of the framework are:
•
A moderately large number of locations should be sampled in a systematic/stratified approach across the batch
(e.g., 40 locations with 3 samples per location, 40 × 3).
•
Two tiers of evaluation: specifically, from across the batch test half of the locations and dosage units (e.g.,
20 × 3) against pre-selected criteria. If the criteria are met, the batch passes; if not, test the remaining dosage
units (that is, the remaining 20 × 3) and compare against the acceptance criteria (using all dosage unit data, e.g.,
40 × 3). This is a two-tiered approach.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 68 =====
Page 66	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
The fixed choice for the number of locations and therefore total samples may not appropriate for all processes. 
For example, very short runs cannot be reasonably sampled in this manner. A company may need to link, with 
justification, the number of locations and total sample size to process risks and duration of the filling run. For blend 
uniformity or CU, a company could decide to test all samples in the same run instead of using a two-tiered approach 
to gain a better understanding of the variation.
The pre-selected criteria should provide a heightened level of assurance that the batch will meet the regulatory 
requirements (USP <905> [46], Ph. Eur. 2.9.40 [51], JP 6.02 [52]) for any additional samples obtained from the batch 
and tested against those requirements. The level of assurance/conservativeness that the sampling plan/test provides 
needs to be justified by the supplier.
Protein Concentration
Protein concentration is a key CQA for a biologic that measures the quantity of protein in the product. Regulatory 
authorities typically expect a specification of ± 10% of the target concentration.
One possible intra-batch PPQ sampling plan for protein concentration is to use a prediction interval for a single new 
protein concentration value as a preliminary acceptance criterion where several samples are taken either randomly or 
systematically as the lot is produced. A prediction interval is an estimated range in which a future observation will fall 
with a specified probability. The evidence of capability sought for PPQ for this example is for the prediction interval to 
be within or equal to the specification for protein concentration. Caution: the cause of any statistical interval extending 
beyond specification may be statistical in nature, such as inadequate sample size, an outlier, etc. Thus, this situation 
should not necessarily trigger PPQ failure, as the process may in fact be quite capable. Instead of a strict failure 
criterion, the PPQ protocol should allow a risk-based assessment of results for intervals beyond specification to 
determine the path forward, with guidance from a process subject matter expert and a statistician when possible.
To develop a plan using a prediction interval, two elements must be decided:
1.
Number of values used to calculate the interval
2.
Confidence level for the interval
The equation for a (1-α)% prediction interval is the following:
_____
X̅  ± (t1-α / 2,n-1) × (s) × (√1+1/n)
Where:
	
X̅  = sample mean
	
n = sample size per batch
t1-α / 2,n-1 = two-sided Student t value with 100 × (1-α)% confidence and n-1 degrees of freedom
	
s = sample standard deviation
The confidence level and the sample size may be selected by evaluating a range of confidence levels and sample 
sizes to achieve a goal as described in Section 5.2. Assuming that this evaluation indicates that a 90% prediction 
interval with n = 10 protein concentration values for the PPQ batch is sufficient, the interval becomes:
_______
X̅  ± (1.833) × (s) × (√1 + 1/10) = X̅  ± (1.923) × (s) 
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Figure 5.7 shows the OC curve for this plan in comparison to the release test of a single protein concentration 
result within or equal to the specification (± 10% of the target concentration) when the process mean is at the target 
concentration. Also shown in Figure 5.7 are the OC curves for a 95% and 99% prediction interval. The protein 
concentration data is expressed as a percent of the target concentration for the graph.
When the 90% prediction interval is within or equal to the specification range, it is possible to state with at least a 
90% confidence level that a future result will be within the specification. The OC curve comparison between the 
release test and the PPQ test (as described in this section) can also be used to describe the assurance the PPQ plan 
provides.
Figure 5.7: Operating Characteristic Curves for Release and Prediction Interval Process Performance 
Qualification Plans for Protein Concentration
Composite Assay
Composite assay is a measurement of the amount of drug substance in a product batch expressed as a percent of 
LC (% LC). The analytical method usually has a sample preparation that involves compositing units (typically in the 
range of 5 – 20 units) prior to measurement. Regulatory expectations for the specification typically are 90.0 – 110.0% 
LC or 95.0 – 105.0% LC.
One possible sampling plan is to use the prediction interval approach provided in the protein concentration example. 
When the specification is 90.0 – 110.0% LC, the OC curves are the same as shown in Figure 5.7.
5.3	
Process Validation (Process Performance Qualification) Acceptance Criteria
Acceptance criteria are developed to provide statistical approaches to sample and to evaluate dosage forms sampled 
during manufacture of a batch that would ensure, with high confidence, that the batch would pass the USP UDU 
<905> [46] and possibly USP Dissolution <711> tests [53].
All of the statistical methods in this section and Section 6.3 require the user to choose a confidence level and a 
probability of passing the USP test. Based on the sampling plan, confidence level, and probability of passing the USP 
test, an acceptance limit table is generated.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 70 =====
Page 68	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
If the acceptance limit, based on the sample results, is met, then the conclusion is that with the desired confidence, 
the probability of passing the USP test is at least the desired probability. For example, if the confidence level is 
90% and the probability of passing the test is 95% and the sample passes the acceptance criteria, then with 90% 
confidence, there is at least a 95% probability of passing the USP test.
Recall from the sampling discussion (Section 5.2.1) that Sampling Plan 1 tests one unit from each sampling location 
in the batch. Sampling Plan 2 tests more than one unit from each sampling location. Sampling Plan 2 is the plan 
shown in the Figure 5.6 flow chart during filling or compression whereas Sampling Plan 1 is generally used for 
routine release. Sampling Plan 2 allows estimation of both between and within location variability and their relative 
contribution to the total variability.
There are three statistical methods:
•
ASTM E2709/E2810 method proposed by the ISPE Blend Uniformity and Content Uniformity Technical Team [44]
•
Tolerance Intervals
•
An “Exact” test based on variable sampling (or its acceptance polygon approximation) described in Section 6.3
for routine release
The ASTM E2709/E2810 [44] and tolerance interval methods have been developed for PPQ when using Sampling 
Plan 2 and are compared in this section.
The “Exact” test may also be developed for PPQ. In situations during PPQ where the units cannot be kept in 
chronological order, Sampling Plan 1 may be appropriate. An example is when the active ingredient is contained 
in the coating. In this case, the dosage units are continually mixed so that the sampling strategy would be a simple 
random sample with one dosage unit per location.
Acceptance limits using Sampling Plan 2 require more calculations since there is a between and within location 
variance component. The acceptance limit table used for Sampling Plan 1 only requires the user to compute the 
sample mean and standard deviation whereas Sampling Plan 2 requires a computation of the standard deviation of 
the location means, the within location standard deviation, and the overall sample mean.
For ASTM E2709/E2810 [44], the pictorial (Figure 6.5) in Section 6.3 also applies when using Sampling Plan 2. 
The only difference is that the standard deviation and the upper limit on the standard deviation (ULS) are computed 
differently due to the two variance components (i.e., between and within location).
The ISPE website [49] contains useful information for ASTM E2709/E2810 that includes:
•
Blend Uniformity and Content Uniformity Group flow charts for PPQ and routine release
•
Common acceptance tables for Sampling Plans 1 and 2
•
Excel spreadsheets that perform all the computations for the Sampling Plan 2 that tests 3 dosage units at each
of 20 or 40 locations
•
Validated SAS program that constructs acceptance limit tables for CU and dissolution for Sampling Plans 1 and 2
(supporting validation documentation included)
The Two-Sided Tolerance Interval approach is based on the coverage discussed in Section 6.3, which states if 
99.34% of CU results fall between 83.5% and 116.5% LC, then there is a 95% probability of passing the USP UDU 
test [46]. The Two-Sided Tolerance Interval for two variance components has been published [54]. The formula for 
calculating the tolerance interval endpoints is given in Appendix 6 Section 14.10. As with Sampling Plan 1 in Section 
6.3, if the endpoints of the tolerance interval are contained within 83.5% to 116.5% LC, then there is at least a 95% 
probability that future samples from the batch will pass the USP UDU test [46].
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 71 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 69
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
The Two-Sided Tolerance Interval method can be compared to ASTM E2709/E2810 [44] using OC curves. Figures 
5.8 and 5.9 show the probability of passing each method when the lot mean is 100% and 96% LC for Sampling Plan 
2 with three test results from each of 20 (20 × 3) or 40 (40 × 3) locations
The probability of passing ASTM E2709/E2810 [44] or the Two-Sided Tolerance Interval method depends on how 
much of the proportion of the total variation is due to location to location variability.
If there were no location variation, then the probability of passing would be similar to Sampling Plan 1 with 60 (20 × 3) 
or 120 (40 × 3) locations, but if almost all of the total variability is due to location, then the probability of passing would 
be closer to Sampling Plan 1 with 20 or 40 locations.
OC curves are shown for the situations where 0%, 50%, and 90% of the total variation is due to location variability. As 
can be seen from the plots (Figures 5.8 and 5.9), the ASTM E2709/E2810-based method [56] has a higher probability 
of passing compared to the Two-Sided Tolerance Interval method when the lot mean is 100% LC. When the lot mean 
is 96% LC, ASTM2709/E2810 has a higher probability of passing up to a total standard deviation of about 3 – 4%, but 
the Two-Sided Tolerance Interval is higher for total standard deviations greater than 3 – 4%.
Figure 5.8: Operating Characteristic Curve Comparison of ASTM E2709/E2810 [44] to Two-Sided Tolerance 
Interval (Lot Mean = 100% Label Claim)

simplicity, the locations have just been listed as 1 through 20).

Figure 5.10 is a plot of the data that can be useful to look for trends or unusual patterns. Plotting the data is highly 
recommended.
Figure 5.10: Data Display Content Uniformity versus Location
A VCA should be used as part of the data review. Variance components split the overall variability into between and 
within location components. The VCA is useful to determine the relative magnitudes of within and between location 
variability.
In the example, the variance components are given in Table 5.4.
Table 5.4: Variance Components
The analysis shows that the total standard deviation for all results is 2.557, with 30.6% of the total variation due to 
between locations, which is statistically significant (p-value = 0.012 for null hypothesis that the variance for between 
locations is equal to zero).
The variance component analysis is useful to determine whether or not the location to location (between location) 
variability is significant, both statistically and practically, as well as how much of the total variation is due to location 
to location variability. A company would have to decide if a total standard deviation of 2.557% with about 30% of the 
variation due to locations is worth the resources to investigate the between location variability.
Note that the within location standard deviation is 2.13%, so reducing or eliminating the location to location variability 
may only result in reducing the total standard deviation from 2.557% to about 2.13%.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
