Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
It is also important to note that the tests and acceptance criteria proposed assume that all dosage units in a given 
batch have an equal probability of reaching a given consumer. One way to look at this is that all dosage units are 
blended together such than any location differences are minimized prior to final packaging.
There are two approaches to analyzing the data: using the ASTM E2709/E2810 method [44] or using a Two-Sided 
Tolerance Interval.
1.	
ASTM E2709/E2810 [44]
	
The statistics required to use the ASTM E2709/E2810 acceptance limit table for Sampling Plan 2 are:
•	
Overall mean
•	
Within location standard deviation (i.e., the square root of the average of the 20 location variances, which is 
the same as the within location standard deviation from the variance component analysis)
•	
Standard deviation of the 20 location means (Note: this is not the square root of the between location 
variance component)
Table 5.5 shows the overall mean, the within location standard deviation (SE), and the between location standard 
deviation.
Table 5.5: Example – Sampling Plan 2
Table 5.6 is a subset of the entire acceptance table for Sampling Plan 2 that contains limits for the overall mean. 
Acceptance criteria tables have been posted for the ASTM E2709/E2810 approach including the complete 20 × 3 
table [55].
The first column is the within location standard deviation (SE) and the first row is the standard deviation of the 
location means. To use the acceptance limit table, the SE and Standard Deviation of Location Means are rounded up 
to the nearest tenth from 2.13 to 2.2 and 1.87 to 1.9, respectively to be conservative. The limits on the overall mean 
are 92.8 to 107.2% LC.
Since the overall mean is 98.59% LC, the sample passes the limits; therefore, with 90% confidence, there is at least a 
95% chance that samples from the batch will pass the USP UDU test [46].

Stage 2.2 Process Review
5.4.1	
Graphical Tools
After Stage 2 PPQ execution is complete, graphical tools such as run charts, individual value plots, and (in some 
cases) box plots can be used to confirm that the process performance is similar to that expected based on Stage 1 
data. As the number of PPQ batches will typically be small, a visual assessment is usually the most appropriate way 
to assess inter-batch variability in Stage 2.
In the case of an in-line product, historical data can also be used. Figure 5.11 shows a control chart for the average 
of the UDU data with the historical, Stage 1, and Stage 2 data combined. In this case, the historical data is used 
to set the control limits, and the Stage 1 and Stage 2 results fall within the control limits, demonstrating consistent 
performance after the process change (scale change, etc.).
Figure 5.12 shows side-by-side boxplots of the UDU averages. Here it is clear that the data from all three sets 
(historical, Development/Stage 1, and PPQ/Stage 2.2) overlap.

Validation Prior to Submission Approval
In cases where validation data must be submitted with the registration application, it is possible that approved 
specifications will be more restrictive than the requested limits. It is also possible that the granted expiration dating 
may be shorter than requested. In these cases, it is important to revisit validation results prior to releasing product.

When specifications are tightened, results need to be compared to the new limits, reduced for analytical variation and 
degradation on stability. Note: when the approved specifications are tighter than those in the Marketing Authorization 
Application, impact on CPP and PAR/NOR also need to be assessed.
Note: be careful about the relaxing of release limits. Expiry might have been shortened, but the expanded release 
limits do not give assurance that the shelf life first requested in the application can be met.

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
6	 Continued Process Verification (Stage 3)
A successful PPQ (Stage 2) confirms the soundness of the process design and control strategy (Stage 1), and 
demonstrates that the commercial manufacturing process performs as expected and is capable of consistently 
delivering quality product.
The main purpose of the CPV (Stage 3) monitoring plan is to provide assurance that throughout the commercial 
phase of the lifecycle, the process remains in a state of control; however, a period of enhanced monitoring after 
completion of PPQ may be required for certain attributes or parameters to further increase the level of confidence in 
the process (see Figure 6.1).
Figure 6.1: Continued Process Verification Strategy
It is important to keep in mind that this stage is known by different names depending on the regulatory body in force. 
FDA and WHO call it Continued Process Verification [3, 26], while EMA uses Ongoing Process Verification [11]. 
Regardless of the terminology, the intent of the regulators is the same: manufacturers need to monitor the state of 
process control, verify the impact of variability, identify potential issues, and determine the best course of action to 
ensure that the process remains in control.
6.1	
Determine the Continued Process Verification Plan
A CPV monitoring plan should be established to evaluate process capability and the ongoing impact of variability in 
process, materials, facility, equipment, or other inputs, continually increasing process understanding and generating 
variability estimates. These estimates can be used to establish long-term Statistical Process Control (SPC) limits and 
to adjust the levels and frequency of routine sampling and monitoring, as appropriate.
Control chart signals (e.g., statistical outliers, western electric rule violations), capability indices, and input-output 
relationships identified from continued process monitoring may trigger an investigation and the need for revisions to 
the control strategy, such as:
•
Adjustments to extended testing levels and/or elimination/addition of monitoring parameters
•
Revisions to the raw material sampling strategy
•
Other process improvements, for instance optimization of analytical methods, process control, equipment,
environmental, etc., that have been found to contribute to process variability
One approach to selecting parameters/attributes for the CPV plan and the overall process is summarized in Figure 6.2.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 80 =====
Page 78	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Figure 6.2: Flow Chart for Establishing a Stage 3 Monitoring Plan [35]
For all new products, it is recommended establishing a monitoring plan as early as possible to enhance process 
understanding. This may mean monitoring during manufacture of Stage 1 batches such as clinical supplies, 
engineering batches, and stability batch manufacture.
Minimally, a monitoring plan should be available following Stage 2 if full PPQ-level sampling and testing will not be 
continued.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 81 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 79
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
The approach to analyzing and reviewing product and process performance data should be documented by 
procedure or protocol. The plan should be developed by SMEs (such as technical personnel, statisticians) and 
approved by the appropriate functional groups (QA/QC, Validation, Manufacturing, etc.). The monitoring plan should 
address, directly or by reference:
•
Which input and output parameters and/or attributes will be monitored for each product
•
How data will be collected
•
Methodology that will be used to evaluate the data (e.g., statistical methods such as control charts, multivariate
analysis)
•
At what frequency the data will be evaluated
The plan could be product specific, covering all unit operations (raw materials, equipment settings, environmental 
conditions, and so forth) or products may be grouped (e.g., platform technology approach).
Other elements to include in the monitoring plan or other associated documents (SOPs, VMP, etc.) are the roles and 
responsibilities of various functions, management reviews, and mechanisms to trigger changes requiring redesign 
and re-qualification.
The plan should be incorporated into the quality system at the manufacturing site to leverage the existing data 
collection and evaluation processes, including the annual/periodic review process. If the monitoring plan results are 
reviewed as part of commercial product release, approval of the plan by the quality unit (QA) at the manufacturing 
site is required by the EU [11], and recommended in other markets.
6.2	
Determine which Variability to Include in the Continued Process Verification Plan
Data to be monitored is determined by the current understanding of the manufacturing process and systems 
(process, materials, components, equipment, facility, utilities, and environment) in relation to product quality 
attributes. This understanding may range anywhere from first principles, prior knowledge, and statistical models 
based on DoEs to empirical models, or even to understanding based on manufacturing experience.
Risk-based tools may be used for the selection of initial Stage 3 elements. The risk of an input (equipment parameter, 
raw material attribute, etc.) to impact the process may be ranked as well as the risk of the attribute (output) to impact 
the patient. For instance, the tablet appearance impact is a lower risk to the patient than tablet potency/stability. 
Risk scores may then be generated to prioritize the elements that are potentially most impactful to the process and 
ultimately the patient.
During Stage 3, estimates of long-term process variability are generated. Adjustments to the control strategy may 
be warranted as process experience and understanding of variability increases, including potential changes in the 
assignment of in-process/raw material attributes or a parameter criticality level. The criticality of finished product 
CQAs would only change based on new clinical data.
Initial Stage 3 monitoring plans, particularly for new products, may involve a greater number of parameters and/
or heightened sampling and testing for certain parameters than during ongoing monitoring. Some CQAs (e.g., viral 
clearance for a biological product) may be confirmed through separate validation studies and not as part of the 
release of each batch; therefore, they may not need to be included in the Stage 3 plan.
The number of parameters and/or extent of testing may increase or decrease as further knowledge is gained from the 
monitoring experience, as well as when specific residual risks are reduced or new risks are identified.
The decision to discontinue heightened/extended testing should be driven by the confidence achieved in the 
manufacturing process during Stages 1 and 2, and throughout Stage 3, based on the robustness of the control 
strategy and its ability to manage sources of variation.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 82 =====
Page 80	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
To guide this process and define when PPQ-level sampling and testing may be reduced, decision trees may be used. 
They may be based on meeting various statistical criteria (e.g., prediction interval, tolerance interval, defect rate, or 
other statistical criteria met with a defined confidence) combined with an overall assessment of the process and the 
associated control strategy.
An example of a decision tree is provided in Figure 6.3. The rationale for the change or discontinuance of the 
monitoring should be documented and implemented through validation documentation, which may include change 
management systems.
Figure 6.3: Example Decision Tree for Moving from Process Performance Qualification to Stage 3 Sampling/
Testing
6.2.1	
New Products [35]
For new products, the control strategy identified during development (Stage 1, Process Design) is based on process 
understanding and QRM, and provides the foundation for Stage 3 monitoring.
The parameters and attributes included in the enhanced sampling and testing in Stage 2 should be evaluated initially 
for continued monitoring in Stage 3. Consider both intra and inter-batch variability when establishing the monitoring 
plan.
Intra-batch variability is typically assessed during PPQ. If it has already been demonstrated that a smaller number 
of samples/values/data points represents the batch (that is, intra-batch variability data demonstrates that worst-
case locations and time points are not significantly different than other points/locations, low defect rates have been 
demonstrated by the process with statistical rigor, etc.), then testing can be reduced to routine levels; however, this 
routine level should be scientifically and statistically justified.
Where it is not reasonable or practical to assess variability of input ranges of material quality attributes that may 
impact intra-batch or inter-batch variability during Stage 2, or if product quality attributes values are approaching the 
edge of an acceptance limit, then it may be prudent to continue to perform Stage 2 levels of sampling and testing 
during some portions of Stage 3 for those potentially impacted attributes.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 83 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 81
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
With the appropriate risk-based analysis and documented justification, certain Stage 2 parameters may be eliminated 
or the level of sampling and testing could be reduced in the Stage 3 plan. An example of such a decision process is 
presented in Appendix 3 (Section 11.1).
One of the goals of initial Stage 3 monitoring for a new product is to finish establishing the capability of the process 
that began during Stage 1 and continued in Stage 2. That is, Stage 3 looks at process performance across many 
batches and continues to build the overall picture of inter-batch variability.
Where there is concern that the established control strategy may allow variability of the inputs that may impact output 
product quality, more batches may be needed to assess capability, and/or interim alert limits considered based on the 
current process understanding.
That is, if the input variability evaluated to date is limited compared to the input specification limits, more data should 
be complied to assess the impact of variability closer to the specification limits. Alternatively, statistically-based alert/
control limits could be established to facilitate evaluation of atypical variation in the input.
It should also be noted that a batch may yield one or more data points. In these cases, process capability analysis can 
be performed with groups of samples (e.g., six samples for each batch), taking into account the intra-batch variability.
Additionally, process capability may be calculated using batches from all three stages of PV. A statistician should 
be consulted when combining batches across stages for capability analysis to ensure that proper data pooling 
techniques are applied.
A criticality/risk assessment determines the level of impact each process variable or material attribute has on the 
CQAs; that is, where each variable falls in the continuum of criticality.
This criticality assessment typically was performed before the start of Stage 2 and may be revised on completion of 
Stage 2 based on increased process understanding. Cause and effect or correlations between inputs and outputs 
may or may not have been established.
Even after Stage 2, however, the impact of specific inputs (raw material properties and process parameters) may 
have not been fully assessed. Where the variability has not been fully determined, one of the objectives of the CPV 
plan will be to evaluate potential sources of variability for their impact on the CQAs or process performance and 
confirm or establish the inputs versus CQAs correlations.
This understanding may be achieved through the use of various statistical tools (e.g., multivariate analysis, principle 
component analysis, partial least squares, box plot/control charts) of the process database at defined points in time or 
observations of performance shifts associated with process changes (in inputs, outputs, or both).
6.2.2	
Existing/Legacy Products [35]
Existing/legacy products developed traditionally (not by QbD) may not have critical attributes or parameters defined in 
their submissions; therefore, a criticality/risk assessment should be performed to identify those attributes and process 
parameters (including raw and in-process material attributes and manufacturing systems) that may impact patient 
safety, efficacy, and product quality.
The assessment should be based on current process understanding and relevant experience of the commercial 
process, including quality system events such as deviations and change controls, as well as product performance 
on stability. An example of such an evaluation is presented the ISPE Discussion Paper: “Topic 2 - Stage 3 Process 
Validation: Applying Continued Process Verification Expectations,” Example 3 [35].
Stage 3 for existing/legacy products should begin with routine monitoring of the defined critical attributes and 
parameters. The initial evaluation may be based on a retrospective review of existing process performance data 
(i.e., from batches made before the decision to formally initiate Stage 3, APR). As with new products, when data is 
collected and analyzed, additional aspects that require evaluation may be identified.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 84 =====
Page 82	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
One suggested action is to monitor additional parameters across batches or implement an intensive sampling plan 
of an output within a batch until the desired level of process understanding is achieved. Monitoring could provide the 
data to develop the correlations and cause and effect relationships, and help identify which inputs need better control 
to reduce output variability.
6.3	
Determining Statistically-based Routine Sampling and Testing for Continued 
Process Verification
When selecting sampling plans and acceptance criteria for CPV, or blend uniformity and CU, one approach is to 
evaluate the OC curves for Stage 2, Stage 3.2, and the regulatory UDU test requirement [46]. A discussion of OC 
curves of sampling plans and acceptance criteria is given in the Appendix 6 Section 14.1
6.3.1	
Example of Statistically-based Criteria: Content Uniformity
The purpose of statistically-based routine release sampling and testing is to provide a high level of assurance 
that dosage unit uniformity is high throughout the batch meeting patient needs and regulatory requirements and 
expectations.
The primary regulatory authority driving the use of statistically-based routine release criteria is the FDA [3]. However, 
given the harmonized UDU test requirements across the three ICH regions [56], the criteria established to meet 
FDA expectations may be applied to the other regions. Therefore, this section addresses the use of statistically-
based routine release sampling and acceptance criteria applicable to the UDU tests USP <905> [46], European 
Pharmacopoeia 2.9.40 [51], and Japanese Pharmacopoeia 6.02 [52]. (Throughout the rest of this section, the use of 
“USP <905>’ represents all three UDU tests.)
The USP UDU test [46] is a tiered test since it is comprised of two stages. The first stage consists of testing 10 
dosage units. If the Stage 1 criteria are met, the sample passes the USP UDU test. If the Stage 1 criteria are not met, 
then another set of 20 dosage units are tested and the total set of 30 dosage unit CU results are compared to the 
Stage 2 criteria. If the Stage 2 criteria are met, then the sample passes the USP UDU test. Otherwise, the sample 
fails.
The same strategy can be used to show that with high confidence, a sample from the lot will pass the USP UDU test 
[46]. The strategy was published by the ISPE Blend Uniformity and Content Uniformity Technical Team [50].
An additional benefit of mimicking the sampling of USP <905> is that the results can also be analyzed against USP 
requirements, that is, the USP <905> AV value can be calculated [46]. While the statistical criteria established for 
CPV are tighter than USP <905> criteria (and therefore meeting these criteria logically provides the assurance 
to claim that the batch meets USP <905>), having the AV calculation available could be of value internally to the 
manufacturer and upon regulatory inspection while industry and FDA transition to applying statistically-based routine 
release criteria that demonstrate compliance with, and heightened assurance to meet, USP <905>.
A flow chart showing the ISPE Blend Uniformity and Content Uniformity Technical Team’s [50] recommended two-
tiered approach is given in Figure 6.4, where at least 30 dosage units are sampled equally spaced throughout the 
tableting (or encapsulation) run. Then at least 10 of the 30 dosage units are tested in tier 1 against the desired 
statistical methodology criteria. If the tier 1 set of samples does not pass, then the remaining dosage units are tested 
and must meet the desired statistical methodology criteria for tier 2.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 85 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 83
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
Figure 6.4: Process Flow Diagram for Assessment of Blend and Content Uniformity for Continued Process 
Verification (Stage 3.2) Batches [50]
6.3.2	
Statistically-based Acceptance Criteria
Note that the ISPE Blend Uniformity and Content Uniformity Technical Team’s [50] recommendation in the previous 
section uses Sampling Plan 1 (one dosage unit tested from each of n locations) that is often used for routine release. 
The recommendation does not provide a specific statistical methodology.
An approach using ASTM E2709/E2810 standards was published by ISPE Blend Uniformity and Content Uniformity 
Technical Team [44]. There are several methods that can be used to evaluate CU data collected using Sampling Plan 1:
•
ASTM E2709/E2810 standards [44] (See 5.3 for description)
•
Two-Sided Tolerance Interval method that captures a specific proportion of the distribution
•
“Exact” acceptance region corresponding to a variable acceptance sampling plan that assures results will fall
within a specified interval (83.5% to 116.5%)
Another published method uses a Bayesian approach [57].
The following subsections provide more details on the Two-Sided Tolerance Interval and “Exact” methods. All of the 
methods provide an upper limit on the standard deviation for a given sample mean such that if the sample standard 
deviation is less than the upper limit, given the desired level of confidence, then the lot will have at least the desired 
probability of passing the USP UDU test [46].
For example, for a given sample mean, if the sample standard deviation is less than the upper limit and the sample 
passes, then with 90% confidence, there is at least a 95% probability that the lot would pass the USP UDU test [46].
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 86 =====
Page 84	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
When criteria are established and justified that demonstrate a high degree of confidence in meeting the USP <905> 
uniformity test [46], then from a statistical perspective, a company may consider that the USP UDU test need not be 
performed. Nevertheless, if a manufacturer intends to suspend the USP <905> test, this will need to be evaluated for 
compliance against their specific product registration.
6.3.2.1	 ASTM E2709/E2810 Method
ASTM E2709 [58] provides a general methodology to assure compliance to a regulatory standard. E2810 [59] applies 
this methodology specifically to CU.
Assuming Normally distributed data, the lower bound on the probability of passing the UDU test for a specific “true” lot 
mean and standard deviation is calculated [60]. Based on the CU results generated using any of the three sampling 
plans above (Section 6.3.2), a confidence region with a specified confidence level is generated for the “true” lot 
means and standard deviations.
For each possible “true” combination of the lot mean and standard deviation in the confidence region, the lower 
bound can be calculated. With the specified confidence level, the probability of passing the USP UDU test [46] is 
greater than the minimum of all possible lower bound values in the confidence interval.
Figure 6.5 provides a pictorial of the ASTM method [44]. The x-axis and y-axis are the “true” lot mean and standard 
deviation, respectively. The solid line is the contour line where the lower bound for passing the USP UDU [46] test 
is at least 95%. Any combination of batch means and standard deviations below this curve has greater than a 95% 
chance of passing the USP UDU test. This is called the acceptable parameter region. A 50% contour would lie above 
the 95% contour and a 99% contour would lie below the 95% contour.
Based on statistics from a random sample or a systematic sample, the mean, X̅ , and standard deviation, s, are 
calculated and are shown in Figure 6.5. Based on the sample mean and standard deviation, a joint confidence 
interval [61] is generated for the lot mean and standard deviation that is represented by the triangle (Z is a standard 
normal critical value and ULS is the upper confidence limit on the standard deviation).
Therefore, if the confidence interval at the desired confidence level is completely contained under the solid line, then 
with the desired confidence (90% in this figure), there is at least a 95% chance that the batch will pass the USP UDU 
test [46].
The point in the confidence interval with the lowest probability of passing the USP UDU test [46] is always at one 
of the vertices at the top of the triangle. The two dashed lines are the combination of sample means and standard 
deviations for n = 10 and n = 100 such that when the confidence interval is generated, one of the upper vertices falls 
on the solid 95% line. The set of all sample means and standard deviations for a given sample size lying below the 
dashed line forms the acceptance region.
As can be seen in the figure, the larger the sample size, the larger the acceptable standard deviation for a given 
sample mean. Any combination of sample mean and standard deviation in the acceptance region generates a 
confidence region at or below in the parameter region. So, with the desired confidence level, there is at least a 95% 
probability that the lot will pass the USP UDU test [46].
The points on the dashed line are the values that can be used to create an acceptance limit table.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

www.astm.org.
ASTM E2709/E2810 [44] can be applied to both Sampling Plan 1 (one result per location) and Sampling Plan 2 (more 
than one result per location). Sampling Plan 1 is more common for routine release whereas Sampling Plan 2 is often 
used for PV.
When one CU result is tested at each location, the standard deviation estimates the sum of the between and within 
location variability since it is not possible to estimate each variance separately. However, in Sampling Plan 2, the 
between and within location variability can be separated using VCA. Therefore, the standard deviation (s) and ULS in 
Figure 6.5 are calculated differently. (See reference [60] for more details.)
6.3.2.2	 Tolerance Interval Approaches
Prior to discussing tolerance interval approaches, it is necessary to show the operating OC curve for passing the USP 
UDU test [46]. The USP UDU test allows the sample mean to fall as far from 100% LC as 83.5% to 116.5% LC.
If the coverage is defined as the probability of a result falling between 83.5% and 116.5% LC, then the USP UDU test 
[46] OC curves are as shown in Figure 6.6. A dashed reference line is shown at the 95% probability of passing the USP 
UDU test. The OC curves are based on simulation. The vertical axis is the probability of passing the USP UDU test 
and the horizontal axis is the coverage that is the probability that a single result will fall between 83.5% – 116.5% LC. A 
separate curve is shown for “true” lot means from 90% to 100% LC. The OC curves for 104% and 102% are the same 
as 96% and 98%, respectively (i.e., the OC curves are the same for lot means the same distance from 100% LC).

Table 6.1: Coverage (% Individuals between 83.5% and 116.5% Label Claim) to Achieve 95% Probability of 
Passing USP Uniformity of Dosage Units Test [46]
Note that as the lot mean decreases from 100%, the coverage required to the pass the USP UDU test [46] only very 
slightly increases from 0.9924 at a lot mean of 100% LC to 0.9934 for lot means of 95% LC to 84% LC. Therefore, 
if the coverage is at least 99.34%, then there is at least a 95% probability of passing the USP UDU test for all lot 
means.
The 99.34% serves one statistically valid quality standard where any methodology that can assure coverage greater 
than 99.34% would provide at least a 95% probability of passing the USP UDU test [46]. Tolerance intervals is 
a method that can satisfy this requirement since it provides an interval that captures a desired proportion of the 
distribution (coverage) with a specified confidence level. If at least 99.34% of the individual results (99.34% coverage 
is used throughout the rest of this document) are contained within 83.5% to 116.5% LC, then there is at least a 95% 
probability of passing the USP UDU test.
Since capturing 99.34% of the distribution requires estimation of percentiles in the tails of a Normal distribution, 
normality of the data is an important consideration and should be evaluated. The tolerance interval methods 
discussed in this Guide assume that the data was collected using a sampling plan that tests one dosage unit per 
each sampling location. This could be a random or systematic sample (i.e., samples taken from approximately equal 
locations throughout the lot based on time or lot size) taken from the lot.
The next two subsections describe two tolerance interval approaches: a Two-Sided Tolerance Interval and an “Exact” 
method.
Two-Sided Tolerance Interval
Given the sample size, confidence level, and coverage, a Two-Sided Tolerance Interval is constructed as follows:
	
X̅  ± k × s
Where:
X̅  = sample mean
s = sample standard deviation
k = depends on the sample size, confidence level, and coverage
Table 6.2 shows the k values for confidence levels of 50%, 90%, and 95% to pass USP UDU [46] with 95% 
probability.
Lot Mean
(% Label Claim)
Proportion of Individuals Between 
83.5% and 116.5% Label Claim

0.9934

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Table 6.2: k Values for Two-Sided Tolerance Interval with Coverage = 99.34%
A very accurate approximation of k is given in the Appendix 6 (Section 14.2).
To meet the goal of having at least 99.34% of the individuals falling within 83.5% to 116.5% LC, a Two-Sided 
Tolerance Interval can be calculated using the desired confidence level and coverage of 99.34%. If the interval is 
completely contained within 83.5% to 116.5% LC, then with the desired confidence, there is at least a 95% probability 
of passing the USP UDU test [46].
As an example, let n = 30, sample mean = 98.60, and sample standard deviation = 3.42. The Two-Sided Tolerance 
Interval with 90% confidence is 98.60 ± 3.34450 × 3.42 = 87.16 to 110.04; which is completely contained in 83.5 – 
116.5, so the sample passes the acceptance criteria.
An acceptance limit table can be constructed by finding the maximum value of the standard deviation for a given 
sample mean such that either the lower endpoint is 83.5% or the upper endpoint is 116.5%. Plots of the tolerance 
interval acceptance limit tables are given in the Appendix 6 (Section 14.3).
A tolerance interval paper [62] was published that uses the interval from 85% to 115% instead of 83.5% to 116.5%. 
However, using 85% to 115% has a significant effect on the OC curves shown in Section 6.2 and 6.3 that gives the 
impression that the probability of passing the USP UDU test [46] increases as the lot mean gets further away from 
100% LC. More details of this effect are in the Appendix 6 (Section 14.4).
Exact Acceptance Region [63, 64]
The Two-Sided Tolerance Interval approach shows that with the desired confidence, there is at least 99.34% of the 
distribution lies between the endpoints of the tolerance interval, which may be tighter than the entire range 83.5% – 
116.5%; therefore, the probability of results falling between 83.5% and 116.5% is greater than 99.34%.
Another approach is to calculate the total probability of a result falling within the entire 83.5% to 116.5% LC interval. 
In acceptance sampling terminology, finding the maximum s for means over a specified range with a stated level of 
confidence is called an “acceptance region of a double-sided specification sampling plan.” The approach determines 
a set of sample means and corresponding maximum standard deviations such that the sum of individual probabilities 
of results falling below 83.5% and above 116.5% is held constant at 0.66% (i.e., 100.00 – 99.34). Plots of the 
acceptance limit regions are given in Appendix 6 (Section 14.5).
The set of (X̅ , s) defines the acceptance region boundary. Details of the “Exact” method are given in Appendix 6 
(Section 14.5). An R program (not formally validated) that computes X̅  and s for X̅ ’s from 83.5% to 116.5% by 0.1% 
LC is given in Appendix 6 (Section 14.6).
A paper was published [65] that uses a polygon approach that approximates the “Exact” method and is easy to apply. 
Although the published method applies to passing the second stage of the USP UDU test, the method could also be 
modified for the overall USP UDU test [46]. More details are given in Appendix 6 (Section 14.7) for the “Exact” test 
and the polygon approximation.

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Note that the “Exact” method provides higher limits on the standard deviation than any other method.
Example
Suppose one dosage unit is tested at each of 30 locations with a sample mean of 97.3% LC and a standard deviation 
of 2.5% LC. Using a 90% confidence level and a 95% probability of passing the USP UDU test [46], the acceptance 
limit for the sample mean would be 3.679%, 4.036%, and 4.404% LC (rounding the 97.3 to 97.0 to be conservative) 
for the ASTM E2709/E2810 [44], Two-Sided Tolerance Interval, and “Exact” methods, respectively. Since 2.5% is less 
than any of these limits, the sample passes the acceptance criteria. Therefore with 90% confidence, there is at least a 
95% chance that the lot would pass the USP UDU test.
Tiered Approach
OC curves can be used to evaluate the three approaches using a single tier approach where there is only one set of 
test results. These curves are useful for choosing a sample size. OC curves and discussion are shown in Appendix 6 
(Section 14.9).
Companies may want to use a two-tier sampling plan that mimics the USP <905> test [46], that is, n = 10 units in tier 
1 and if the tier 1 criteria are not met an additional n = 20 units are tested in tier 2 for a total of n = 30 dosage units.
Figure 6.8 shows the probability of passing each method for lot means of 100% and 96% LC based on the 2-tiered 
plan. The x-axis is the lot standard deviation and the y-axis is the probability of passing each approach. Solid, 
dashed, and dotted lines are at the 50%, 90%, and 95% confidence levels.
For example, the probability of passing the USP UDU test [46] is 95% (vertical reference line) when the lot mean is 
100% and the lot standard deviation is about 6.25% LC. The corresponding probability of passing the ASTM E2709/
E2810 tiered approach [44] is about 1% using the 90% confidence level. At the 50% confidence level, it would be 
about 19%. All three approaches are assuring that in the “worst” case, there is at least a 95% probability of passing 
the USP UDU test.
Figure 6.8: Operating Characteristic Curves for ASTM E2709/E2810, Tolerance Interval, and “Exact” Methods 
at 50%, 90%, and 95% Confidence Level
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
Conclusion
The purpose of statistically-based routine release sampling and testing is to provide a high level of assurance 
that dosage unit uniformity is high throughout the batch, meeting patient needs and regulatory requirements and 
expectations. The sampling plan(s) and criteria can be established considering:
1.
OC curve relationships between PPQ, CPV, and the UDU test [46]
2.
Whether a single tier or two-tier sampling plan should be performed
3.
Which acceptance criteria should be used for evaluation of CU data
The ASTM E2709/E2810 [44], Two-Sided Tolerance Interval, and the “Exact” acceptance limits can all be used to 
evaluate CU data. All methods can provide a desired degree of assurance that a sample from a lot will pass the USP 
UDU test [46].
The ASTM E2709/E2810 method [4] is a standard, the Two-Sided Tolerance Interval acceptance table is the easiest 
to create, and the “Exact” method provides the largest acceptance limits. The ASTM E2709/E2810 and “Exact” 
methods require a computer program unless the table has been generated. The acceptance polygon approximation 
approach is also simple to use.
Although not included in the discussion or OC curves for the three methods, it is recommended that an additional 
criterion be included: that no CU result can fall outside 75% to 125% LC.
6.4	
Dissolution
The specification for immediate release dissolution contains a value of Q and dissolution time point (e.g., Q = 80 at 30 
minutes). The USP Dissolution <711> test [53] criteria are all relative to the value of Q and are provided in Table 6.4.
Table 6.4: USP Dissolution Criteria
Stage 1
Test 6 units (Result = % released at specified dissolution time point)
Pass if all 6 results ≥ Q + 5
Otherwise go to Stage 2
Stage 2
Test 6 additional units
Pass if for all 12 units the following criteria are met:
1) Mean result ≥ Q
2) No result ≤ Q – 15
Otherwise go to Stage 3
Stage 3
Test 12 additional units
Pass if for all 24 units the following criteria are met:
1) Mean result ≥ Q
2) No more than two results ≤ Q – 15 with no results ≤ Q – 25
Otherwise fail
ASTM E2709/E2810 [44] can be applied to dissolution immediate release. However, since the criteria for the 
dissolution test is different from the UDU test [46], all of the mathematics to obtain the lower bound to pass the test 
must be performed and therefore, the acceptance limit tables change.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 94 =====
Page 92	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
The strategy is the same as with CU with one exception. The confidence interval for the lot mean is a lower one-sided 
interval instead of two-sided since the only way to fail the test is to have low results. A pictorial is given in Figure 
6.9 showing the 95% contours for passing the USP Dissolution test based on simulation (thin solid red line) and by 
calculating the lower bound (thin dashed blue line) on passing the USP Dissolution test [53].
Figure 6.9: Pictorial of the ASTM E2709/E2810 Methodology Applied to Dissolution
Since the probability of passing the USP Dissolution test [53] only depends on how far the “true” lot mean is above Q, 
the x-axis is the difference between the lot mean and Q. The y-axis is the “true” lot standard deviation. The thin solid 
red line is the contour line where the probability of passing the USP UDU test [46] is at least 95%. Any point below the 
thin solid red line is in the parameter region meaning that any point in the region has greater than a 95% probability of 
passing the USP Dissolution test. The thin dashed blue line is the lower bound on passing the USP Dissolution test.
Any combination of batch means and standard deviations below this contour has at least a 95% chance of passing 
the USP Dissolution test. A 50% contour would lie above the 95% contour and a 99% contour lie below the 95% 
contour.
Based on statistics from a random sample or a systematic sample using Sampling Plan 1, the mean, X̅ , and standard 
deviation, s, are calculated and are also shown in Figure 6.9. Based on the sample mean and standard deviation, 
a joint confidence interval [61] is generated for the lot mean and standard deviation that is represented by the bold 
dashed black line.
Therefore, if the confidence interval at the desired confidence level is completely contained under the thin dashed 
blue line, then with the desired confidence, there is at least a 95% chance that the batch will pass the USP 
Dissolution test [53].
The point in the confidence interval with the lowest probability of passing the USP Dissolution test [53] is always at 
the vertex. The set of all sample means and standard deviations for a given sample size whose vertex fall on the thin 
dashed blue line forms the boundary for the acceptance region.
Any combination of sample mean and standard deviation on the acceptance region boundary generates a confidence 
region at the parameter region boundary. Acceptance regions for n = 6, 12, and 18 are shown in Figure 6.10. Any 
point falling in the acceptance region has, with the desired confidence level, at least a 95% probability of passing the 
USP Dissolution test [53].
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 95 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 93
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
The points on the acceptance region boundary are the points used to construct an acceptance limit table. An 
acceptance limit table for various sample sizes is given in Appendix 6 (Section 14.11).
The acceptance limits for dissolution are very conservative; therefore, other methods may provide wider limits. One 
approach is to simulate the probability of passing the USP Dissolution test [53] based on the vertex values for the lot 
mean and standard instead of using the lower bound calculation. A Bayesian approach may also provide an improved 
set of acceptance limits.
Acceptance Limit tables for Sampling Plan 2 can be constructed but are not included in this document.
Figure 6.10: Acceptance Regions for Dissolution Sampling Plan 1 (n = 6, 12, and 18)
Acceptance tables may also be developed for extended release using ASTM E2709 [58] but are not included in this 
document.
6.5	
Evaluate Legacy Products for Heightened Monitoring/Testing Need
Legacy products require a completed PV to ensure processes have the appropriate level of control to provide 
customers with products that comply with specifications. The steps to evaluate legacy products’ need for heightened 
monitoring/testing are shown in Figure 6.11.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.

ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Figure 6.11: Heightened Monitoring Evaluation Steps for Legacy Products [33]
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 97 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 95
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
Step 1 – Create/review company high-level strategy for legacy products.
Step 2 – Perform a gap assessment of the legacy products list to determine remediation priorities.
Step 3 – Begin product-specific evaluation by checking the legacy control strategy against the CPV to confirm 
process/data is in place so that CQA and release criteria can be evaluated against product process performance and 
variation. If not, establish a CPV program.
Step 4 – Perform capability test to check CQA data against CPV requirements. If insufficient data exists, perform 
product process capability study or execute a CPV protocol to gather data and ensure process control.
Step 5 – If the data shows that process is not capable, go back to process design phase data and evaluate CPP/
CQAs. Identify process steps/equipment that may cause increased process variation.
Step 6 – Evaluate monitoring/sampling system to determine if data collection steps are sufficient to provide a true 
view of critical process step capabilities and controls. Add increased monitoring/sampling to PV plan to remediate 
gaps.
Step 7 – Check designed process against actual process to confirm current process represents what is filed. Meet 
with regulatory team to discuss impact of changes and revise PV plan to include process changes and evaluate effect 
on control strategy. Execute PV if there is insufficient data to demonstrate control.
Step 8 – Move to routine CPV once CQA data demonstrates process control and process monitoring sufficiently 
demonstrates that effective controls are in place.
6.6	
Periodic Review and Revalidation
The principles contained in FDA PV Guidance [3] and Annex 15 [11] are consistent with enhanced process 
understanding, increased use of risk management in the pharmaceutical industry, and continual improvement as 
outlined in ICH Q8(R2), Q9, and Q10 [1, 2, 6].
Additionally, technology available for process monitoring and control has significantly advanced in recent years. While 
ongoing monitoring and periodic heightened testing represents a superior approach by assessing process health in a 
timelier fashion, periodic revalidation should still be considered.
Frequency of revalidation should depend on the rigor of the CPV program, including the frequency and thoroughness 
of within and between batch variability evaluation. While a between batch variability assessment likely occurs much 
more frequently than annually, heightened within batch testing should occur at least annually and be coordinated in 
advance of the APR, within which a statement of validation status should be made.
Even with a thorough CPV program, challenging of the process with worst-case extended control strategy variables 
such as different operators and shifts, worst-case hold times, worst-case material variabilities (as feasible) and 
laboratory variabilities at least once every three to five years is recommended.
Following evaluation of challenge batch results, the validation package should be updated with yearly evaluations 
back to the last full revalidation and the results of the challenge batch.
In determining whether or not revalidation is warranted, statistically-based routine results should also be considered. 
Where rigorous statistical standards are applied for within batch variability prior to the release of each batch, 
heightened within batch testing may not be periodically required when batches meet routine batch tier-one criteria. 
If batches regularly require additional testing and/or application of second-tier criteria, then revalidation should be 
considered.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 98 =====
Page 96	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
It is also recommended that in the decision to revalidate or conclude a validated state is being maintained, a risk 
assessment be completed annually and the results and associated priorities updated in the VMP.
These risk assessments can be completed by use of an excel-based risk assessment (see Figure 6.12), and should 
include:
•
Age of the validation packages
•
Number of process-related deviations that year
•
Product volume
•
Number of equipment changes
•
Type of dosage form
•
Product strength
•
Whether or not the existing validation package meets contemporary standards
•
Batch rejections
•
Assay qualification status
•
Data trends
The risk tool should include the rationale for the categories included and the scoring system. Process risk 
assessment results should be considered in conjunction with the results from the within batch heightened testing 
results and the inter-batch variability assessment.
It should be noted that revalidation never solves any process control issues; remediation needs to take place prior to 
revalidation.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

6.7	
Report: Frequency, Recommendations for Further Actions, Relevance with 
Annual Product Review
A key element of CPV is that the routine product reviews occur in as “real time” a basis as possible. The review 
discussions should include process performance evaluations, potential process-related customer complaints, 
stability performance, and deviation-prevention effectiveness, with action plans and follow-up for any mid to high-
level concerns. Rapid awareness of product concerns helps to ensure timely resolution of potential issues prior to 
impacting patient supply.
Both the events and actions from routine product reviews should be captured in a documented form that ensures 
awareness of any issues, actions, or both by key stakeholders such as quality and operations.
Depending on production volumes, formal documentation of these reviews and action endorsement could vary from 
the issuance of periodic reports (possibly bi-annual for mid to high-volume products) summarizing performance, to 
approval of meeting minutes by key stakeholders where CPV reviews and discussions take place, with more formal 
documentation via reports issued only as part of the APR.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 100 =====

ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
As mentioned earlier, it is recommended to leverage CPV data analysis and trending for the process performance 
review portions of the APR. The occurrence and conclusions of any statistical trend outliers identified as part of CPV 
and periodic team reviews should be tracked and recorded for use as part of the APR. Maintaining a summary of 
this information eliminates the need for rework in this area and avoids conducting the same process investigations 
significantly after the event has occurred.

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
7	 Contract Manufacturing
Many companies are turning to Contract Development Organizations (CDOs), CMOs, and/or Contract Packaging 
Organizations (CPOs) to develop and manufacture their products. One site may provide all services (CDMPOs), or 
several contract sites may be used to produce one product. The type of companies who are contracting work range 
from virtual companies (with all of their manufacturing and development activities outsourced) to large companies that 
use CDMPOs to augment their internal capacity, or as a risk mitigation strategy.
CDMPOs may be responsible for a portion of the product lifecycle or be involved from initial development to 
commercial launch. As PV in the current paradigm encompasses development of the design space through 
demonstration of process control throughout the product lifecycle, the CDMPO industry should be active participants 
in the PV process no matter what portion of the product lifecycle they directly impact. These organizations need to be 
knowledgeable in all aspects of the PV lifecycle so they can meet their client’s long-term goals.
While the license holders are ultimately responsible for the products’ adherence to the current regulatory expectations 
surrounding PV, it is important that CDMPOs have well-defined programs and the skills and knowledge to execute.
Example case studies are described in Appendix 4.
7.1	
Contract Development, Manufacturing, and Packaging Organizations and 
License Holder Relationship
The significance of the relationship between the CDMPO and license holder(s), including partners, cannot be 
understated. Creating a partnership between all parties ensures that everyone is working toward a common goal with 
the end user, the patient, always in mind. It is essential to establish clear expectations at the beginning of a project. 
This may include exchanging procedures and templates, and both parties should be able to freely share information 
to maximize the potential for success.
When using multiple contracting organizations, it is important to consider how the different organizations will 
communicate. The ability to have multi-party confidentiality agreements to maximize communication is a best 
practice. It is particularly important that information from Stage 1, usually generated by a CDO or at an internal site, 
is clearly communicated to those who will be responsible for later stages of manufacturing. In addition, knowledge 
of small scale and clinical batch manufacturing is very helpful when determining the number of batches to be 
manufactured in Stage 2 and determining the potential enhanced sampling required in Stage 3.
7.2	
Maintaining Compliance while Ensuring Flexibility
One of the most important attributes of a successful CDMPO with respect to PV is the ability to be flexible enough 
to meet the needs of all clients while maintaining the ability to have a consistent and defendable program. CDMPOs 
should have policies in place that outline the minimum requirements for performance at all stages of PV but should 
allow flexibility within the bounds of regulatory expectations to ensure that they best meet the needs of their clients.
7.3	
Best Practices in Stage 1
During Stage 1, the process design is defined. The difficulties that many contract organizations face is that the 
expectations for defining design and developing products can vary widely by their clients. Some clients will expect a 
significant number of experiments, stability data, and documentation, while some may have very little to no data and 
are not able to invest significant resources to generate this data.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 102 =====
Page 100	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
For example, when manufacturing generic or legacy products, the amount of data available may only be a batch 
record from a previous manufacturing site. However, the products are still important to the patient population 
relying on them, and a minimum standard needs to be defined related to how much data is required to ensure the 
manufacturing process will produce a consistent product.
If a CDO is used to generate Stage 1 data, this contracting organization should have general knowledge about the 
planned manufacturing site. Some of the important attributes to consider include what equipment will be used, the 
basic process flow at the manufacturing site, and the planned technical capabilities of that site. If this information is 
understood up front, then the development organization can generate experimental designs that provide relevant data 
to the future manufacturing process.
However, the commercial manufacturing site is often not defined at the start of development. One approach is to have 
a product-specific control strategy that outlines the CQAs, process parameters (all classifications), and in-process 
and final product controls required to effectively manufacture the product, and then match these requirements with 
the manufacturing site. If this approach is taken, then it is important to understand the common equipment and 
technical capabilities of most manufacturing sites to ensure that the design will not limit manufacturing site choice.
Another approach is to utilize a Contract Development and Manufacturing Organization (CDMO) that would develop 
the process and manufacture the product at the same site. In this scenario, the development scientists generally 
have systems designed with the manufacturing process in mind. It is also important, in this case, to document the 
development activities and define how the developed process will ensure the product manufactured meets all desired 
quality attributes.
Using a control strategy or Quality Target Product Profile (QTPP) document, the CDO or CDMO should clearly 
articulate all process steps, the critical attributes of each step, and how control is achieved to the CMO and CPO. A 
review of raw material requirements and expected controls (including excipients, containers, and disposables) should 
also be included in the control strategy.
This control strategy should reference all studies performed in support of the product design. Having a single document 
that references all data related to product development is helpful during subsequent regulatory audits to demonstrate 
that the process and product knowledge has been captured and that an adequate design was determined.
For new products, performing process-specific risk assessments before manufacturing batches for PPQ is useful to 
ensure that the manufacturing process is capable as designed to meet the parameters determined in development 
and documented in the control strategy.
For site transfers, performing a process-specific risk assessment is also appropriate to augment the control strategy 
and demonstrate how the local process compares with the product being transferred. If the product is a technical 
transfer or is being manufactured at another site, generating a comparability report at this stage can also help with 
later regulatory filing.
While transferred products may already be developed, it is important to perform some development studies during 
site transfer that could impact the product at scale. For example, product hold or mixing studies at scale should 
be considered prior to moving into Stage 2 at scale at each site of manufacturing. The CDO and CMO sites, as 
applicable, should have procedures in place that describe how these types of studies are performed and should be 
able to provide evidence that the program is ready to incorporate this data into the control strategy.
Analytical development and method qualification should be completed prior to proceeding to Stage 2. Mapping the 
testing sites and documenting a list of sampling requirements plus shipping and storage requirements prior to starting 
Stage 2 is considered a best practice.
In cases where clinical manufacturing, initial registration, and stability batches are done at one facility and validation 
and commercial launch at another facility, attention to detail when documenting data in Stage 1 and any other 
batches prior to validation will increase the chance of success after site transfer.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 103 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 101
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
7.4	
Best Practices in Stage 2
The expectations of Stage 2 PV are generally the same for CMOs, CPOs, and internal site manufacturing 
organizations. However, contract organizations have some unique challenges as they are often manufacturing a 
greater number of different processes on the same equipment than an internal manufacturing site, providing services 
to clients with different expectations and procedures.
A VMP summarizing all the activities to be performed and a high-level rationale for strategy decisions is often helpful. 
This document can describe bracketing, matrixing, or family approaches and the rationale for the total number of 
batches to be manufactured. In the situations where concurrent activities are being performed, such as completing 
filter validation, PPQ batch manufacturing, packing qualification, and shipping studies simultaneously, the VMP is a 
good tool to wrap all of these activities together and provide one consolidated overview of the product qualification 
approach.
All PV activities should be summarized in a report. For complicated strategies in which multiple protocols and reports 
are generated, it may be useful to generate a master validation report summarizing all completed activities in addition 
to the process report. For large validation exercises it may also be appropriate to have multiple validation plans. For 
example, in an oral solid dosage process there could be a plan for granulation, one for compression, and one for 
coating. The site-specific procedures or VMP should define the requirements for reporting.
Robust documentation confirming site readiness prior to PPQ batches is a best practice and is especially important at 
a contract manufacturing site where customers may not have ready access to non-client specific documentation.
The readiness document should include an evaluation of the qualification status of all equipment, facilities, utilities, 
and methods to ensure all product-specific and non product-specific items are in a qualified state in alignment with 
the product’s requirements, in addition to the general requirements in place from the contract manufacturer. As 
CMOs often have many activities going on at the same time, it is important to confirm that a comprehensive review 
of a product’s requirements is performed considering all other ongoing activities, including any product change-over 
requirements, as CMOs are almost always processing multiple products on the same manufacturing lines.
One of the unique challenges faced by a CMO is protocol generation, given the different requirements, terminology, 
and expectations from each client. The operators performing the tasks are less likely to make errors if documentation 
is consistent, but clients are often looking for customized solutions. A best practice is defining a template protocol that 
can present consistent information to operators, while allowing flexibility in content to support clients.
The completion of PPQ batches is often a prerequisite to filing the product, so it can be an activity that is requested 
to be expedited. A best practice is defining a consistent project schedule that can be used for planning as well as 
providing the contracting site with sufficient time for report generation and review. Quality of information and robust 
evaluation of data to ensure conclusions are logical and based on fact cannot be risked in an effort to achieve a 
timeline; therefore, up-front planning is critical.
Another best practice when writing PPQ reports includes referencing the Stage 3 plan.
PPQ is most frequently used to describe the process of ensuring the consistency of product manufacturing and 
placement into a primary container. When the primary container is then further processed to include a device, 
performing PPQ of the packing process is also considered a best practice, in which case a CPO should have a 
documented strategy to support their customers.
While the CPO may not need to complete a formal PPQ process for a manufacturing process that only requires the 
primary container to be cartoned, the CPO should have a strategy that defines when PPQ is required for packaging 
activities.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 104 =====
Page 102	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
7.5	
Best Practices in Stage 3
Stage 3 PV is an important tool for ensuring that product manufacturing remains in control.
The role of contracting organizations, specifically CMOs and CPOs, in the CPV process may not be well defined; 
however, best practices include sharing information across sites (for example, CMO and internal site, multiple CMOs, 
separate CDO, CMO, and CPOs). This allows for appropriate actions to be taken to ensure the process remains in 
control and the product continues to consistently meet all defined quality attributes.
One best practice related to CPV is to make certain that the CMO includes specific information in the quality 
agreement as to who is responsible for execution of the CPV program.
The CMO should have a defined program that specifies how they will administer a CPV program if responsible, and if 
not responsible, how they will respond to information provided by the responsible party.
The quality agreement should require that information related to trends is shared.
The contracting organization (CMO or CPO) should have the ability to include additional sampling to support Stage 
3.1, if required. It should have a defined mechanism for removing this additional sampling when no longer needed 
and to tighten control parameters as the data demonstrates that this is desirable.
The contracting organization should understand what is critical to a process, what should be monitored, and how 
the data should be analyzed. The CPV system should allow flexibility in the reporting period and the types of data 
to be collected, while having defined minimum standards. Additionally, the CMO or CPO should have qualified 
professionals on staff to support statistical analysis of data.
The CPV program should be designed to be proactive and have mechanisms by which to facilitate interaction with 
other sites in the product’s supply chain that may contribute to the findings or benefit from the information.
APRs should also be performed either by the Contract Manufacturing and Packaging Organization (CMPO) or the 
license holder in partnership with the CMPO, and a best practice would include a description in the quality agreement 
of how the CPV and APRs work together. APRs may cover multiple sites and facilities as well and the CMO should 
have processes in place to support multisite APRs if needed.
7.6	
Stage Gate Processes
One useful tool when working with a CDMPO is to have a stage gate process in place where the responsibilities for 
specific deliverables are defined. These deliverables should encompass all stages of the PV that will be performed at 
the CDMPO and ensure that specific time-bound reviews of progress occur.
As a part of the stage gate process, the first step is to agree on roles and responsibilities. An example RACI is 
provided in Figure 7.1.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
Figure 7.2: Example Stage Gate Process for Product Transfer
Following a stage gate process will ensure that nothing is missed and that the project teams are on track. Ensuring 
project documentation is complete early, data analysis from previous batches are effectively studied, and robust 
operator training is complete increases the chance of success and reduces misses throughout the process.
Contracting organizations often have significant experience with PV as they frequently manufacture many different 
products. Using RACI and stage gate tools makes sure that any complexity in the supply chain, which is inherent 
when working with multiple organizations, is well managed. Each contract manufacturer should define the activities 
required at each gate based on site-specific procedures.
7.7	
Regulatory Audit Expectations
Once Stage 2 activities are completed successfully, often there is a period of time when all parties are jointly 
preparing for filing and eventual commercial launch. While the license holder is ultimately responsible for the 
product filing, it is an expectation that during the Prior Approval Inspection review, the CDMPO can clearly articulate 
information related to all stages of PV, even if they are only responsible for one piece.
This is specifically true for CMOs, which most frequently own the Stage 2 process. Ensuring that all development 
activities are clearly documented, linked to the final production process, and understood at the manufacturing site is 
an important aspect of audit preparedness.
7.8	
Contract Development, Manufacturing, and Packaging Organizations – Specialized 
versus One Source Models
Contracting organizations can specialize in one attribute of the manufacturing process or provide services to meet the 
needs of some or all manufacturing activities. This is applicable to the PV design as the more product transfers that 
occur, the more carefully knowledge transfer should be planned.
The benefit of a specialist may be added capabilities and/or increased focus on a particular attribute of the product 
lifecycle.
An organization that is able to develop, manufacture, and package provides the ability to streamline processes and 
improve communication. This can result in reduced supply chain complexity and the ability to share data more freely.

8	 Change Management
8.1	
Change Management Principles
The principles of an effective change management system apply regardless of the stage within the product lifecycle.
In the development phase, a change management system is necessary to evaluate and implement changes, for 
example, from resolution of troubleshooting exercises.
Where a pharmaceutical system is required to support products and processes developed using an enhanced, QbD 
approach and the principles described in ICH Q8(R2) [1], an effective change management system is essential. 
Section 3.2.3 in ICH Q10 [6] describes items for inclusion in the change management system in relation to the stage 
in the lifecycle, such as:
•
The potential impact of the change using QRM tools
•
Evaluation of the proposed change by SME
•
Evidence that the change objectives were achieved
The GMP requirements must be met regardless of the stage of the lifecycle or whether the product and
processes have been developed using minimal or enhanced, QbD approaches. GMP requirements usually
include:
-
An SOP that describes the approach to evaluation, approval, and implementation of the proposed change,
and confirmation that the change was effective and did not have negative consequences
-
Clear accountabilities for all change management activities
-
Prioritization of urgent and important changes (e.g., a corrective or preventive action)
•
With regard to change management, the quality unit should:
-
Ensure that a change management system is established
-
Approve or reject proposed changes
-
Audit the impact of changes post implementation
The change management process requirements become more defined as products move from development to 
qualification and lifecycle continued verification stages. The process needs to assure the safety, efficacy, and quality 
of the product that results from the change.
The extent and depth of the application of the change management principles can vary according to the stage in 
the lifecycle where the change is proposed. For example, there may be less rigorous application of these principles 
during development, particularly during early stage development, than in commercial manufacture.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

8.1.1	
Change Management System Model
ICH Q10 [6] defines change management as:
“A systematic approach to proposing, evaluating, approving, implementing and reviewing changes.”
This description makes clear that the scope of change management is broader than the scope of change control, 
which is described in ICH Q7, Section 13 [66]. “Change control” often refers to the execution step of an individual 
change, whereas “change management” is a more systematic holistic approach to the review and management of a 
portfolio of changes and the change process, including all components of “change control.”
In an ICH Q10-based [6] Pharmaceutical Quality System, change management applies to the entire product lifecycle. 
Additionally, if an organization chooses to operate an ISO 9001 system [67], the change management system could 
include elements not covered under ICH Q10 [6] or GMP, for example, changes to business processes.
The high-level model described in this section is adapted from the definition of change management and the four 
steps described in ICH Q10 [6] (see Figure 8.1). Each step ends with either a gate or a decision point, which should 
be completed before proceeding. This section also provides further information on each step within the model.
Following these steps is considered an effective way to ensure that proposed changes are evaluated and 
implemented effectively. It may be necessary to return to an earlier stage, for instance, if the benefits of 
implementation are not fully realized. The level of formality of application of these steps can vary depending on the 
stage in the lifecycle, see below.
Figure 8.1: Step and Gate Model for Change Management [9]
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.
