8.1.2	
Change Management in the Product Lifecycle
Change management needs to be utilized throughout the product lifecycle process as a way to communicate and 
manage changes in a documented manner. The initial step during this process is to define a product control strategy, 
including raw materials, utilities, facilities, equipment, and procedural controls. As the process evolves, the control 
strategy should be refined.
The control strategy will act as a way to identify which developmental data can be used to determine the process 
control state. For example, certain facilities implement design control early in the process, which allows data to be 
utilized later. Other sites do not utilize change control until after the process is qualified, and therefore development 
data is suspect and probably will not be allowed.
Changes in the control strategy may affect the state of process and product control.
8.1.3	
Change Management of Design Space and Control Strategy
It is important to use change management in the development phase to facilitate knowledge transfer and ensure 
appropriate controls are in place. Each step identified as part of the control strategy needs to be defined as early as 
possible so team members know what changes were tested and how they were developed.
At a minimum, process changes during development need to be recorded in a development notebook. A better 
approach is to utilize protocols or batch records that define what process controls are implemented during the design 
phase as well as the status of raw materials used.
QA review and approval of records may be required if batches are used in a GMP environment.
8.2	
Changing Manufacturing Sites
The steps for changing a manufacturing site, such as expansion to another site within the company or performing a 
transfer to a CMO, are detailed the ISPE Good Practice Guide: Technology Transfer [41].
The steps for identifying the effect of changing manufacturing sites on the validated state begins with initiating a 
change control as indicated in Section 8.1.2. The change control process should include a process risk assessment 
to determine the impact to the CPPs and CQAs of any process step changes.
Typically manufacturing site changes include component or raw material changes that need to be reviewed. It is 
recommended to keep changes to a minimum at first to demonstrate equivalency. Steps to consider are utilizing 
the current manufacturing site’s components, raw materials, and process steps for the initial transfer, and then 
implementing changes after the process transfer has demonstrated control.
All steps should be documented as part of the change control including an overall assessment, testing, results, and a 
final report. A risk analysis should provide a review of process changes, environmental changes, personnel training, 
and equipment assessment with input from engineering, manufacturing, regulatory, purchasing, and quality.
A review of design development (Stage 1) design space, and Stage 2 equipment qualification and process 
qualification information, will provide insights into the level of risk and required corrective action. Each of the 
assessments should be recorded in protocols, assessments, white paper studies, or meeting minutes. This data will 
provide the framework for the decision on risk level and consequently the type of testing required to implement the 
change.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 112 =====
Page 110	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Gaps identified as part of the risk assessment need to be remediated. The remediation typically includes specification 
updates, engineering/water runs (including data gathering with sampling plans), procedural updates, and test method 
updates. After data gathered from engineering/water runs demonstrate that the process performs consistently, and 
maintains or reduces process variability, the team can proceed to revalidation.
The number of revalidation runs is based upon the level of residual risk from the risk assessment and the number 
of batches (Section 5.1) required to demonstrate that the process will maintain a state of control and identify 
opportunities for improvement.
One approach to revalidation is to utilize CPP and CQA data from a statistically-significant number of manufacturing 
batches from the primary manufacturing site to define control limits and to define acceptance criteria for both Cpk and 
Ppk data of transfer batches. Increased sampling should be instituted based upon risk levels until the level of control 
attained by the new site is equivalent or greater than that achieved by the primary manufacturing site.
It is important to note that no product should be released to the market until all assessments, studies, and reports 
have been reviewed by the regulatory group and released by the quality unit. Often material component changes will 
result in changes to CQAs, and therefore may be considered adulterated if released for sale.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Appendix 1
Appendix 1
9	 Appendix 1 – Case Study: Legacy Products
Appendices 1 to 4 provides a series of case studies consisting mainly of real products produced at companies utilizing 
approaches deemed compliant at the time. The authors of the case studies did not have the benefit of this ISPE 
Good Practice Guide: Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation when validating their 
processes, and some regulatory inspectors were early in the knowledge curve when these products were approved.
9.1	
Executive Summary
This chapter presents a PV lifecycle case study wherein a legacy solid oral dose manufacturing process in the US 
was scaled from a 40 ft3 (1.13 m3) bin blender unit operation to an 80 ft3 (2.27 m3) bin blender unit operation. The 
case study outlines an overview of good practices for systematically assessing and managing risk in the lifecycle 
approach for PV Stage 1 (Process Design), Stage 2.2 (PPQ) and Stage 3.1 (CPV).
Historical data from the 40 ft3 blending process was reviewed and assessed. Comparable product and manufacturing 
processes at the 80 ft3 scale were evaluated to ascertain expected manufacturing variability. Following the Stage 
2 PPQ study, additional commercial lots were produced. The data was statistically evaluated to demonstrate an 
exceptionally robust process.
This case study combines qualitative and quantitative assessments of product risk, using statistical tools that quantify 
process understanding and illuminate process robustness. The sampling plan philosophy reviewed past knowledge 
and experience to estimate the sampling required to obtain the desired assurance. The process was successfully 
scaled from a 40 ft3 bin blender to an 80 ft3 bin blender without introducing any measurable additional process variation.
9.2	
Case Study Overview and Background
The examples and data provided in this case study were derived from the modification of an approved manufacturing 
process.
The product was an immediate release film coated tablet manufactured in a 40 ft3 bin blender based on ICH Q8(R2) 
QbD principles [1]. The original process underwent a Stage 2.2 PPQ study and was monitored as part of the firm’s 
Stage 3.2 CPV program.
Per current FDA SUPAC-IR guidance [68], changes in the batch size from the pivotal/pilot-scale bio-batch material to 
larger or smaller production batches requires submission of additional information in the application. The change to 
an 80 ft3 bin blender unit operation was classified as a Level 1 change requiring [68]:
•	
Notification of change and submission of updated batch records in the annual report.
•	
One batch on long-term stability reported in annual report.
•	
All scale-up changes properly validated.
The proposed Stage 2.2 strategy met all regulatory requirements. Note that products that underwent QbD-based 
product development may apply tools such as a comparability/post-approval change management protocol.
Fifty successful batches of this product at the 40 ft3 scale were manufactured without any process deviations or 
failures. A run chart for the CU attribute (50 lots) of the produced batches is shown in Figure 9.1.
For each commercial batch, 10 tablets were randomly sampled and assessed for CU following USP <905> Uniformity 
of Dosage Units [46] and submitted specifications.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

The blend process parameters are similar for each of the four formulations. Qualitatively, because of extensive 
experience with comparable products at this scale, the risk of adapting the same process parameters for the scale-up 
verification study (Stage 1) is low.
A bin fill level of 58% was chosen for the 80 ft3 bin, which is close to the optimal recommended level of 60%. This 
bin fill level improves sheer among particles in addition to axial/radial mixing, and improves mixing dynamics. The 
blending processes with kinematic (same number of revolutions) similarity were adopted.
A preliminary qualitative assessment created a scientific review of past experience (by application of knowledge 
management tools as an enabler) and potential issues, and provided guidance for appropriate settings and 
conditions, thus negating the need to develop and determine blend process parameters from scratch (see Table 9.3).
The scientific review and assessment eliminated the need to acquire raw materials for multiple full-scale batches 
and saved valuable manufacturing floor time and other associated resources for more productive operations. The 
approach provided qualitative assurance that proper blend process parameters were identified prior to a full-scale 
verification at PV Stage 1.
Stage 1 demonstration batches were manufactured to primarily characterize the bin parameters. Other processes/
equipment (i.e., compression and coating parameters) were verified during demonstration batch manufacturing.

9.3	
Critical Quality Attributes Operating Characteristic Curves
A break from the case study story is now made to express a more detailed description of statistical tools applicable to 
understanding product risk.
A basic objective of many statistical analyses is to provide assurance that the product will consistently and reliably 
meet the quality attributes for each CQA. A limited number of samples are removed from the process and the 
analyses of these samples are used to infer the characteristics of the entire population. If the population follows a 
Gaussian distribution (i.e., the distribution is Normal), the overall characteristics can be reasonably described by two 
variables, the mean and the standard deviation.
Specific CQA acceptance criteria transform the data distribution into the probability of passing the test for the 
particular CQA. The acceptance probability provides assurance that samples removed from the described distribution 
will meet the defined requirements (that is, the acceptance probability is a measure of the process capability).
For simple attributes, such as assay, wherein a single measurement is compared to specification limits, it is 
reasonable to apply statistical tools such as traditional capability measures to provide this assurance.
However, the acceptance criteria for most CQAs in the pharmaceutical industry pose a challenge for a direct 
application of traditional capability approaches. For example, immediate release dissolution requirements, per USP 
<711> guidelines [53], follow a stage-wise acceptance; thus, a direct application of a capability approach becomes 
complicated.
While the acceptance criteria appear to pose daunting complications to the analysis process, the defined acceptance 
rules (i.e., specification requirements) can be analytically resolved through the use of an OC curve. The OC curve 
analytically transforms the “true” population statistics (mean and standard deviation) into the probability of meeting or 
passing the acceptance criteria. As an example, the transformation for assay can be determined analytically using the 
following equation that uses the Normal distribution function:
	
Prob of Passing = NORM.DIST(‘True Pop’n Mean’, USL, ’True Pop’n StDev’, TRUE) - (NORM.DIST(‘True Pop’n 
Mean’, LSL, ’True Pop’n StDev’, TRUE))
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 117 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 115
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
Appendix 1
Figure 9.2 provides an example set of OC curves derived using the above equation for assay with specification limits 
of 90.0 – 110.0%.
Figure 9.2: Operating Characteristic Curves for Assay
Figure 9.2 plots a series of True Population Standard Deviations curves along a True Population Average Assay 
on the x-axis. The standard deviation is provided in 0.1% increments with solid curves representing unit standard 
deviation values. These curves are analytically derived based on the probabilities of meeting the specification 
requirements for an associated Normal distribution with denoted statistics (mean and standard deviation).
Solid vertical lines represent the 90.0% and 110.0% specification limits. The curves relate the probability of meeting 
the specification requirements if the population statistics were precisely known. Note that if a population had a 90.0% 
average, then a single sampled unit pulled for an assay analysis has a 50% chance of meeting the specification.
A similar set of curves can be derived for both Stage 1 and Stage 2 acceptance of the CU specification requirements 
outlined in USP <905> [46]. The Stage 2 curves presented here represent the cumulative probability and thus 
effectively provide the overall probability of meeting the USP <905> guidelines. Because the specification rules for 
CU are more complex than for assay, the derivations of the OC curves are more intricate than for the assay CQA.
Note that the USP <905> acceptance criteria [46] for CU are based on both the mean and variance of a small 
randomly derived sample set of the entire population. Once appropriately developed, the OC curves properly 
transform the true population statistics into the probability of meeting or passing the defined requirements.
OC curves for CU based on the requirements of USP <905> [46] are provided in Figure 9.3. The Stage 2 curves 
(representing the overall test requirements) are directly comparable to Sampling Plan 1 and, when properly used in 
conjunction with measures of the within and between variation of a stratified sampling process, will provide a similar 
assessment as offered by Sampling Plan 2.

Determining the Assurance Probability (Pa and Pal) and the Capability (Dpk)
The above OC curves theoretically define the transformation of the detailed CQA requirements into the probability of 
passing the specific CQA test. Two terms (Pa and Pal) that describe the assurance of meeting CQA testing are used 
herein.
Based on the sampled statistics, the acceptance probability (Pa) provides the chance that a test result derived 
from the same population will meet the CQA test requirements. Pa relates the expected performance based on the 
observed sample statistics.
Pa is related to standard statistical process capability terms (Cpk and Ppk); however, for Content (Dosage) Uniformity, 
the USP stage-wise acceptance specifications [46] distort a direct application of these process capability measures. 
A comparable capability measure, Dpk (based on the Pa computation), is used that provides a measure similar to the 
standard statistical terms. Dpk provides a capability measure based on the probability of producing a defect (assuming 
a Normally distributed data set).
Using the OC curves, Pa (and subsequently) Dpk capability can be determined for each stage in the USP method [46], 
and relates the capability of the process to meet the requirements for that specific stage. Table 9.4 expresses how Pa 
and Dpk are related.
Table 9.4: Relationship of Pa and Dpk
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 119 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 117
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
Appendix 1
A lower limit of Pa, termed Pal, provides a confidence bound on how well sampling defines the Pa measurement. 
Pal provides a measurable assurance, based on a desired confidence, that the process will meet the specification 
requirements. It provides an assurance similar to that provided by a tolerance interval except where a tolerance 
interval only provides an assurance of meeting a defined bound at a given confidence, Pal provides a measurable 
bound for a given confidence. Where a tolerance interval only provides a “pass/fail” indication (e.g., the process will 
meet a 95/95 criteria), Pal relates a continuum that indicates how close the process is to an acceptable assurance 
level. For example, Pal may indicate 95% confidence that 99.8% of future tests will meet the requirement, which 
clearly passes a 95/95 criterion by a reasonable margin.
There are four possible confidence interval combinations, though only the upper (or High StDev) combinations 
provide practical relevance to the limiting concern.
1.	
Low mean and Low standard deviation
2.	
Low mean and High standard deviation
3.	
High mean and Low standard deviation
4.	
High mean and High standard deviation
These confidence interval combinations, when applied to the OC curve, generate a confidence interval “box”. 
Recall that the OC curve provides a theoretical transformation of the CQA acceptance criteria of the true population 
distribution into the probability of passing or the acceptance bound. Confidence intervals describe how well the 
sampled statistics capture the true population statistics.
Figure 9.4 graphically presents an example of a confidence interval box applied to the assay OC curve. In this 
example, a set of 10 units was removed from the population and tested for assay. The sampled average and standard 
deviation were determined and plotted as an asterisk (). This point defines the expected assurance probability (Pa).
The four confidence interval combinations are determined (with α = 0.05) and represented by a lined box. One can 
now infer, with the desired (95%) confidence, that the true population statistics reside within this confidence box. The 
lowest probability of passing point on this box represents the highest risk and is defined as the Lower Assurance 
Probability (Pal). In this specific example, one can state with 95% confidence that 97.4% of possible assay tests 
removed from this population will meet the 90.0 to 110.0% specification requirements.
Figure 9.4: Confidence Interval Box
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

9.4	
Estimate the Number of Samples for the Process Validation Stage 2.2 Protocol
Returning to our case study example, a review of historical data provided a perspective of the statistical capability of 
the current 40 ft3 process, as well as the capability of the production operations experience with comparable 80 ft3 
processes.
The qualitative risk assessment indicated that the scale-up presented a low-risk situation. For the current study of the 
scale-up between 40 ft3 and 80 ft3 bin blenders, data from the long history of 40 ft3 production batches was used to 
estimate the average CU of the process (average CU = 100.69). A VCA of data from the comparable 80 ft3 processes 
(averaging the variance from across the 50 produced batches of four comparable processes) was used to project 
the estimated variability of the process (providing an estimated within lot standard deviation = 1.71 and between 
lot standard deviation of 0.85). These products evaluated showed low variability, achieved by implementing robust 
product control strategies throughout the lifecycle.
An assessment of the historical data projected that three Stage 2.2 PPQ batches would be sufficient to assure 
that the blend process at the 80 ft3 scale could consistently produce uniform powder blends per defined process 
parameters.
Three Stage 2 PPQ batches were manufactured and the data demonstrated with 95% confidence that greater than 
90% of future lots (with consistent materials, a stable, constant manufacturing environment, and robust test methods) 
would meet the desired specification requirements.
Applying the lower acceptance probability method (Pal – see detailed description in Section 9.3) with the estimated 
statistics derived for this case study example, and a sampling plan that includes 60 units for each batch, a projection 
of anticipated Pal was generated for a successive number of produced lots (shown in Figure 9.5).
Figure 9.5: Projection of Anticipated Pal
If the estimated statistics properly represent the scaled-up process, these computations project the number of lots 
required to achieve a 95% confidence that 90% of tests will meet the USP <905> CU requirements for both Stage 1 
and Stage 2 (recall that Stage 2 represents the overall test requirement) [46]. In this particular case, three produced 
batches should be sufficient to achieve the desired assurance.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

Appendix 1
Note that this assessment concludes that if a PPQ validation study were completed with just two lots of data and if 
the observed statistics were as expected from the projection (mean = 100.69, within standard deviation = 1.71, and 
between lot standard deviation = 0.85) then there would be 95% confidence that only about 50% of lots would meet 
the USP requirements. Using the same statistics with the quantity of data from a third lot projects that there will be 
95% confidence that at least 90% of the lots will meet the USP requirements indicates that three PPQ lots should be 
sufficient to satisfy the protocol conditions. A fourth produced lot would increase the lower 95% confidence level of 
assurance above 99.7%.
9.5	
Assess Stage 2.2 Data
In the specific instance of this case study, three batches of the process using the 80 ft3 bin blender were produced. 
Samples were removed according to the described protocol, which included a standard 20 × 3 (60 units total) 
stratified CU assessment for each batch produced.
Recall again that the basic objective of the statistical analysis of Stage 2.2 data is to provide assurance that the 
product will consistently and reliably meet the quality attributes for each CQA. The basic Pa method provides this 
assurance; however, the analysis can be complicated by the effect of within and between batch variation.
There are two general scenarios related to variation sources:
a.
Those scenarios where the between batch/location variation is small and insignificant compared to the within
batch/location variation
b.
Those scenarios where the between batch/location variation is significant when compared to the within batch/
location variation
In Scenario (a), it is reasonable to pool individual data for the acceptance probability analysis as the between location 
variation is insignificant (this is comparable to Sampling Plan 1), but in Scenario (b), care must be taken to determine 
the effect of batch-to-batch variation (an assessment comparable to Sampling Plan 2).
An Analysis of Variance (ANOVA) provides a statistical hypothesis test that assesses, at a rudimentary level, if the 
variation between the means of several groups (e.g., between batches/locations) are statistically significant when 
compared to the within batch/location variation. Table 9.5 provides an example ANOVA for 20 locations of CU data (3 
units were assessed for each location) acquired from the first 80 ft3 scale-up Stage 2.2 PPQ batch.
Table 9.5: Example Analysis of Variance (ANOVA) Content Uniformity Data

Figure 9.6: Example Analysis of Variance (ANOVA) Content Uniformity Data Run Chart
The p-value for the CU data collected in Stage 2.2 PPQ Lot 1 was greater than 0.05, which indicates that the between 
location variation is not statistically significant when compared to the within location variation. In this scenario, where 
the between location variation is not significant when compared to the within location variation, a Pa computation can 
be reasonably completed by using the overall mean and standard deviation (and associated confidence intervals) of 
all pooled batch data with the OC curve (essentially a Sampling Plan 1 assessment).
This was accomplished as described earlier in assurance probability determination (Section 9.3). The computed 
Pa and Pal for this specific batch indicates with 95% confidence that this lot should meet the CU acceptance value 
requirements described in USP <905> [46] more than 99.99% of the time if repeatedly tested. This conclusion is 
consistent with the result derived using the ASTM E2810/E2709 method [44].
A similar analysis was completed for each of the three distinct Stage 2.2 PPQ batches and indicated that each batch, 
assessed independently, had a high probability of routinely meeting the USP requirements [46].
The above analysis (comparable in practice to the assurance provided by ASTM E2810/E2709 [44]) reviewed each lot 
distinctly and independently and provided the desired assurance that each specific lot would routinely meet the USP 
<905> requirements [46]. From a PPQ perspective, it is also important to assess the effects of batch-to-batch variation
and provide an assurance that the process, if maintained, is robust and will continue to meet the desired requirements.
For this case study assessment, three lots, each with 60 units, were manufactured and measured. Note that since 
the individual lot assessment concluded that the between location was not significant when compared to the within 
location, each lot of 60 data points was pooled for the between batch assessment. Table 9.6 contains an ANOVA that 
compares the within batch and between batch variation.

In this multi-batch analysis, the ANOVA hypothesis indicates that the variation between batches is statistically

graphical representation of the three Stage 2.2 PPQ batches CU data is provided in Figure 9.7.
Figure 9.7: Content Uniformity Run Chart
Since the between batch variation is statistically significant when compared to the within batch variation, a more 
comprehensive statistical analysis was warranted, as it is not reasonable to assume that the data can be combined 
into one large pool.
The first step in this more comprehensive analysis is to complete a variance component analysis to derive estimates 
of both the within and between batch standard deviation, as well as the associated confidence intervals. The variance 
component analysis for this assessment is provided in Table 9.7.

The process used to determine the expected assurance probability (Pa) is similar to that previously described, but 
with an added step of integrating the contributing fraction of each expected “between” batch segment.
Conceptually consider that multiple batches are produced that have a varying (Normally distributed) between lot 
variation that dictates the average CU for that batch, and that each conceptual lot is produced with a consistent within 
lot variability. The overall acceptance probability is provided by an integration of these conceptual lots.

Using this method, it is expected that > 99.9999% (Dpk > 2.0) of 10 unit CU samples removed (per USP <905> [46]) 
from any of these three batches will meet the Stage 1 AV requirements described in USP <905> and minimum of 
96.13% (Pal) will meet the requirements with 95% confidence. Although the between batch variation was statistically 
significant, this assessment provides reasonable assurance that the observed between batch variability is not of 
practical significance; it has a medium-low risk potential (the slight risk is associated with batch-to-batch uncertainty) 
for the CU CQA. The batch-to-batch uncertainty will diminish as more batches are produced and tested.
9.6	
Product Risk Evaluation and Enhanced Controlled Strategy for Continued Process 
Verification (Stage 3.1)
The statistical results for each CQA from the Stage 2.2 PPQ studies were compiled using the appropriate OC curves 
to derive both Pa and Pal. As mentioned, acceptance probabilities (Pa) are comparable to a process capability index 
measurement (Dpk). This capability measure is used to provide a quantitative assessment of process understanding 
and risk. Table 9.8 shows an example of risk associated with each CQA.
Table 9.8: Example of Risk Associated with Each Critical Quality Attribute
Low Risk Critical

A low risk CQA is associated with a process with a comparable process capability greater than 1.33 and a lower 
confidence assurance of a process capability greater than 1.0. This indicates good confidence that the process for 
that CQA is statistically robust.
In contrast, a high risk CQA is associated with a process that has demonstrated either marginal capability (i.e., 
capability less than 1.0) or has insufficient data collected to assure with reasonable confidence that the process is 
robust.
After the three-batch Stage 2.2 PPQ assessment for the 80 ft3 bin scale-up, the CU attribute displayed a Pa > 
99.9999% (Dpk > 2.0) and a Pal of 96.13% (Dpk-low = 0.69). This indicates that the process should be quite robust, but 
insufficient evidence is available to conclude this with high certainty.
It was thus decided that a comprehensive statistical monitoring assessment would be completed after two additional 
commercial batches were produced (total of five batches) to re-evaluate the product risk. The philosophy here is that 
compiling data from a few more batches will substantially increase the confidence in the overall process robustness. 
A summary of the CU data is provided in Figure 9.8 and Table 9.9.

10	 Appendix 2 – NDA Products Case Studies:
	
Small Molecules
10.1	
Case Study 1: Selection of CQAs and PPs for CPV – Applying Risk-based Approach 
to CPV Determination to a New Small Molecule Drug Product, Utilizing Data from 
the ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 2 – Illustrative Example [35]
The ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 2 – Illustrative Example [35] describes the model product PaQLInol Tablets [8]. Based 
on the data and development knowledge presented in the PQLI Guide, a science- and risk-based approach is used 
to determine which subset of CQAs and parameters are subject to heightened monitoring and sampling in Stage 3 
(CPV). In this case, heightened monitoring is the same or at a similar level of monitoring and sampling as conducted 
in Stage 2, which is increased or enhanced compared to the eventual routine situation.
The flowchart shown in Figure 10.1 outlines the decision-making process.
Figure 10.1: Heightened Monitoring Assessment Decision Making Process [35]
This assessment is performed separately for each CQA of the output drug product or drug substance.
For each CQA, questions (Steps 1 to 4 in Figure 10.1) are applied taking into account formulation and process 
development studies, as well as manufacturing information obtained from using this formula and process (e.g., 
manufacture of clinical batches or scale-up/technology transfer batches). Data generated during Stage 2 is also 
reviewed.
If sufficient data has been collected to demonstrate control and capability, and the impact of all sources of variability is 
understood, these CQAs do not need to be included in a heightened monitoring and testing plan during CPV.

The risk assessment criteria applied during development studies shows that the impact on all drug product CQAs is 
Low risk when a comprehensive control strategy is applied.
As part of Step 2 in building a CPV program, the residual risk in the control strategy is evaluated.
The residual risk assessment, using a different risk assessment tool more suitable for the evaluation of routine 
manufacturing (e.g., the FMECA risk ranking guide given in Appendix 3 of ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 2 – Illustrative 
Example [8]), confirms that PaQLInol Tablets have a Low level of residual risk, leading to an appropriate number 
of batches, for example three, being manufactured satisfactorily to support Stage 2.2. For further discussion, see 
Section 5.7 of ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 2 – Illustrative Example [8].
Introduction of Real Time Release Testing (RTRT), as proposed in ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 2 – Illustrative Example 
[8], requires continued monitoring during the initial part of Stage 3 (Stage 3.1), at the same level as during Stage 2.2.
In Step 2 of building a CPV program, the residual risk in the control strategy is evaluated. Theoretically, all of the unit 
operations have been mitigated to the predicted green or low-level risk for each unit operation’s impact on each CQA, 
but low variability across a wide range of input material attributes in combination with operational conditions may not 
have been demonstrated.
Regarding robustness, there could be some residual risk. For the purpose of this example, the focus is on the impact 
of compression on dissolution and CU. A summary of the control strategy risk assessment for UDU and dissolution 
after Stage 2 is shown in Table 10.4, leading to propose the PV Stage 3 monitoring plan elements.

dependent on:
-	 Drug substance particle size
-	 Specific surface area of lubricant
-	 Lubrication mixing time
- 	 Tablet crushing force
•	 Model developed at small scale and limited 
confirmation during Stage 2
•	 Stratified sampling applied for testing of tablet cores
Control strategy established and confirmed
Consideration for Stage 3.1 (based on decision 
process Step 2 and control strategy)
Continue stratified sampling at the same level as 
above for a defined number of batches, as well as 
measurement of material attributes of drug substance 
particle size, specific surface area of lubricant and 
tablet crushing force, and the process parameter of 
lubrication time. Maintain end product testing. Verify 
multivariate model and demonstrate robustness, and 
provide comparative data between prediction and 
measurement to support movement to RTRT.

•

There are two raw materials used in PaQLInol Tablets with the potential to have a significant impact on dose 
uniformity and dissolution. Based on prior knowledge, it was theorized that API particle size could have significant 
impact on content and dose uniformity; however, tablet strength and testing during development have provided 
enough data to downgrade this risk to a Low level (see Table 10.5).
Conversely, due to the feed forward control strategy model, limited development data, and the desire to eliminate 
routine testing of dissolution as part of batch release testing, the CQA of dissolution is considered an excellent 
candidate for heightened testing as part of a CPV plan.
The variability of within batch results should be considered in determining the number of samples tested and 
sampling locations for each batch.
Although the powder blend is designed to be free-flowing, variability is estimated during early phases of production 
with different batches of excipients. Continued monitoring of dissolution along with material attributes and process 
parameters impacting dissolution is justified during the first part of CPV to confirm that the model and predictions are 
acceptable.
10.1.4	 Step 4 – Assess Operational Practices Impact
The following operational practices could impact CU or dissolution of product:
•
Multiple operators
•
Multiple shifts
•
Multiple equipment sets
•
Process interruptions (breaks, lunches, weekends, equipment malfunctions, etc.)
A review of the level of process understanding and the design of the control strategy for PaQLInol Tablets indicates 
that these factors are very low risk. For example, for UDU, there are many control strategy elements that are 
operator-independent, such as in-line monitoring of blend homogeneity linked either to an automatic system to 
stop blending, or an easy-to-read revolution counter. Homogeneity is confirmed after the lubrication unit operation; 
therefore, reduced testing is justified during Stage 3 CPV.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

Decision Process Risk Assessment Summary
Dissolution is largely dependent on three material attribute properties for PaQLInol Tablets: drug substance particle 
size, magnesium stearate surface area, and tablet crushing force, and one process parameter, lubrication time.
Operators can have an impact on the equipment set up and the resulting process parameters and crushing force. 
Although the powder blend is designed to be free-flowing, variability is estimated during early phases of production 
because of the different excipient batches used and use of operations personnel.
Continued monitoring of dissolution plus material attributes and process parameters impacting dissolution is justified 
during the first part of CPV to confirm that the model and predictions are acceptable.

10.1.5	 Process Validation Stage 3.1 Monitoring Plan Summary and Criteria
Criteria could be set, such as a defined confidence interval of process capability (e.g., Cpk > 1, with 99% confidence 
per USP <905> utilizing greater than 30 samples [46]) that, when achieved, would drive a review to decide if and 
what testing could be reduced to the routine monitoring level, and which elements of the control strategy could be 
considered for change.

10.1.6	 Proposed Routine Monitoring Program
When the defined capability measures are achieved as part of Stage 3.1 CPV, the levels of testing may be adjusted 
to statistically-appropriate routine levels, which in the case of dissolution, involves RTRT. This level of testing is 
applied as part of the process performance and product quality monitoring system, which is likely to lead to the 
application of statistical evaluation, for example, capability. If RTRT is implemented, frequent monitoring of some of 
the direct process measurements, such as blender revolutions, should be considered.
Criteria could be set, such as a defined confidence interval of process capability (e.g., Cpk > 1, with 99% confidence), 
which when achieved, would drive a review to decide if and what testing could be reduced to the routine monitoring 
level, and what elements of the control strategy could be considered for change.
10.2	
Case Study 2: Batch Size Reduction Process Performance Qualification
This case study describes the PPQ for reducing the batch size for an oral suspension drug product from the qualified 
batch size of 1892 L (499.8 US gal) to 946 L (249.9 US gal). This represents a 50% batch size reduction, which will 
allow greater flexibility with respect to product demand.
The manufacturing process for 946 L batch size consists of blending pre-weighed quantities of the formulation 
ingredients in a stainless steel process vessel according to the master batch record. A flow diagram depicting the 
compounding process is provided in Figure 10.2. Following QC release, the tank containing the blended formulation 
is transferred to a multi-head filling line where the blended product is dispensed into plastic containers.

10.2.1	 Prerequisite Work
Prior to PPQ, a number of prerequisite activities were completed, including engineering studies to determine the 
blending process design space, and risk assessments to determine the criticality of each process step. A schematic 
overview of the batch rescale plan is provided in Figure 10.3.
Figure 10.3: Oral Suspension Drug Product Batch Size Rescale Plan
The engineering study consisted of operating the blender at a variety of mixing speeds and times to determine the 
optimal parameter settings and the limits at which acceptable product can be produced.
The experimental design also included one center point run in order to detect any curvature in the process response. 
Samples drawn from the center point run were placed on stability. Figure 10.4 is a contour plot of assay versus 
mix time and speed. In this case study, the knowledge space with respect to assay, mix time, and mixing speed 
is represented by the entire graph. The design space is defined as the area in the graph representing acceptable 
assay results. The design space required to achieve batch homogeneity in this example was defined as mixing times 
greater than 25 minutes and mixing speeds greater than 125 rpm.

A cross-functional team consisting of representatives from manufacturing, engineering, QC chemistry, and validation

diagram for product assay is provided in Figure 10.5.
Figure 10.5: Ishikawa Diagram for Oral Suspension Drug Product Assay

according to a defined risk priority scheme. The prioritization (Risk Priority Number – RPN) assigned to each failure 
mode was then used in conjunction with the risk associated with each CQA to determine an overall risk level (Table 
10.9).

After successfully completing the prerequisite engineering studies and risk assessments, and assigning appropriate 
statistical risk levels for each CQA, the process was deemed ready for PPQ. The PPQ of the new batch size had the 
specific objectives to:
•
Verify that the blending procedure as described in the production batch record using a 1000 L process vessel
was adequate to provide a uniform product
•
Verify that the filling equipment/procedure was appropriate to provide uniform content in the filled bottles to
support the label claim
•
Verify that the finished product consistently met requirements specified in the finished product specifications
10.2.2	 Validation Methodology
A minimum of three validation batches or runs were executed for PPQ. The PPQ study was performed in two phases:
Phase 1 established the preliminary estimate of the process capability. During Phase 1, no PV batches were 
released until a long-term process capability (Ppk) of at least 1.0 was demonstrated for each CQA. Upon successful 
completion of the PPQ Phase 1 study, an interim report was written and approved allowing release of the PPQ 
batches to distribution. A minimum of three consecutive successful Phase 1 PPQ batches needed be completed prior 
to graduating to PPQ Phase 2.
In Phase 2, PPQ batches were released for distribution; however bulk and finished product sampling and testing 
continued at PPQ-established levels until the lower capability confidence interval bound calculated for each CQA was 
demonstrated to be at or above 1.0 and a state of statistical control demonstrated.

Appendix 2
10.2.3	 Sampling Plan Rationale
The sample size chosen for finished product testing in any individual validation batch or run is eight. This sample 
size is based on the acceptable assay range of 4.50 – 5.50 mg/mL and worst-case standard deviation of 0.13 mg/mL 
obtained from engineering study bulk-product test data. The sample size was calculated using the one-sample t test 
sample size formula:
s
n = (tα/2 + tβ)2 (__)
2
δ
Where:
n = Required sample size
α = Risk of rejecting a true hypothesis
β = Risk of accepting a false hypothesis
s = Sample standard deviation (historic)
δ = Difference to be detected
The α and β risk values used in the sample size calculation were obtained from the PFMEA. According to the 
PFMEA, the highest RPN value for any process step in the manufacture of the oral suspension drug product following 
mitigation is 45. Any maximum RPN value within 26 – 50 indicates that the sampling plan chosen must provide a 
minimum confidence (1-α) of 0.95 and a minimum test power (1-β) of 0.99.
The sample size calculated provides 95% confidence (α = .05) that true assay differences of 0.25 mg/mL or greater 
will be detected with a probability of at least 99% (β = .01). The 0.25 mg/mL assay difference is approximately 1/4 of 
the acceptable range for the API content in the oral suspension drug product.
The number of validation runs was determined based on the number of runs required to demonstrate a lower 
capability confidence interval bound (1-α = 0.95) greater than or equal to 1.0. The minimum number of runs in any 
case was not less than three.
The confidence level (1-α) used to calculate the capability confidence interval was based on the output of the PFMEA 
previously developed for the oral suspension drug product manufacturing process. According to the PFMEA, the 
maximum RPN value determined for a process step following mitigation is 36. Any maximum RPN value within 26 – 
50 indicates that the long-term process capability calculated must provide a minimum confidence (1-α) of 0.95.
Upon successful completion of the PPQ Phase 2 study, a final report summarizing the protocol testing and results 
for both Phase 1 and Phase 2 was written and approved qualifying the use of the 1000 L process vessel for 946 L of 
the oral suspension drug product. Approval of the PPQ final report indicated that sampling and testing of subsequent 
product batches at normal production levels as indicated in the master batch record was appropriate.
Assay data for the finished drug product is provided in Table 10.11. Capability statistics were used to assess the 
performance of the process.
The capability index chosen was the Ppk, which is calculated based on the overall process variation. Because of 
the limited amount of data available at the beginning of PV, the standard 3-sigma tolerance band used to calculate 
capability was replaced with a tolerance factor multiple of sigma calculated to cover the desired population proportion 
at the given confidence level.
The value of the tolerance factor (k2) was determined based on the total number of data available from all batches 
collectively. The value for k2 was updated at the completion of each batch to reflect the additional data available. 
Figure 10.6 contour plot shows the PV assay results depicted graphically.

As can be seen in Figure 10.6, k2 decreases asymptotically as more PV batches are added to the data set. This 
decrease is roughly mirrored by the simultaneous increase in Ppk and the LCCB. The gap between the capability 
estimate and the LCCB also decreases as the chart progresses to the right. This narrowing is the result of increasing 
sample size as more PPQ runs are added, which in turn yields improved estimates of Ppk.
Additionally, Figure 10.6 shows that assay capability reached the minimum capability criterion at PPQ run 6. To 
demonstrate that significant variability estimates had been achieved, additional runs were monitored at the PPQ-
established sampling and testing levels until the LCCB also rose above the minimum capability criterion. At run 11, 
the LCCB rose above the minimum capability criterion, thus completing the second PPQ phase.

Appendix 3
11	 Appendix 3 – NDA Products Case Studies: 
	
Large Molecules or Bio-derived Products
11.1	
Case Study 1: Large Molecule Example Column Chromatography Step – 	
Parameter Selection for CPV [35]
The greatest challenge for demonstrating continuation of the validated state for a biological product (i.e., CPV) is 
often the limited amount of data that a manufacturer has at the commercial scale prior to the commencement of both 
PPQ and CPV.
Since biological manufacturing processes are typically very expensive and time consuming, sponsors will often run 
a limited number of engineering batches at the commercial scale prior to the initiation of PPQ, and may complete a 
relatively low number of commercial batches before submitting a regulatory application. FDA suggests [3]:
“…continued monitoring and sampling of process parameters and quality attributes at the level established 
during the Process Qualification stage until sufficient data are available to generate significant variability 
estimates…”
It is clear that there is regulatory concern about reducing the testing plan prematurely (i.e., with a lack of statistical 
confidence in the process).
That said, there are a considerable number of parameters assessed during the PPQ exercise that are not subject to 
variability estimates. They may be subject to some type of trending, but it was determined that SPC methods are not 
needed because the results are below an analytical method’s Limit of Quantitation and/or Limit of Detection, or they 
are not subject to Normal distribution, or are Pass/Fail results.
In addition, most statistical tools require a minimum number of batches before trend limits/control limits/variability 
estimates can be applied. This creates a bit of “grey area” between PPQ and when a manufacturer can objectively 
apply statistical tools for assessing maintenance of the validated state.
In light of the comments above, Table 11.1 shows an example of a bridging strategy between PPQ and CPV. The 
parameters included in the table represent those parameters that were defined in the control strategy as requiring 
inclusion in the PPQ exercise (including all critical operating parameters, IPCs, In-Process Acceptance Criteria 
(IPAC), and additional sampling).
From that starting point, parameters were assessed for whether or not they needed to be included in the formal CPV 
effort. The Action/Rationale column contains a justification for the decision reached by a multi-functional project team.
Just as it is important during PPQ to state clearly that “All parameters matter, but some more than others,” so it 
is with CPV, where it should be understood that “All parameters matter, but only a subset are subject to statistical 
assessment and/or trending.”
It may be unnecessary to trend certain parameters during CPV, but that does not mean they are insignificant to 
demonstrating the validated state. It just means that there are existing quality systems that should be leveraged to 
ensure the validated state.
While specific parameter determinations will vary by sponsor, this example of product “AEX” should provide some 
guidance with regard to the types of discussion necessary prior to the initiation of the CPV program.

The four parameters identified as requiring inclusion in the formal CPV exercise were:
•	
Eluate endotoxin
•	
Eluate bioburden
•	
Step yield
•	
Eluate purity by RP-HPLC
Data from 10 batches (including 2 representative engineering batches as well as the 3 PPQ batches) were collected 
and assessed on an ongoing basis, but summarized for the purpose of this example. It is assumed that testing/
monitoring would be ongoing until the defined number of batches is achieved, at which time the CPV acceptance 
criteria may be reassessed.
Eluate endotoxin is a routine IPAC with an associated alert limit of ≥ 5 EU/mL and an action limit of ≥ 10 EU/mL.
Eluate bioburden is a routine IPAC that has an associated alert limit of ≥ 10 CFU/10 mL and an action limit of ≥ 50 
CFU/10 mL.
As stated in Table 11.2, neither eluate endotoxin nor bioburden are subject to Normal distribution since results are 
frequently at or near the limit of detection for the respective analytical methods. Consequently, the data cannot be 
control charted or subjected to Cpk, but they can be tabulated and subjectively assessed for observable trends.
While there is a defined quality system in place that would identify an IPAC failure, for example a deviation 
investigation, such an investigation may not include an assessment for data trending upward toward the alert or 
action limit. In this case, it is valuable to include this parameter in the formal CPV assessment to ensure that such 
a trend would be detected and any problems with associated systems like environmental control, water quality, and 
equipment cleaning could be identified early and addressed prior to impacting product quality.
From the individual data sets in Table 11.2, the conclusion could be drawn after each lot that there are no observable 
trends; therefore, the process is in a state of control with regard to AEX eluate endotoxin and bioburden.

Lot 005 was investigated to assess the out-of-control limit result. While the step yield met the defined IPAC of ≥ 75%, 
the result of 76% was below the calculated lower control limit. Upon investigation, it was determined that an operator 
had switched over to the waste fraction prematurely on the back side of the main peak collection (Target: 0.2 AU; 
Actual: 0.5 AU).
Since this out-of-control value was explained by operator error, the resulting step yield value meets the defined IPAC, 
and the out-of-control result is not attributable to inherent process variability, there is no impact to the validated state 
and no further action is required.
Inclusion of the data point artificially inflates the calculated control limits, which would no longer represent the true 
process variability. Additional data is presented in Table 11.4 and in the revised control chart in Figure 11.2.

Appendix 3
11.2	
Case Study 2: Process Validation of a Cell Culture Product
Using a cell culture production bioreactor step as an example, this case study describes the risk assessment and 
process characterization studies performed to define the control strategy, confirm the control strategy during PPQ, 
and conduct the CPV program. The description of the drug substance “MabX” validation illustrates that the process is 
in a state of control.
The manufacturing process of MabX is shown in Figure 11.4.
Figure 11.4: MabX Manufacturing Process Flow Diagram
11.2.1	
MabX Process Design and Characterization
11.2.1.1	 Process Control Strategy Development Overview
The MabX manufacturing process and control strategy was developed based on initial process development studies, 
clinical manufacturing experience, process characterization (robustness and range finding) studies, and process and 
product risk assessments.
Product risk assessments were carried out to identify CQAs. In addition, process risk assessments were conducted 
to identify high and medium risk parameters, and to help select parameters for investigation and modeling process 
characterization studies.
Process characterization studies included multiple variables using a statistical DoE approach. The CQAs were 
monitored as appropriate during these studies, and individual parameters within unit operations were identified that 
impact CQAs, titer, and yield.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 152 =====
Page 150	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Appendix 3	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
The results were used to classify the process parameters and define the process control strategy. Process 
parameters, including ranges tested in development and the control ranges for GMP manufacturing, were 
documented in Process Parameter Reports (PPRs) for both the cell culture (upstream) and the purification 
(downstream) processes. The PPRs were approved prior to initiation of the PPQ campaign.
After the PPQ campaign, additional structure-activity-relationship and process characterization studies were 
completed. The product and process risk assessments were revised to incorporate knowledge gained from these 
studies and the PPQ studies. The PPRs were updated with revised parameter classifications and control ranges.
11.2.1.2	 Risk Assessment Overview
Risk assessments were carried out throughout the product development lifecycle of MabX in order to increase 
assurance of patient safety, product efficacy, and manufacturing consistency.
The methodology employed for product risk assessment and determination of MabX CQAs involved ranking the 
potential impact of each quality attribute on potency, efficacy, and/or safety of the product, and the certainty with 
which the impact was understood at the time of the risk analysis.
Numerical scoring systems for the “Impact Factor” and “Uncertainty Factor” were developed and used to determine 
the overall risk score, the RPN. The RPN determined whether an attribute was classified as a CQA or a non-
CQA. Following identification of the initial CQAs of MabX, two process risk assessments were conducted on the 
manufacturing process focusing on those parameters that impact CQAs and process consistency.
In the initial process risk assessment, prior knowledge of the process, including development and manufacturing 
history, was used to identify high and medium risk parameters and to select parameters for subsequent process 
characterization studies.
The information acquired from those studies, plus knowledge gained from manufacturing experience and structure-
activity-relationship studies, provided the basis for a second assessment to evaluate process risk.
For parameters that had high or medium risk after the second assessment, the process control strategy was adjusted 
to mitigate their risk, which included adjustment of control ranges or addition of IPCs or tests.
11.2.1.3	 Process Characterization
Prior to PPQ, process characterization studies were carried out on each unit operation of the MabX process. These 
consisted of DoE process modeling studies, column lifetime studies, centrifuge and ultrafiltration robustness studies, 
column elution fractionation studies, intermediate hold time studies, and solution lot record studies.
An initial process risk assessment was conducted to determine which parameters would be further investigated in 
process characterization studies for the cell culture process (summarized in Table 11.6).
Some additional parameters identified as medium risk during the initial risk assessment (such as total antifoam 
addition and batched medium hold time) were studied in a univariate fashion in order to support action limits in the 
manufacturing process. These parameters are not included in Table 11.6.
For production bioreactor characterization, effects on product quality were assessed by clarifying the cell culture 
harvest using bench top centrifugation and 0.2 µm filtration, followed by purification using Protein A chromatography.
For the production bioreactor, a 3 L scale-down model (same functions and design as the full-size production 
bioreactor) was used for the process characterization experiments, which were performed using a DoE strategy. 
The statistical design and the relationship between the input and output parameters examined during process 
characterization are provided in Table 11.7.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

In the production bioreactor step, three parameters, namely temperature, pH, and dissolved oxygen, were found to 
impact the product glycoforms (especially Man5, G0F, and G1F), product purity levels, and charge isoforms.
Cell culture duration was identified as a low-risk parameter during the initial process risk assessment; however, 
subsequent experiments showed an increase in acidic isoforms and oxidation with an increase in culture duration. 
These parameters were classified as critical parameters and appropriate action limits were set.
The set points and action limits for pH and culture duration varied from the manufacturing set points used in the 
previous clinical campaign, but the changes were justified by the improved robustness on the PQ attributes.

11.2.1.4	 Define Process Control Strategy
Based on the risk assessment and results from process characterization studies, the control strategy for the MabX 
process was determined, including the identification of key controlled parameters and critical controlled parameters, 
along with IPCs and In-Process Tests (IPTs).
Process parameters for each unit operation were first categorized as input parameters or output parameters. Input 
parameters were then classified as critical, key, or non-key based on the process characterization data. (Note: these 
are company-specific terms.)
A critical controlled parameter is an input process parameter that impacts product quality within a unit operation 
and may also affect process performance. Critical controlled parameters have operating ranges and action limits. 
Exceeding the action limit for a critical controlled parameter triggers the filing of a deviation.
A key controlled parameter is an input process parameter that may affect process performance (e.g., yield), but is 
unlikely to affect product quality. Key controlled parameters have operating ranges and action limits. Exceeding the 
action limit of a key controlled parameter triggers the filing of a deviation.
A non-key controlled parameter is an input process parameter that is unlikely to impact either the process 
performance or product quality. Non-Key controlled parameters have operating ranges.
Output parameters were classified as IPCs*, IPTs**, or In Process Measurements (IPMs). IPCs and IPTs were further 
classified as critical or not. Each output was monitored during PPQ along with its action limits.
*IPCs and **IPTs are steps and adjustments that occur during the production process to ensure that the process
remains in control.
The process controls for the production bioreactor step are presented in Tables 11.8 to 11.10.
The rationale for the categorization of key controlled parameters and critical controlled parameters for the production 
bioreactor is summarized in Table 11.8.
The action limits for these key controlled parameters and critical controlled parameters were assigned based 
on process characterization studies and the resulting statistical models. The action limits for critical controlled 
parameters were set to achieve the proposed specification for charge heterogeneity and product purity in the MabX 
drug substance, as well as to maintain adequate control of oxidation and glycosylation.

Appendix 3
11.2.2	 MabX PPQ – Campaign Summary
Manufacturing MabX PPQ was performed in a large-scale facility that consists of three equivalent bioreactor trains 
and two equivalent purification suites.
Each bioreactor train contains four seed train bioreactors (60 L, 235 L, 950 L, and 3,750 L) and two equivalent 15,000 L 
production bioreactors. The harvest equipment is located in the cell culture area. The purification suites and rooms 
have chromatography, viral inactivation, viral filtration, and ultrafiltration equipment.
The MabX PPQ campaign consisted of five batches planned and designated as PPQ batches on equivalent 
bioreactor Trains 2 and 3, Harvest Area 2, and Purification Suite 1 as shown in Table 11.11.
Three runs were performed in bioreactor Train 3, utilizing production bioreactor T3-BR-1. Two additional runs were 
produced in bioreactor Train 2, in production bioreactors T2-BR-1 and T2-BR-2, respectively. The five PPQ runs 
successfully qualified bioreactor Trains 2 and 3, and production bioreactors T3-BR-1, T2-BR-1 and TR-BR-2 for 
commercial manufacturing of MabX.

Process consistency validation was completed for each step in the manufacturing process. An approved protocol 
defined the scope of each process consistency validation exercise and the acceptance criteria, which were based 
on the ability to maintain controlled parameters within allowable ranges and to produce outputs that reflected the 
appropriate performance of the unit operations.
The acceptance criteria for MabX process consistency validation protocols are as follows:
•	
All critical controlled parameters, CIPTs, and Critical IPCs (CIPCs) that fail to meet the action limits and/or ranges 
must be assessed and determined to not impact the consistent performance of the unit operation and the validity 
of the study.
•	
Key controlled parameters, IPTs, and IPCs that fail to meet the action limits and/or ranges must be assessed and 
determined to not impact the consistent performance of the unit operation and the validity of the study.
•	
As specified in the Process VMP, consecutive batches are required to demonstrate consistency and/or 
reproducibility. Non-consecutive batches separated by failed batches whose failure can be attributed to a non 
process-related cause (e.g., operator error) are acceptable.
•	
Critical, major, and minor manufacturing and testing deviations that occur during the execution of this unit 
operation must be listed in the final report and determined to have no impact the validity of the study.
•	
Any validation protocol exceptions must be described in the final report and determined to have no impact on the 
validity of the study.

•
Any failure of the drug substance to meet product quality release testing requirements specified in the Certificate
of Analysis (CoA) or release requirements specified in the certificate of conformance must be shown to be
unrelated to the unit operation being validated.
The key controlled parameters, critical controlled parameters, CIPTs, IPTs, CIPCs, and IPCs were monitored for the 
production bioreactor steps from all of the process consistency validation campaign batches. All five batches were 
successfully completed and met all product quality specifications.
Based on the acceptance criteria outlined in the validation protocol, the production bioreactor step was validated on 
bioreactor Trains 2 and 3 and production bioreactors T2-BR-1, T2-BR-3, and T3-BR-1. All controlled parameters, 
IPCs, and IPTs were maintained within the action limits. Upon investigation, any manufacturing or testing deviations 
and exceptions to the protocol did not impact the validity of the study.
11.2.3	 MabX CPV
After successful PPQ and prior to the commercial manufacturing campaign, the CPV plan is established, which 
defines control parameters, IPTs, and IPCs, and drug substance to be monitored and trended.
Figure 11.5: Continued Process Verification Strategy
Variables monitored in the CPV plan consist of the minimum variables, including:
•
All release assays for drug substance, CIPCs, and CIPTs
•
Critical controlled parameters and CMAs (if defined)
•
Overall and step yields and cell culture titers
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

Appendix 3
In addition, the risk-based variables are generated from the risk assessment and monitored for a defined period. The 
CPV plan can be updated for the addition or removal of these variables with appropriate justifications documented. 
Risk-based variables include:
•
Variables monitored during PPQ (excluding minimum variables)
•
Variables monitored for the at-scale verification of the resin lifetime of chromatography steps
•
Parameters and attributes affected by the changes of sites, process, assay, critical raw material, and equipment
Trending methods and rules for detecting signals are defined for the variables in the CPV plan. Run charts with 
specifications and action limits will be used in CPV Stage 3.1, during which limited batches (typically < 30) have been 
manufactured. Statistical Control Limits (SCL) will be established at the end of Stage 3.1 or after the required sample 
size is achieved.
Control charts will be used in the CPV Stage 3.2 for variables with SCL. Run charts can be continued for variables 
without SCL in the CPV Stage 3.2.
If a signal (within specification or action limits) is detected from CPV monitoring, it will be assessed for risk to the 
product or process. An investigation may be initiated to identify the root causes, and corrective and preventive actions 
assigned, if necessary, to implement process changes or process improvements.
Implementation of the CPV plan ensures that MabX manufacturing is in a state of control during commercial 
production.

Appendix 4
12	 Appendix 4 – Contract Manufacturing 
Product Case Studies
The following case studies are examples of real products that have been manufactured and/or developed at a 
CDMPO. Some details have been changed to maintain confidentiality. These examples are meant to highlight some 
scenarios that may occur when the best practices described in Chapter 7 are followed.
12.1	
Case Study 1: Insufficient Information Transfer in Design
Product A is a generic product purchased by a virtual company who contracts out manufacture and packaging. This 
product is a non-sterile solution that is sold over the counter. The company has previously executed batch records 
and a copy of the original new drug application filing, but no other information. There was no CDO or CPO involved in 
this process.
The CMO did not have a standard minimum amount of data required to continue with manufacturing but did have a 
requirement to generate a risk assessment if parameter criticality had not been defined. The client wanted to proceed 
directly into PPQ without engineering or clinical runs. The original filing did not list critical parameters but included a 
copy of an unexecuted batch record. The original equipment used for manufacturing was not known.
The CMO generated a batch record using their template with the parameters defined in the batch record provided by 
the client. The CMO performed a risk assessment (using an FMEA approach) to identify potential risks in the process 
using the equipment owned by the CMO. It was determined that the mixer used by the CMO was not capable of 
mixing the product at the speed previously utilized, but all other parameters could be met. The mixer was not capable 
of reaching the highest speed parameters.
The CMO generated a scaled-down control strategy that outlined the CQAs and CPPs defined in the risk assessment 
and evaluated all materials. During this process, it was determined that one raw material was not pharmaceutical 
grade. The CMO recommended that a higher-grade raw material be purchased and that a mixing study be performed 
using the onsite equipment. Both recommendations were accepted and the mixing study involved mixing the product 
at a lower speed, but identified that significant foaming occurred.
Due to the potential impact of dose accuracy with foaming product, the CMO recommended that an engineering batch 
be performed prior to starting PPQ. The client accepted the recommendation and the engineering batch confirmed 
that the product was able to be dosed accurately using the process that included the mixing study parameters.
While there was not a significant amount of manufacturing data available, the process was very simple and only had 
three critical parameters defined that were well controlled; therefore, three PPQ batches were manufactured. All three 
batches met expectations and a CPV plan was generated by the CMO.
During administration of the CPV program, a trend was identified (via Nelson rule 2, nine or more points in a row on 
the same side as the mean) and investigated. It was determined that the trend was due to variability in an excipient 
that did not impact the ability of the product to meet all acceptance criteria. No additional actions were required; 
however, the raw material vendor was notified and able to tighten controls on their process, which eliminated the 
trend.
The completion of the PPQ report took three months longer than expected to initially qualify due to the addition of the 
engineering batch and mixing study, but resulted in a consistent manufacturing process, acceptable PPQ study, and 
an easily managed CPV plan.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Appendix 4	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
12.2	
Case Study 2: Allowing Flexibility
Product B is a new parenteral monoclonal antibody that is manufactured, filled, and packaged into a syringe with a 
safety device at one client location. The development of this product was performed at the client’s site. The addition 
of a CMPO site was a risk mitigation strategy as the existing site did not have sufficient capacity. The company 
provided drug substance and fill/finish batch records from their internal site as well as a QTPP but did not share the 
development reports. There was no CDO involved in this process.
The CMO generated a site-specific control strategy and risk assessment to define site-specific parameter criticality. 
The client agreed to manufacturing engineering batches for both the drug substance and final product at the CMO. 
There were differences in the equipment used at each site and some differences in manufacturing parameters as a 
result.
The CMO generated a batch record using their template with the parameters defined in the batch record provided 
by the client. The language in several of the steps was modified to match the client’s documentation and the order 
of some steps were changed. The engineering batches did not produce the expected results as the operators made 
several errors. The root cause was determined to be confusion related to the changes in the batch record. The batch 
record was revised to ensure the process was the same as the transfer site but aligned the flow and language to 
the routine process and format used by the CMO. An additional engineering batch was manufactured and met all 
expectations.
A PPQ protocol was developed using the sampling strategy defined by the client. After the engineering runs were 
performed, a new sampling tool was introduced. Due to the concern that the sampling mechanism was new, hands 
on training was developed and delivered jointly by the CMO and client SMEs. An engineering run using a surrogate 
was manufactured challenging the ability to sample effectively. It was determined that one set of samples could not 
be taken in the time frames specified and the protocol was adjusted.
There was a significant amount of process data available from the alternate manufacturing site and the changes 
made during transfer were not considered significant; therefore, three PPQ batches were manufactured. All three 
batches met expectations and a CPV plan was generated by the CMO. This CPV plan contained a formal mechanism 
to compare data from both sites.
During administration of the CPV program, the performance from the two sites was found to be slightly, but 
statistically, different with respect to yield. It was determined that this was due to variability in a processing time that 
did not impact the ability of the product to meet all acceptance criteria. The maximum time in the bioreactor was the 
same for both sites, but one of the sites worked on all shifts, and therefore was able to transfer earlier than the other 
site, which only had a single shift. The site with the single shift changed their process to allow earlier transfer when 
endpoint was reached and improved their yield.
The PPQ report was completed one month earlier than planned. A formal RACI and stage gate process was in place, 
which provided opportunities for effective information flow between the two teams. Due to the expectation that site 
differences could result in performance of multiple engineering batches, a contingency plan for four engineering 
batches was originally designed into the schedule to ensure that the filing date would not be impacted if unexpected 
data was received.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Ranking Characteristics to Assess Risk – An ASTM E2475 Based Approach [42]
The knowledge and decisions from PV Stage 1 are compared to ranking characteristics to determine the relative risk 
ranking for product, process, and control strategy. Tables 13.1 to 13.2 below provide examples of characteristics used 
to guide the ranking assignment for these three elements, respectively.
13.1.1	
Evaluation of Product Knowledge
The evaluation of product knowledge focuses on severity of harm to the patient and probability that variability has an 
impact on the safety, efficacy, and quality of the product.
The risk ranking level is assigned based on an evaluation of methodology applied to identify CQAs and an evaluation 
of how well the impact of variability is understood.
