Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
13.1.2	 Evaluation of Process Understanding
13.1.2.1	 Development Phase
For new products, establishing process understanding should include exploration of the relationship between 
variability of process parameters, material characteristics, and other sources of variability and the effect on CQAs. For 
new drug products developed under QbD principles, predictive models of these relationships may be available.
Development and clinical batches provide preliminary estimates of process variability and capability. Criticality 
levels for process parameters are derived from development, characterization, and manufacturing experience (pilot, 
demonstration, and clinical batches).
For legacy products, full-scale manufacturing data may be useful in establishing the effects of input variations on 
product attributes.
13.1.2.2	 Prior Knowledge
In many cases as new products are developed and commercialized, the manufacturing platform chosen for the 
commercial process is familiar to the firm. The process technology is likely to have been used to manufacture other 
products and considerable knowledge has been realized. This knowledge base can be drawn on to make estimates 
of the expected performance of similar processes being developed and validated.
Inherent process variability can be estimated from this prior knowledge, including experiments and knowledge 
of similar products and processes. This, along with consideration for variability the process will experience due 
to process operators and shifts, process interruptions, hold times, multiple equipment sets, and multiple material 
suppliers, can also help define the number of validation batches that should be planned. If the variability from any of 
the sources is high, or knowledge about the impact or range of the variability is low, then the risk of demonstrating 
process reproducibility is increased.
The risk ranking level is assigned from an evaluation of the degree of process understanding, process predictability, 
and understanding of effect of scale as well as effect of variability on process output.

Rankings are aligned with definitions and characterizations provided in ASTM E2475, Standard Guide for Process 
Understanding Related to Pharmaceutical Manufacture and Control [42]. For the purpose of this PV Guide, the 
characterizations in the ASTM publication have been condensed from five to three risk rankings and some aspects 
have been included in the control strategy (Section 13.1.3) evaluation rather than here.
13.1.3	 Evaluation of Control Strategy Effectiveness
The control strategy should be designed based on product and process understanding to ensure that a product of 
required quality can be produced consistently. The evaluations of control strategy effectiveness therefore focus on the 
suitability of the defined control strategies to manage variability.
The risk ranking level is assigned from an evaluation of methodology applied for setting material specifications and 
process controls as well as an evaluation of equipment capability versus process requirements.
Table 13.3 provides an example of characteristics used to guide the ranking assignment. This table is not meant to 
be a comprehensive listing of all the factors of the control strategy for a manufacturing process. Rather it attempts to 
identify those factors most likely to contribute to differences in variability between processes. The overall residual risk 
levels are shown in Table 13.4.

13.2	
Determining the Number of PPQ Batches Based on a Risk-based Approach Utilizing 
the ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 2 – Illustrative Example [8] [34]
To demonstrate the risk-based approach for justifying and determining the appropriate number of Stage 2 PPQ 
batches, a case study based on the ISPE PQLI® Guide Series: Part 2 – Product Realization using Quality by Design 
(QbD): Illustrative Example [8] is presented here. The Illustrative Example describes a thorough development 
approach that results in a low-risk process. For detailed information about designed experiments, design spaces, risk 
assessments done during development including identification of CPPs and material attributes, and relevant elements 
of the proposed control strategy, refer to the ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 2 – Illustrative Example [8].
The example illustrates the development of a tablet formulation, 30 mg (small molecule), called PaQLInol, using the 
enhanced, QbD approach. PaQLInol is a BCS Class 2 compound (low solubility and high permeability). The drug 
product manufacturing process includes the following steps: dispense, blend, lubricate, compress, and coat.
Although the ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 2 – Illustrative Example discusses quality attributes systematically, it focuses on 
the highest risk CQAs, which are dissolution and UDU [8]. This PV Guide concentrates on the dissolution example 
since the risk assessment (Table 13.5) identifies it as the highest risk quality attribute. Table 13.5 shows the cause 
and effect risk assessment matrix for PaQLInol Tablets 30 mg after formulation development and before process 
development.

The process understanding factors are assigned as Medium to Low risk, based on the following arguments 
explained in the ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 2 – Illustrative Example [8]:
•
Causes of interrelationships between variables are identified and explored sufficiently by DoEs to be expressed
in a design space algorithm for dissolution. The algorithm contains factors for:
-
Drug substance particle size
-
Magnesium stearate specific surface area
-
Lubrication time
-
Crushing force of tablets
The ranges assessed in the DoE for these four factors are included in the proposed design space for dissolution 
as acceptable ranges. The largest contribution to the prediction of dissolution values using this algorithm comes 
from drug substance particle size.
•
A design space is proposed.
•
PAT tools are applied during process development (Stage 1) but are not relevant for controlling dissolution.
Dissolution is highly dependent on three material attributes – drug substance particle size, magnesium stearate
surface area, and tablet crushing force, all of which are process- and scale-independent. Lubrication time is
easily determined and controlled.
•
Material-specific CQAs are identified.
•
Effect of scale changes is predictable.
Three process understanding factors are considered to be Medium risk level, one factor is considered Low; 
therefore, the relative risk ranking for process understanding is assigned as Medium.
13.2.2	 Evaluation of Control Strategy
Several control strategy options are considered in the ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 2 – Illustrative Example [8] and four are 
discussed.
One option, a feed forward mechanism, is preferred. In this option, the API Particle Size Distribution (PSD) value is 
obtained from its CoA, the MgSt SSA (Magnesium Stearate Specific Surface Area) value is determined on receipt, 
and assuming a target crushing force of 85 N (Newtons), the lubrication time is calculated based on the algorithm to 
target 90% dissolution.
The dissolution value for release is calculated from the equation using actual lubrication time and crushing force, 
instead of being measured using a QC method. Tables 13.8 and 13.9 summarize this control strategy. A detailed risk 
assessment regarding CPPs is available in the ISPE PQLI® Guide Part 2 – Illustrative Example [8].

Risk ranking for all control strategy factors is assigned as Low risk from the following arguments:
•
Raw materials: critical raw material attributes have been identified as PaQLInol PSD, and magnesium stearate
surface area, and the impact of these critical attributes on dissolution has been explored extensively in
development. This factor is Low risk.
•
Equipment capability versus process requirements: equipment capability is not fully described in the ISPE PQLI®
Guide Part 2 – Illustrative Example [8]. However, process understanding indicates that there is Low risk of impact
of the process on dissolution CQA when a diffusion mixer, and a gravity and power-assisted press are used.
Different equipment of these designs may influence the algorithm, which requires verification for any new piece
of equipment. For the purpose of this example, equipment capability is assumed to meet the requirements of
process parameter control, the major process parameter being lubrication time, which can be set automatically or
be controlled manually, both facile equipment capabilities to achieve. This factor is Low to Medium risk.
•
Experience with process performance to date: although not extensively discussed in the ISPE PQLI® Guide
Part 2 – Illustrative Example [8], the formula and process are used to manufacture batches for Phase III clinical
studies. It is unlikely that the final proposed control strategy will be fully applied for clinical manufacture and the
scale may not be that proposed for commercialization; however, it is assumed that all batches are manufactured
satisfactorily and product complies with CQA acceptance criteria. Scale impact is well understood. This factor is
Low risk.

14	 Appendix 6 – Statistical Detail
14.1	
Operating Characteristic Curves of Sampling Plans and Acceptance Criteria
When selecting sampling plans and acceptance criteria for CPV or blend uniformity and CU, one approach is to 
evaluate the OC curves for Stage 2, Stage 3.2, and the regulatory UDU test requirement [46]. An idealistic depiction 
of this relationship is shown in Figure 14.1.
Figure 14.1: Idealistic Relationships of Operating Characteristic Curves
In Figure 14.1, it is observed that the PPQ OC curve is the most conservative/restrictive, the UDUIdealized test is the 
most liberal, and the OC curve for CPV is in between. Except where the probability of passing is 1 or 0, there is no 
overlap of the OC curves. This is ideal for the following reasons. When PPQ or CPV criteria are met, the batch has 
a 100% probability of passing the UDUIdealized test. Further, for CPV, it is desired that any batch meeting PPQ criteria 
would also meet the CPV criteria. Stated another way, it would be undesirable to pass PPQ only to later fail batches 
of the same quality/variability during Stage 3.
While Figure 14.1 shows the ideal situation, reality is less neat. The manufacturer will need to make trade-offs on how 
conservative the PPQ and CPV OC curves are relative to the UDU test [46] and how conservative PPQ is relative to 
CPV.
If the PPQ criteria are too tight, it will be difficult to meet the criteria while having negligible, if any, practical impact to 
the patient as demonstrated by having no practical impact on the probability of passing the UDU test [46].
For example, if the PPQ OC curve in Figure 6.6 was moved to the left to start coming down at a batch sigma of 2.5% 
label claim, then standard deviations between 2.5% and 5.0% LC, all of which have a 100% probability of passing the 
UDU test [46], would fail PPQ. This would be an unnecessary tightening of the PPQ criteria.
Additionally, to the degree there is overlap of the curves there is risk in both false accept and false rejection of 
batches. Figure 14.2 depicts one set of OC curves that represent a reasonable risk trade-off.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

ISPE Good Practice Guide:

Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Figure 14.2: Relationship of Process Performance Qualification, Ongoing Verification, and USP Operating 
Characteristic Curves
In Figure 14.2, both the PPQ and CPV OC curves are very conservative relative to the USP OC curve. In fact, when 
there is still a 90% probability of passing the USP test [46], there is only a 10% probability of passing the PPQ criteria 
and a 31% probability of passing the CPV criteria.
There is some crossover of the PPQ and CPV curves. However, the crossover covers a relatively small interval of 
batch standard deviations, between 4.3% and 5.0% LC, while having a high probability of passing both criteria, that 
is, > 90%.
Additionally, the risk of failing CPV batches after a successful PPQ of at least three batches is small. This low risk is 
further confirmed in Figure 14.3, which shows the OC curve for passing three consecutive PPQ batches in relation to 
the OC curve for a single PPQ batch and the USP test [46].
Figure 14.3: Operating Characteristic Curves for Passing Three Consecutive PPQ Batches, a Single Process

14.2	
k Value Approximation for Two-Sided Tolerance Interval
culate the k value for Two-Sided Tolerance Intervals (i.e., R, JMP, or Minitab). A very accurate approximation of k 
given by Howe [69] is:
	
	
__________________________
2	
2
	
k = √((n – 1) × (1+1/n) × Z(1-p/2) / Xα,n-1)
Where:
n = sample size
Z = standard normal quantile
p = coverage
α = 1- confidence level
2
Xα,n-1 = chi-square quantile
14.3	
Generation of Acceptance Limit Table for Two-Sided Tolerance Interval Approach
Due to symmetry about 100% LC, this means that the distance from 100 + k × s must be less than or equal to 16.5. 
The maximum s for a given k and sample mean can be found by solving the |100 – X̅ | ± k × s = 16.5 for s given below:
	
Maximum s = (16.5 – |100 – X̅ |)/k
Using this relationship, an acceptance limit table can be generated for various sample means. A plot of the 
acceptance table for n = 10, 30, and 60 is shown in Figure 14.4 for confidence levels of 50%, 90%, and 95% to pass 
the USP <905> UDU test [46] with at least 95% probability.
Figure 14.4: Two-Sided Tolerance Interval Acceptance Limits for n = 10, 30, and 60
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

14.4	
OC Curves for USP UDU <905> Test [46] Based on 85% – 115% Coverage
Figure 14.5 shows the OC curve for passing the USP UDU test [46] based on simulation. The vertical axis is the 
probability of passing the USP UDU test and the horizontal axis is the coverage which is the probability that a single 
result will fall between 85% – 115% LC. A separate curve is shown for “true” lot means from 90% to 100% LC. The 
OC curves for 104% and 102% are the same as 96% and 98%, respectively (i.e., the OC curves are the same for lot 
means the same distance from 100% LC) [70].
Figure 14.5: Operating Characteristic Curves for Passing USP Uniformity of Dosage Units [46] versus % 
Individuals Between 85.0% and 115% Label Claim for Lot Means between 90% and 110% Label Claim
The probability of passing USP UDU [46] has a dependency on the “true” lot mean giving the impression that the 
probability of passing the USP UDU test increases as the lot means get further away from 100% LC.
14.5	
“Exact” Method – Statistical Details
The “Exact” method is similar to a sampling plan by variable with RQL = (1-0.9934) = 0.66% at β = 10% (1-confidence 
level) acceptance. This is illustrated in Figure 14.6.

Figure 14.6: Operating Characteristic Curve and Acceptance Region Corresponding to 99.34% Coverage 
between 83.5% and 116.5% with 90% Confidence for 30 Samples
Wallis [64] provided the following four step algorithm to generate an acceptance region (i.e., the set of means and 
maximum standards such that the total percent of results outside 83.5% to 116.5% is 0.66%).
Step 1: Compute k from non-central t-distribution
t-1(1-α, n-1, Φ)
k =	___________
√n
Where:
1-α = confidence level
	
n = sample size
Φ = √n × Zp (p = coverage (0.9934))
This is the k value used to construct a one-sided tolerance interval at a 100 × (1-α) % confidence level and a 100 × 
p coverage level. k values for various n at confidence levels of 50%, 90%, and 95% are given in Table 6.3 (Section 
6.3.2).
Several approximate methods for calculating k exist in the literature. One of the most used approximate for k factor 
for one-sided tolerance intervals [71] uses the following set of formulae:
______
		
Zp + √Zp – ab
k =	___________
a
2
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Appendix 6	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Note that the exact k values are less than those using the Two-Sided Tolerance Interval method.
Step 3: Divide po into two parts po’ and po” such that po = po’ + po”
po’ and po” are the selected proportions less than 83.5% and greater than 116.5%, respectively, that add up to po. 
Note each pair results in a different mean and standard deviation, which are computed in Step 4.
Step 4:
Let LSL = Lower Specification Limit (83.5%) and USL = Upper Specification Limit (116.5%),
For a given po’, po”, LSL, and USL, LSL = X̅  – z(1-po’) × s and USL = X̅  + z(1-po”) × s.
Solving this system of equations for X̅  and s gives the following:
	
	USL × z(1-po’) + LSL × z(1-po”)
 	
USL – LSL
X̅  =	 	_____________________	
s =	 _____________
	
	
z(1-po’) + z(1-po”)	
z(1-po’) + z(1-po”)
Note that X̅  is a weighted average of the interval endpoints (LSL and USL) with normal z scores as weights 
associated with po’ and po”, respectively.
	
	
2
	
	
Z1-∝
	
a = 1 –	_______
	
	
2(n – 1)
	
	
2
	
2
	
	
Zp – Z1-∝
	
b =	 _______
	
	
n
The approximation and non-central t-distribution method lead to very similar results when n > 10.
Step 2: Find po, such that z(1-po) = k

14.6	
R Program to Generate Acceptance Limit Tables for the Exact Method
rm(list=ls())
n <-60
#nb samples
P <-0.9934 			
#coverage
conf<-0.50			
#confidence
nc<-qnorm(P)*sqrt(n)		
#non centrality (one sided)
k<-qt(conf,n-1,nc)/sqrt(n)		
#this is the k value from non-central t distribution
LSL<-83.5			
#Lower specification limit
USL<-116.5			
#Upper specification limit
pp<-1-pnorm(k)	
		
#fraction outside the LSL to USL
xx<-c(0.5,0.48,0.45,0.4,0.3,0.2,0.1,0.05,0.01,0.001,0.0001,0.00001,0.000001, 0.0000001,1e-12, 0)
#log scaled multipliers
p1<-xx*pp			
#p1 is what is below LSL
p2<-pp-p1			
#p2 is what is above USL
#p1+p2=coverage
z1<- -qnorm(p1)
z2<- -qnorm(p2)
Xbar<-(USL*z1+LSL*z2)/(z1+z2)
s<-(USL-LSL)/(z1+z2)
Xbar[16]<-USL #cosmetic for last line
s[16]<-0
Xbar2<-200-Xbar
s2<-s
p2_1<-p2

14.7	
Approximation to Exact Acceptance Limits Using a Polygon
Notice that the exact acceptance regions in Figure 14.7 are quite linear outside the range from 98.5% – 101.5% LC. 
Schilling [72] suggested using a polygon as an approximation to the exact acceptance region called the k method. 
Figure 14.8 shows the acceptance region as well as the polygon approximation for a 90% confidence level and 
sample size of 30.
Figure 14.8: Acceptance Polygon
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 183 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 181
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
Appendix 6
The polygon acceptance region is defined as follows:
	
min((16.5 – |100 – X̅ |) / k, MSD)
where MSD is the value of s on the acceptance region when X̅  = 100 and k is the value from Table 6.2 (Section 6.3.2).
As can be seen from the plot, the approximation is very close if the means are outside 98% – 102% LC. The 
maximum difference occurs around 99% and 101% LC.
Using the acceptance polygon, the acceptance criteria simplify to:
s < MSD
X̅  + ks < 116.5
X̅  – ks > 83.5
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

Appendix 6
14.9	
Operating Characteristic Curves to Help Select Sampling Plan
Figure 14.10 (a,b,c) are OC curves for sample sizes n = 10, 30, and 60 at confidence levels of 50, 90, and 95%. The 
x-axis is the “true” lot standard deviation. The y-axis is the probability of passing the acceptance limit table associated
with each plan: ASTM E2709/E2810 [44], Two-Sided Tolerance Interval, and “Exact,” as well as the USP UDU test [46].
Note that all of the plans assure with the specified confidence level that a lot will pass the USP UDU test [46] with 
95% probability. A reference line is drawn at the 95% probability of passing.
These plots can be used to select a sample size. For example, in Figure 14.9, if the “true” lot mean and standard 
deviation were 100% LC and 4.0% respectively, then the probability of passing an acceptance limit table based on 
a sample size of 10 would only be about 73%, 89%, and 96% for ASTM E2709/E2810 [44], Two-Sided Tolerance 
Interval, and “Exact” approach, respectively. However, passing the acceptance limit tables based on a sample size of 
30 would pass all three methods with over 99% probability.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

11.01
Example
Suppose the specification is Q = 85 at 30 minutes. A sample of 12 tablets using Sampling Plan 1 are collected 
from the lot with X̅  = 92.0 and standard deviation of 3.70% Released. Then using Table 14.2, the upper limit on the 
standard deviation is 5.40% Released. Therefore, the sample meets the acceptance criteria since 3.70% is less than 
5.4%.

ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 199
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
Appendix 8
16.2	
Definitions
Attribute (Data) (ASTM E2587 [73])
Observed values or test results that indicate the presence or absence of speciﬁc characteristics or counts of 
occurrences of events in time or space.
Capability (Statistical)
A measure of the ability of a process to meet specification or acceptance criteria (often computed as a ratio of a 
statistical estimate for the process range compared to the specification or acceptance criteria).
Capability of a Process (ICH Q10 [6])
Ability of a process to realise a product that will fulfil the requirements of that product. The concept of process 
capability can also be defined in statistical terms. (ISO 9000:2005)
Note: also see Process Capability
Common Cause Variability (Chance Cause) (ASTM E456 [74])
Source of inherent random variation in a process which is predictable within statistical limits. E2587
Confidence (Statistical)
The confidence level associated with a statistical interval (such as confidence or tolerance intervals) is equal to the 
percentage of similarly computed intervals that contain the true parameter value or claimed population proportion. 
More generally speaking, statistical confidence reflects the sufficiency of data-based evidence to support a 
hypothesis or claim.
Confidence Interval (ASTM E456 [74])
An interval estimate [L, U] with the statistics L and U as limits for the parameter θ and with conﬁdence level 1-α, 
where Pr(L ≤ θ ≤ U) ≥ 1-α.
Conﬁdence Level (ASTM E456 [74])
The value, 1-α, of the probability associated with a conﬁdence interval, often expressed as a percentage.
Continued Process Verification (FDA PV Guidance [3])
Assuring that during routine production the process remains in a state of control.
Continuous Process Verification (ICH Q8(R2) [1])
An alternative approach to process validation in which manufacturing process performance is continuously monitored 
and evaluated.
Control Chart (ASTM E456 [74])
Chart on which are plotted a statistical measure of a subgroup versus time of sampling along with limits based on the 
statistical distribution of that measure so as to indicate how much common, or chance, cause variation is inherent in 
the process or product.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 202 =====
Page 200	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Appendix 8	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Control Limits (ASTM E456 [74])
Limits on a control chart that are used as criteria for signaling the need for action or judging whether a set of data 
does or does not indicate a state of statistical control based on a prescribed degree of risk.
Control Strategy (ICH Q10 [6])
A planned set of controls derived from current product and process understanding that ensures process performance 
and product quality. The controls can include parameters and attributes related to drug substance and drug product 
materials and components, facility and equipment operating conditions, in-process controls, finished product 
specifications and the associated methods and frequency of monitoring and control.
Critical Aspects (ASTM E2500 [36])
Critical aspects of manufacturing systems are typically functions, features, abilities, and performance or 
characteristics necessary for the manufacturing process and systems to ensure consistent product quality and patient 
safety. They should be identified and documented based on scientific product and process understanding.
Critical Material Attribute (CMA)
A material attribute of either a raw/starting material or intermediate that has an impact to a critical quality attribute and 
therefore should be monitored or controlled to ensure the process produces the desired quality. Examples of CMAs 
are a reagent CQA, a CQA for an excipient added at a blending stage, and a packaging material CQA.
Critical Process Parameter (CPP) (ICH Q8(R2) [1])
A process parameter whose variability has an impact on a critical quality attribute and therefore should be monitored 
or controlled to ensure the process produces the desired quality.
Critical Quality Attribute (CQA) (ICH Q8(R2) [1])
A physical, chemical, biological or microbiological property or characteristic that should be within an appropriate limit, 
range, or distribution to ensure the desired product quality.
Design Space (ICH Q8(R2) [1])
The multidimensional combination and interaction of input variables (e.g., material attributes) and process parameters 
that have been demonstrated to provide assurance of quality. Working within the design space is not considered as a 
change. Movement out of the design space is considered to be a change and would normally initiate a regulatory post 
approval change process. Design space is proposed by the applicant and is subject to regulatory assessment and 
approval.
Legacy Product (ISPE [33])
A product initially validated and commercialized prior to the formal introduction of the current lifecycle architecture.
Measurement Uncertainty
A parameter associated with the result of a measurement, that characterizes the dispersion of the values that could 
reasonably be attributed to the measurement.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 203 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 201
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
Appendix 8
Ongoing Process Verification (also known as Continued Process Verification) (EU Annex 15 [11])
Documented evidence that the process remains in a state of control during commercial manufacture.
Operating Characteristic Curve (ISO 3534-2:2006 [29])
Curve showing the relationship between probability of acceptance of product and the incoming quality level for a 
given acceptance sampling plan.
Process Capability (ASTM E456 [74])
Statistical estimate of the outcome of a characteristic from a process that has been demonstrated to be in a state of 
statistical control.
Note: also see Capability of a Process.
Process Capability Index (ASTM E456 [74])
An index describing process capability in relation to speciﬁed tolerance.
Process Performance (ASTM E456 [74])
Statistical measure of the outcome of a characteristic from a process that may not have been demonstrated to be in a 
state of statistical control.
Process Performance Index (ASTM E456 [74])
Index describing process performance in relation to speciﬁed tolerance.
Process Performance Qualification (PPQ) (FDA PV Guidance [3])
The PPQ combines the actual facility, utilities, equipment (each now qualified), and the trained personnel with 
the commercial manufacturing process, control procedures, and components to produce commercial batches. 
A successful PPQ will confirm the process design and demonstrate that the commercial manufacturing process 
performs as expected.
Process Robustness (ICH Q8(R2) [1])
Ability of a process to tolerate variability of materials and changes of the process and equipment without negative 
impact on quality.
Process Validation (PV) (EU Annex 15 [11])
The documented evidence that the process, operated within established parameters, can perform effectively and 
reproducibly to produce a medicinal product meeting its predetermined specifications and quality attributes.
Process Validation (PV) (FDA PV Guidance [3])
The collection and evaluation of data, from the process design stage through commercial production, which 
establishes scientific evidence that a process is capable of consistently delivering quality products.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 204 =====
Page 202	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Appendix 8	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Quality by Design (QbD) (ICH Q8(R2) [1])
A systematic approach to development that begins with predefined objectives and emphasizes product and process 
understanding and process control, based on sound science and quality risk management.
Quality Risk Management (QRM) (ICH Q9 [2])
A systematic process for the assessment, control, communication and review of risks to the quality of the drug 
(medicinal) product across the product lifecycle.
Quality Target Product Profile (QTPP) (ICH Q8(R2) [1])
A prospective summary of the quality characteristics of a drug product that ideally will be achieved to ensure the 
desired quality, taking into account safety and efficacy of the drug product.
Revalidation (ISPE Glossary [75])
Validation of a previously validated system that has been changed or modified. Revalidation can be performed as the 
result of a change to the system, or a time-based assessment.
Risk (ICH Q9 [2])
The combination of the probability of occurrence of harm and the severity of that harm (ISO/IEC Guide 51).
Risk Management (ICH Q9 [2])
The systematic application of quality management policies, procedures, and practices to the tasks of assessing, 
controlling, communicating and reviewing risk.
Risk Ranking (ICH Q9 [2])
Risk ranking of complex systems typically requires evaluation of multiple diverse quantitative and qualitative factors 
for each risk. The tool involves breaking down a basic risk question into as many components as needed to capture 
factors involved in the risk. These factors are combined into a single relative risk score that can then be used for 
ranking risks.
Robust Process
A process that demonstrates acceptable quality and performance while tolerating input variability.
Simple Random Sample (ASTM E456 [74])
• 
(without replacement) probability sample of n sampling units from a population of N units selected in such a way 
that each of the	
N!	
subsets of n units is equally probable.
	
	
________
	
	
n!(N – n)!
• 
(with replacement) a probability sample of n sampling units from a population of N units selected in such a way 
that, in order of selection, each of the Nn ordered sequences of units from the population is equally probable.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 205 =====
ISPE Good Practice Guide:	
Page 203
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation	
Appendix 8
Stable Process (ISO 3534-2 [29])
A process in a state of statistical control (constant mean and variance) subject only to random causes. Note: this 
state does not imply that the random variation is large or small, within or outside of specification; but rather the 
variation is predictable using statistical techniques. Also, very importantly, a process can fail to meet the definition of a 
Stable Process due to shifts in the mean, but still be considered in a State of Control.
Stage 1 Process Validation – Pharmaceutical Development (ICH Q8(R2) [1])
The aim of pharmaceutical development is to design a quality product and its manufacturing process to consistently 
deliver the intended performance of the product.
Stage 1 Process Validation – Process Design (FDA PV Guidance [3])
The commercial manufacturing process is defined during this stage based on knowledge gained through 
development and scale-up activities.
Stage 2 Process Validation – Process Qualification (FDA PV Guidance [3])
During this stage, the process design is evaluated to determine if the process is capable of reproducible commercial 
manufacturing. This stage has two elements: (1) design of the facility and qualification of the equipment and utilities 
and (2) process performance qualification (PPQ).
Stage 2 Process Validation – Process Validation (EMA [12])
Process validation should establish whether all quality attributes and process parameters, which are considered 
important for ensuring the validated state and acceptable product quality, can be consistently met by the process.
Stage 3 Process Validation – Continued Process Verification (FDA PV Guide [3])
Ongoing assurance is gained during routine production that the process remains in a state of control.
We recommend continued monitoring and sampling of process parameters and quality attributes at the level 
established during the process qualification stage until sufficient data are available to generate significant variability 
estimates. These estimates can provide the basis for establishing levels and frequency of routine sampling and 
monitoring for the particular product and process. Monitoring can then be adjusted to a statistically appropriate and 
representative level.
Stage 3 Process Validation – Ongoing Process Verification (EU Annex 15 [11])
Documented evidence that the process remains in a state of control during commercial manufacture.
State of Control (ICH Q10 [6])
A condition in which the set of controls consistently provides assurance of continued process performance and 
product quality.
Statistical Distribution
A mathematical function that describes the probability of occurrence of possible outcomes of a population.
For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
Downloaded from https://guidance-docs.ispe.org/ by Doris Kopp on April 9, 2024.
For personal use only. No other uses without permission.
Copyright © 2024 International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering. All rights reserved.

===== [ispe_gpg] PAGE 206 =====
Page 204	
ISPE Good Practice Guide:
Appendix 8	
Practical Implementation of the Lifecycle Approach to Process Validation
Technology Transfer (ICH Q10 [6])
The goal of technology transfer activities is to transfer product and process knowledge between development and 
manufacturing, and within or between manufacturing sites to achieve product realisation. This knowledge forms 
the basis for the manufacturing process, control strategy, process validation approach and ongoing continual 
improvement.
Tolerance Interval
A tolerance interval provides limits with confidence on a proportion of a population. More speciﬁcally, a 100 × p%/100 
× (1−α) tolerance interval provides limits within which at least a certain proportion (p) of the population falls with a 
given level of conﬁdence (1−α).
Trend (ICH Q9 [2])
A statistical term referring to the direction or rate of change of a variable(s).
Uncertainty (ASTM E456 [74])
An indication of the magnitude of error associated with a value that takes into account both systematic errors and 
random errors associated with the measurement or test process. E2655
Validation Master Plan (VMP) (EU Annex 15 [11])
The key elements of the site qualification and validation programme should be clearly defined and documented in a 
validation master plan (VMP).
Variability
A measure of the spread or dispersion of a distribution of data. Intra-batch variability reflects variability within a batch; 
Inter-batch variability reflects the variability between batches.
Variables, Data (ASTM E456 [74])
Observations or test results deﬁned on a continuous scale.
Verification (ASTM E2500 [36])
A systematic approach to verify that manufacturing systems, acting singly or in combination, are fit for intended use, 
have been properly installed, and are operating correctly. This is an umbrella term that encompasses all types of 
approaches to assuring systems are fit for use such as qualification, commissioning and qualification, verification, 
system validation, or other.
